5, January 10, 11 a.m.
Embassy's 1, January 4, 5 p.m., American oil
interests in Japan.
One. The conversations between the special
representatives of the American and British oil
interests and the Japanese officials commenced
yesterday at the Tokyo Club in order to avoid prem-
ature puplicity in a pleasant and conciliatory at-
mosphere. The first day was devoted principally to
generalities, the Japanese dcing most of the talking.
Conversetions will be resumed this afternoon.
Two. No Embassy officials are attending meet-
ings but the oil interests will keep the Embassies
fully informed. Important developments will be con-
veyed to the Department by telerraph and memoranda of
the conversations by mail.
GREW

Department of State,
Washington, D.C.
Dear Dr. Hornbeck:
Dreare et State
I acknowledge vith appreciation your
letter of January 8th, confirming our telephone conversation
with Mr. Mackay of the Department, in respect to the question
of transmission of telegrams through the Department to our
representatives in Japan.
1 fully realize the position of the Department
and you may rest assured that oniy in matters of the greatest
importence and urgency will we request the Department to act
for us in this manner.
Further, of course, we understend that if such
occasicns arise, you will be at perfect liberty to paraphrase
whatever message or messeges are mutuelly arranged for trans-
mission. In addition, the Department will naturally be re-
imbursed for any expenses incurred on our behalf.
F/FG
We greatly appreciate your continued and help-
ful efforts extended to our interests.
Sincerely yours,
A. Dimdas

=== Page 18 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
January 30, 1935.
Tokyo's despatoh No. 1ll7 of December
29, l934, in regard to the oil situation
in Japan and in Korea, --
As, for the most part, the despatch
under reference gives a detailed account
of events already known to the Department
in outline, I do not believe you need read
the despatch in its entirety.
It is interesting to note, however,
that, according to Mr. Kurusu, the oil
control law is largely a child of the
brain of Mr. Hashimoto, iresident of the
Nippon Oil Company, who, failing in his
expectations of receiving a government
subsidy for the expense of stock-holding,
is now caught in his own net. Also, ac-
cording to Mr. Kurusu, the Japanese oil
companies are fighting the battles of the
foreign oil companies in regard to gasoline
prices in the same way that the foreign oil
oompanies are fighting the battles of the
Japanese companies in regard to stock-holding.
I suggest that you read the half-page
section entitled "Rumor of an oil monopoly
in Korea" appearing on pages 8 and 9 of the
despatch under reference.
RCM:EJL

EMBASSY OF THE
UNITED STATES OF AMERICA
Tokyo, December 29, 1934.
No. 1117.
ile
Subject:
Americen Oil Interests in Japan.
894.6363
894.6363/171
22
(Gr 1
For
The Honorable
The Secretary of State,
Washington.
100
Sir:
I have the honor to refer to my despatch No. 1085,
dated December 13, 1934, and to previous despatches and
telegrams on the subject of the difficulties being ex-
perienced by the American and other foreign oil interests
in Japan under the new Petroleum Industry Law, and to
FILED
report the following developments in the situation.
F/DEW
Representations.
In compliance with the Department's telegraphic
154
instruction No. 210, December 20, 8 p.m., the Embassy
consulted

=== Page 20 === (confidence 0.99)
-2 -
consulted with the British Embassy in Tokyo and found
the British Ambassador and members of his staff most
willing to cooperate in any way possible in making re-
presentations in a manner acceptable to the Department.
It was agreed that both Embassies sho ld make oral and
informal representations, outlining t e points which
the foreign oil conpanies found difficult in the Petro-
leum Industry Law, stoting that the assurances so far
given by the Japanese Government were not sufficient
to remove the anxieties felt by the foreign oil com-
panies in regard to the future of their business in
Japan, and expressing the  ope of the respective Govern-
ments that the Japanese Government wou'd reconsider the
question and endeavor to remove th causes of the anxie-
ties. Both Embassies were to ad (unles: the subject was
first broached by the Japanese official) that high offi-
cials of the British and American oil interests were at
present in Shanghai and, if there was any prospect of a
solution of the difficulties through discussion between
the Japanese authorities and the of icials of the oil com-
panies, no doubt the officials would be authorized by
their principals to come to Japan for the purpose. The
various points of the representations were to be embodied
by each Embassy on sheets of paper without title or date,
to be left at the Foreign Office if the Japanese official
interviewed so desired, and it was further agreed that the
representations were to be made by the Counselors of Em-
bassy to Mr. Kurusu, the Chief of the Bureau of Commercial
Affairs

=== Page 21 === (confidence 0.98)
- 3 -
Affairs of the Foreign Office.
In compliance with the Department's instruction re-
ferred to above, the question of making such representations
was referred to the oil companies, who agr ed to the pro-
cedure outlined on December 27, 1934. The representations
were therefore made on that day, the British by Mr. Sansom,
Commercial Counselor of the ritish Embassy, at about 2
o'clock and the American by Mr. Neville, assisted by Mr.
Dickover, at 3 o'clock. The representutions were purpose-
ly made on the same afternoon in order to give the impres-
sion of a united front. Mr. Kurusu received the represen-
tations sym athetically and discussed the various problems
of the oil companies, both Japanese and foreign, in a
frank manner. He said, anong other things, that the oil
control law of Japan was largely, he thought, a child of
the brain of Mr. Hashimoto, the President of the Nippon
Oil Company, who hoped thereby to obtain some advantages
in his competition with the two large foreign oil companies.
Mr. Hashimoto, however, had expected to he subsidized by
the Japanese Government for the expense of stcck-holding,
and when he found that the Finance Department would not
consent to a subsidy for stock-holding, he discovered that
he was left "holding the bag". Hashimoto was therefore
trying very hard to obtain an increase in the price of
gasoline. The Nippon Oil Company, according to Mr. Kurusu,
was in serious financial difficulties and unable to obtain
funds from the banks to finance its stock-holding duties;
therefore it needed an increase in the price of gasoline
to improve its financial outlook and so obtain accommoda-
tion

=== Page 22 === (confidence 0.97)
- 4 -
tion from the banks. Mr. Kurusu said that the Japanese
oil companies were fig ting the foreign oil companies'
battles in regard to gasoline prices, just as the fore-
eign oil companies were fighting the Japanese oil com-
panies' battles in regard to stock-holding.
In ad ition to the points brought out on the pa-
1/
per (see nclosure No. 1) later left with Mr. Kurusu at
his recuest, r. Nevilic tated that the explanations
which had kincly Leen made b. the Japarese Government in
its memorundum of October 31, 1934, as vel1 as the oral
explanations which it was understood were macc by the
Vice Minister for Com erce and Industry to rcpresenta-
tives of t'e for ign oil companies on Yove mer co, 1934,
had unforturetely not been sufficient to remove the an-
xieties felt by the Americin oil interests in regard to
the future of their busines: in Japan, nd thct therefore
the Areric n Govornment felt cons rained again to point
out to the Japanese Governnent some of the causes of those
anxieties and to express the hope that the Japanese Govern-
ment voulc again consider the problems confronting the
American oil interests in Japan and would endeavor to re-
move the causes of the anxieties. Mr. Kurusu said that
he would discuss the question again wit Mr. Yoshino,
the Vice Minister for Commerce and Industry, and that he
was sure that a solution of the difficulties could be
found "within the frame-work of tho present law". He al-
so said that Mr. Machida, the present Minister for Commerce
and Industry, was an "old-fashioned" statesman, "who still
believes

=== Page 23 === (confidence 0.98)
- 5 -
believes in laissez faire and free trade", and that
therefore the foreign oil companies could count on
a liberal attitude on his part.
Mr. Kurusu then brought up the (uestion of the
possihility of iscussions betv en  r. Parker, Presi-
dent of the Stancard-Vacuum OiI Conp any, ho is nov in
Shanghai, and the Japanese officials. Mr. Dickover stated
that if Mr. Kurusu and Mr. Yosnino belicved that fruit-
ful results might flow fron such conversations and if the
possibility existed that the discussion might bring about
a solution of the cifficulties confronting the American
oil interests in Japan, he believed that the Company
might authorize Mr. Parker to procccd to Japan for the
purpose. Mr. Kurusu said that he was sure that the con-
versat:ons would bring good results, but that he would
have to consult Mr. Yoshino on the subject, as the dis-
cussions were rather in Mr. Yoshino's province than in
Mr. Kurusu's.
The British representations were much the same as
ours, except that thc British went more into detail in
the paper left with the Foreign Office. A copy of the
2/
British paper is enclosed herewith.
On December 28, 1934, Mr. Kurusu asked Mr. Neville
to come to the Foreign Office and there informed him that
Mr. Yoshino would welcome discussions with the Directors
of the Standard-Vacuum Oil Company now in Shanghai; that
if the foreign oil companies wished to build refineries
in Japan, such refineries would be treated on an equal
footing

=== Page 24 === (confidence 0.98)
- 6 -
footing with Japanese refineries as regards participa-
tion in the natural ncrease of the Japanese petroleum
trade; that the Government intended to develop the
Japanese ref ning industry, but nevertheless would al-
lot & part of the increase in de and for petroleum products
to the importers of rofined proiucts; thot the Japanese
oil companies could not comply with the stock-holding
rovisions of th law because of lack of funds and that
therefore some adjustment would be necessary;
and th&t
such points could best he discussed by the oil men them-
selves. A copy of Mr. Neville's memorandum of his con-
3/
versation with Mr. Kurusu is enclosed herewith. Mr.
Neville gained the impression from his talk with Mr. Kuru-
su that the Japanese officials are adopting a much more
reasonable attitude than formerly toward the problems of
the foreign oil companies in Japan. Mr. Kurusu also saw
Mr. Sansom on the same day, as a result of which Mr.
Sansom also obtained the impression that the Japanese au-
thorities are adopting a more lenient attitude.
Although Mr. Kurusu did not say so, it is probable
that the information given to Mr. Neville and Mr. Sansom
was intended as the reply to the representations made on
the previous day.
Both this Embas y and the British Embasry promptly
conveyed the information obtained from Mr. Kurusu to the
two oil companies, which thereupon telegraphed to their
special representatives in Shanghai to proceed to Japan.
As the Japanese Government offices close on December 29
and

=== Page 25 === (confidence 0.98)
- 7 -
and remain closed until January 6, it was arranged
with Mr. Kurusu that the conversations should start, if
possible, on or about January 7.
Cooperation betveen the Eabassy and the American Oil
Interests.
In regard to the Department's telegraphic instruc-
tion No. 215, December 24, 1 p.m., to the effect that by
some unknown means the special renresent tives of the oil
companies now in Shanghai had obtained a mistaken impres-
sion that the American and British Governments might change
their attitude toward the oil questions of Japan and Man-
churia because of the inconclusive results of the London
naval conversations, and instructirg the Embassy to give
the local represent: tive of the Standard-Vacuum Oil Com-
pany the fullest information possible in regerd to the
exact situation at the moent, 1 have the honor to report
that the local American oil interests have always been
kept fully and currently informed in regard to all phases
of the American Government's attitude except that phase
having to do with the relations between the American and
British Governments. As an example, the De artment's
140
telegram No. 201, December 10, 8 p.m., which contained
much information of interest and value to the special re-
presentatives of the American oil interests, arrived after
the departure for Shanghai of Mr. Parker and Mr. Goold.
The Embassy therefore promptly called Mr. Eaton, the Act-
ing General Manager of the Standard-Vacuum Oil Company
in Japan, to Tokyo and gave him full information in re-
gard

=== Page 26 === (confidence 0.98)
-8 -
gard to the latest dcvelopments of the American Govern-
ment's attitude. Mr. Eaton embodied this information in
a letter to Mr. Goola, which he sent to Shanghai by a
special messenger. The f ct that there hos been no change
in the Department's attitude was likewise conveyed to
Mr. Eaton, who telegraphed it to Mr. Goold by referring,
in his coded message, to words contained in the letter
previously sent to Mr. Goold. Neither this Emhassy nor
Mr. Eaton can understand the source of the mistaken im-
pression obtained by the special representatives at Shang-
hai.
Rumor of an Oil Monopoly in Korea.
The Embassy has been irformed by thc representative
in Japan of the Associeted Oil Conpany of California,
which has a half interest in the Mitsubishi Oil Company,
that a plan is being formed to estab ish an oil monopoly,
somewhat similar to that established in Manc uria, in
Korea. The first move would be to organize an oil re-
fining company in Korea, the capital to be subscribed
by the Nippon Oil Company, Ogura Oil Company, and Mitsu-
bishi Oil Company (the representative of theAssociated Oil
Company obtained his information regarding the scheme be-
cause the firm in which he is interested was approached for
a subscription). The refinery would probably be establish-
ed in Chemulpo and would be able, in the beginning, to
supply about one-third of the demand in Korea for petroleum
products.

=== Page 27 === (confidence 0.96)
- 9 -
products. This, in the opinion of the representative
of the Associated Oil Company, would be the first step
toward the establishment of an oil sales monopoly in
Korea.
Respectiully yours,
Moi.
Josenh C. Grew

Conversation.
Decmber 28, 1934.
Mr. Neville and Mr. Kurusu, Chief
of the Commercial Bureau of the
011.
Foreign Office.
Mr. Kurusu called me on the telephone and asked me
to call on him at his office at about 3:00 P M, which
I did. He tolc me t'at he hac spoken to the Vice Minister of
Commerce and Industry in r gard to the conversation
which Mr. Dickover and I had had with him, and referred
to the suggestion that he had made that the Directors
of the Standar Vacuum Oil Company now in Shanghai should
come to Japan. He said th:t Mr. Yoshino had welcomed the
suggestion.
He then told me that there would be no discrimination
in the allotment of quotas for refineries in Japan; that if
foreign companies were to build refineries here, they
would participate in any increase of business on equal foot-
ing with Japanese refineries; that as to the importation
of refined products, the Government intended to keep the
Japanese refineries busy if possible, but as the refineries
were now operating somewhere around 80% of their theoretical
capacity, the import quotas would have to be increased, and
the increase would be allotted to all.
Turning to the question of stock-holding requirements,
he said that this was becoming a problem for the Japanese
concerns, who could not possibly comply with the law, as
they did not have the money to do so. The net result was
bound to be an adjustment of some sort, presumably includ-
ing

=== Page 35 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
ing an increase in prices. He said that this was one
of the points which he felt could best be discussed by
the oil men themselves, and which he could not discuss
in detail, but he ascured me that the oil men would
find the Department of Comnerc: and Industry "reasonable".
He said that he honestly felt that the Diroctors would
find it to their interest to come and that it would be
worth while to do so. I said that I should be glad to
pas: on this information. He ad ed that ho had an ap-
pointment with ir. Sansom of the British Embassy later
in the afternoon, to whom he would give the same advice.
He then said that Sansom ha spoken to him on a pre-
vious call about the ianc urian Monopoly. He said that
a Mr. Hoshino, of whom he had spoken to me before, the
Vice Minist.r of Finance of "Manchukuo" was still in
Tokyo. He told me that Hoshino had come over in connec-
tion with the sale of the Chinese Eastorn Railway, but
as Vice Minister of Finance he could speak with authority
in regard to Monopolies, actual or proposed. He said
that the oil men, if they came to Tokyo, could see this
Hoshino without arousing comment, and without committing
themselves; that he (Hoshino) was coming to the Foreign
Office later in the afternoon and that he would sound
him out on this subject. I told Kurusu that I was not
authorized to speak on this matter one way or the other.
He said that he knew that, but was just giving me some
information which I could use as I liked, and which he
was going to give to Sansom when the latter came to call.
I thanked him and took my leave.
Note:
E.L.N.

I do not know just how this …r. Hoshino fits into

in oonneotion with the conversations now

await initiative at that end. On the
other hand, I peroeive no objection, other
than the costs involved, to requesting the

sults to date.

of the seotion entitled "Japan" of the at-

allach.d.h.

believe you will be interested in reading

It would appear (see paragraph

under reference) that the foreign oil
companies may ask that the regulations be

Japan. As the oil interests originally
were opposed to maintaining stocks in ex-

apparent willingness to increase such stocks
to a four months' supply would appear to
indicate a concession on their part.

Progress of the Conversations.
The special repru entatives of th Standare and Shell
interests arrived in Japan on January i, 19a5, but owing
to the New Year's nolidays, Wic' in Japan continue until
January Cth, no action could be ttken by the representatives
until January 7, 1955. On that lay the Emoasny arranged
for an informal meeting b tween the A eric:n representatives
Ar. Dickover being present.
and r. Kurusu at the Tokyo Club,/ The Britisn representa-

the oil intercsts the efore drew up a memoranlum of points
which they wished to discuss, which was sent to Mr. Kurusu
1/
on the 8th. A copy of this memorondun is enclosed herevith.
It will be noted that the memoranlun contains no mention
of the petroleum sales monopoly in "Manchukuo". As the
monopoly question, unlike the petroleum control question
in Japan, involves international rights and agreements as
well as comaercial interests, the special representatives
of the oil companies fecl that it will be best not to at-
tempt to negotiate on the question, at least until such
time as all diplomatic resources have been exhausted. At
the

=== Page 47 === (confidence 0.98)
- 6 -
the same time they intend, by occasional mention of the
"Manchukuo" monopoly, to convey the impression to the
Japanese officials that the two questions are more or
less linked in their minds.
The serious discussions coa enced on January 9,
1935,and were held at the Tokyo Club (almost the only
place in Tokyo where one c'n escape from newspaper ropor-
ters) in order to avoid premature and misleading news-
paper publicity. The foreign re pr sentatives consisted
of the three American and Britis) special representatives
of the oil companies, assisted by Mr. Goold and Mr. Malcolm,
while the Japanese representutives consisted of Mr. S.
Kurusu, Chief of the Bur au of Comnercial Affairs of the
Foreign Office, and Mr. Yoshino, Vice Minister of Coamerce
and Industry. Mr. Kurusu's secretary acted as secretary
of the meetings and took notes of the ciscussions. No
representatives of the American and British Embassies at-
tended the meetings, as it was deemed best not to inject
an official note into the discussions, at least until it
became necessary to secure official recognition of some
agreement or devlopment.
The discussions of the 9th and loth were confined al-
most entirely to explanations by the Japanese officials of
the aims and operation of the Petroleum Industry Law and
its enforcing regulations. The atmosphere of the discus-
sions was friendly and, according to the American representa-
tives, the Japanese appeared to have a sincere desire to
find some modus operandi which would be satisfactory to
the foreign oil companies, but, for the time being, at
least,

=== Page 48 === (confidence 0.99)
- 7 -
least, refused to comnit themselves by advancing any
definite proposals for modification of the law or regu-
lations. The American represent:tives believ that the
discussions will not bring forth any definite results
until about the mid le of next week.
Notes of the discussions are oeing taken in Japanese
by Mr. Kurusu's secretary. These notes will be translated
by the foreign oil comoanies, and after being checked, will
be embodied in memoranda of tn conversations. Copies
will be supplied to the Embassy and will be transmitted
to the Department as soon as available.

Offered For Sale
To Japanese Firm
Asahi Report Creates

Petroleum Industry
MANY OFFERS MADE
Government Desires Diversi-
fication Of Supply In
Interests Of Defense
A "certain foreign firm" has
formally proposed the sale of
petroleum-fields which it owns in
Mexico to the Nippon Oil Co.,
causing a sensation in Japanese
oil circles, reports the Tokyo
Asahi this morning.
Details of the offer, according
to the same paper, are being kept
secret, but in view of its nature,
great importance is attached to
developments in the matter.
Following the enforcement last
year of the Oll Law providing for
tne establishment of oll refneries
in the country under State super-
vision, the question of how to recure
permanent sources of crude oll sup-
plies has come to the fore.
In this connection, efforts have
silicc been made jointly by the au-
thoritles and the leading oil rms
of the country, but no satlsfactory
plons have yet been mapped out.
The Asahi points out that the
nnior portion of crude oil at pre-
sent is being imported fron the
United States, but that this is con-
sidered undesirable from the view-
pcia! of national policy.
Herce, the paper states, plans
tave been under way for some time
past to import crude oll from other
parts of the world.

MEXICAN SOURCES DENY
OIL FIELD DEAL IS ON
Purchase of Producing Terri-
tory by Japan Declared 'Im-
possible' by Officials
Nippon Dempo-United Press
MEXICO CITY. Jauary 5.—Strong
denial was made here today that ne-
gatiations are under way for the pur-
chase of a large oil field in Mexico
by the Japan Oil Company, as report-
ed in the Tokyo Asahi. Officials of
il concerns characterized such a deal
is "impossible." and the spokesman
of the Japanese Legation said he was
even ignorant of the souree of the re-
port.
Unefficial observers expressed the
opinion that the Mexican Government
would prevent such a transaction, as
a company was recently organized
under Government auspices to pre-
serve Mexico's natural oil resources.
This company. it was remarked. will
move immediately to purchase any
worthwhile field placed on the market.
Representative banking interests re-
cently announced they had offered to
refund Mexico's foreign debt in ex-
change for a petroleum monopoly.
and that the offer had been refused.

JAPAN
A corrcspondent inquired whether the Japanese Government
had made a reply to the last American communication concerning
the oil situation in Japan. The Under Secretary informed the
correspondents FOR BACkGROUND that the oil question was await-
ing tho outcome of conferences between representatives of the
oil companies and Japanese authorities. He added that he
presumed the meetings were continuing to take place but that
he could not give very procisc information because no definite
informaticn rogarding the meetings had been received of late.
In reply to a query whether the Japanese authorities had
agreed also to take up directly with the oil representatives
the Manchukuo oil monopoly question, the Under Secretary said
that he would prefer not to discuss this matter for the prosent,
although he assumed that the whole question was under discussion.
A correspondent observed that there had been a report from
Tokyo to the cffect that the representatives of the oil companies
were discussing with the Japanese officials the possibility of
havins to put in storage only a threc-months' supply of oil
instead of a six-months' supply as originally stipulated. The
Under Secretary stated that he could not say what were en-
visagcd in the discussions taking place because no reports
regardin the discussions had been received here.
CHAHAR

ceived any information regarding tho reported Japanese attack
on Chahar. Mr. Phillips said that one or two inconclusive
reports had been reccived on this subject but that the Depart-
ment was not fully informed rogarding what had transpired.
Asked whether the cvents taking place in Chahar constituted
a Japanesc invasion or a revolt on the part of the Chahar Arnry
in order to establish an independent government, Mr. Phillips
said that he would prefer not to discuss the matter until more

CONFIDENTIAL.
The Honorable
The Secretary of State,
Washington.
E/B
514119
Sir:
I have the honor to refer to the Embassy's despatch
No. 1126, dated January 10, 1935, in regard to the conver-
sations now being held in Tokyo betwecn representatives
of the Standard-Vacuum Oil Company and the British Shell
oil interests on the one side and the Vice Minister for
Commerce and Industry and the Chief of the Bureau of Com-
mercial Affairs of the Foreign Office on the other, and
FTLED
to report that the memoranda of the conversations, to
which reference was made on page 7 of the despatch mentioned
above.

=== Page 68 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
above, have only just come into the possession of the
Embassy. The notcs of the conversations were taken in
Japanese by Mr. Yoshino's secr tary. These notes were
translated into English by the foreign oil com anies,
checked with the ment:l notes of te conversations of
each one of the reprcsentatives, and the rcsult finally
embodied in memoranda. The memoranoa are lengthy and
the process of compiling them required considerable time.
The Embassy has not sufficient time to copy the memoran-
da for this mail, nor even to compile a thorough summary
thereof, but the following brief resumé of thc results of
the conversations up to January 15 will serve to indicate
the progress of the discussions.
Progress of the Conversations.
The conversations of the first two days (January 9
and 10) were devoted to explanations by Mr. Yoshino, the
Vice Minister for Commerce and Industry, of the purposes
and probable operation of the Petroleum Industry Law. Ac-
cording to Mr. Yoshino, the objectives of the Law are the
stabilization of the whole petroleum market in Japan and
the protection of the Japanese petroleum industry. The
objective of the system of licenses is to protect the oil
interests by preventing raids on the market such as vas
made recently by Soviet gasoline. There is to be no dis-
crimination between Japanese and foreign oil com anies,
but the Government wishes as far as possible to develop
refining in Japan and to import crude oil instead of re-
fined products. Powers under the Law are given to Army
and

=== Page 69 === (confidence 0.98)
- 3 -
and Navy authorities, but these are to be used only in
time of emergencies and the real purpose of the Law is
to secure a smooth and steady supply of oil. It seemed
that the oil companies, which would benefit by the law,
should be willing t bear in return th burden of carry-
ing stocks.
In regard to a guarentee of future trace, Mr. Yoshino
stated that the demand in Japan for petroleum products
was developing too f: st to per: it th. fixing of : definite
period for licenses. The law, combined with the Japanese
Constitution (which guaruntees freedon of trade), however,
gives the foreign oil com anies the right to resort to
litigation against the Government if the: belicve them-
selves to have been wronged in regare to licenses or quo-
tas. of course, if the demand for oil should suffer re-
duction because of the discovery of some new fuel, the
decrease would have to be borne by all the companies equally.
The Government, however, ight consicer some system which
would sanction past trace and recuire licenses only to cover
the increase in the trace.
The Department of Commerc an Industry considered the
question of refineries in Japan purely from an economic
viewpoint and not from thc standpoint of national defense.
(Mr. Yoshino on several occasions voiced the opinion that
the Petroleum Industry Law was primarily economic in charac-
ter and had little relation to national defense - probably
to avoid arguments such as might be advanced by the Embassies
in regard to violations of provisions in the treaties between
Japan and other countries). Now that Japanese refining
capacity

=== Page 70 === (confidence 0.99)
- 4 -
capacity was nearly filled, the Government might consider
the giving of a guarantee of future trade, based on a per-
centage of the total trade and not on volume alone.
Stock-keeping, according to Mr. Yoshino, is not a
crisis measure, lut is necessary for t e protection of
Japanese industries. It will be necessary to find measures
to pay for stock-keeping, as the oil com anies cannot be
expected to meet the cost from their own pockets. Govern-
ment subsidies, a raise in prices an economies through
rationalization have been considered. Japan never subsi-
dizes foreign firms and cannot afford any subsidies at pre-
sent. Price r ising is possible, but the Government wishes
first to try economies through rationalization as a means
to meet the cost of stock-keeping. However, the Japanese
Government does not adopt a "take it or leave it" attitude
toward the law. If the law proves to be economically un-
workable, steps must be taken. Mr. Yoshino suggested that
in such event, the foreign and Japanese oil companies join
forces and protest, thus protecting the Department of Com-
merce and Industry from attacks in the Diet and from the
public. If six months' stocks prove to be too great a bur-
den, the matter will be reconsidered.
In regard to price fixing by the Government, Mr. Yoshino
said that it as not the purpose of the law that the Govern-
ment should regulate prices under normal conditions. The
Government intends to interfere only if prices become too
high. The problem is to ascertain what constitutes too high
a price.
In regard to the operation of the exchange control law
n

=== Page 71 === (confidence 0.99)
- 5 -
in connection with the possibility that the foreign
oil companies might wish to sell out their Japan in-
vestments, Mr. Yoshino stated that the purpose of the
exchange control law was to prevent speculative exports
of capital hut not to impede legitimate business. The
Government would not, therefore, refuse a legitimate re-
quest to export funds, and should thc foreign oil companies
wish to retire from the Japanese market and send the pro-
ceeds of the sale of their properties to their home coun-
tries, Mr. Yoshino believed that permission could easily
be obtained. He hoped, however, that this contingency
would not arise.
In regard to the cuestion of rofining in Japan, Mr.
Yoshino st ted that there was nothing in the law vhich
allowed discrimination because of nationality. At present,
however, there is idle refining ecuipment in Japan and
therefore it would be unwise to permit the erection of fur-
ther refining equipment. Exceptions would, however, be
made in the case of the foreign oil companies, because of
their established trade in Japan. In the roply made by the
Japanese Government to the representations of the Embassies
in Tokyo, it was suggested that Japanese capital be taken into
any refining companies established by the foreign firms in
Japan. This suggestion was made because of the possibility
that subsidies might be given to Japanese refineries in the
future, and in order to qualify for such subsidies the foreign
firms would have to take in more than half Japanese capital.
Such subsidies are a remote possibility. Otherwise there
will be no discrimination between Japanese and foreign refiners.
In

=== Page 72 === (confidence 0.99)
- 6 -
In regard to the restrictions in the Petroleum Indus-
try Law on the transfer, abolition or suspension of business,
Mr. Yoshino said that permission for such actions was re-
quired because the oil industry partook of the character
of a public utility which must be maintained intact for
the public good. The law is intendec, however, to prevent
speculative transactions in the accuisition and transfer
of rights to import or refine, and is not intended to in-
terfere with bona fide transacticns.
In the course of the first two days' conversations,
the representatives of thc foreign oil co anies briefly
presented their views on th situation. They stated that
the foreign oil interests realized Japan's oil problems
and wished to assist in finding a solution of those problems.
They thought, however, that the foreign oil companies, be-
cause of their interest in this market, should have been
consulted before the Petroleum Industry Law was drafted.
They thought that the trade and investments of the oil im-
porting firms should receive consideration equal to that
accorded to other (Japanese refining) firms. If the Japan-
ese refiners have idle equipment, it should be remembered
that refiners in other countries are in even a worse situation,
and that therefore they might consider it necessary to put
an embargo on the exportation of crude oil. Refining in
Japan is not economical, for various reasons. The problem
therefore exists of trying to reconcile the purely economic
view of the foreign oil companies with the views of the
Japanese Government, which are based on other than purely
economic grounds.
The

=== Page 73 === (confidence 0.99)
- 7 -
The representatives of the foreig oil companies also
pointed out that large stocks of oil are not really neces-
sary from an industrial viewpoint and that therefore the
investment therein is a dead loss. Great Britain, as an
example, never has more than three months' stocks in the
British Isles. The Japanese Government, however, insists
upon six months' stocks and requires the industry to pay
for these stocks. Further rationalization of the industry
will not meet te cost of carrying these stocks, and it
will be necessary to modify the regulations.
The later conversations, held on January 14, 15, 16,
17 and 23, dealt with the various problems in detail and
consisted largely of discussion of technical points. The
conversation of January 14 was concerned principally with
the question of guarantees of trade, and eventually the
Japanese officials agreed in principle to guarantee a per-
centage of the total trae, based on past sales, but only
after the idle Japanese refining equipment is cared for.
New comers to the field are not ordinarily to be recognized
or given licenses. An absolute percentage guarantee cannot be
given now, however, because of the necessity of taking up the
slack in the refining capacity of Japan, and also because
Japan may find a cheap supply of crude which would, through
the natural laws of economics, drive the importing firms out
of the field.
The conversation on the 15th was devoted principally to
a discussion of the various costs which should be considered
in connection with price-fixing, and it was decided to ap-
point a sub-committee to draw up a formula for the Government's
assistance

=== Page 74 === (confidence 0.98)
-8-
assistance in considering prices. The conversation also
included a discussion of the stock-keeping requirements.
Mr. Yoshino in this connection assured the representatives
of the foreign oil companies that the required six months'
stocks could include all stocks in the country, wherever
situated and in whatever containers packed (in a later con-
versation, the memorandum of vwhich has not yet come to the
Embassy, Mr. Yoshino stated that the stocks could also in-
clude all oils on the water and en route to Japan). Mr.
Yoshino also stated that the purpose was to interpret the
law as flexibly as possible.
Although over two weeks have elapsed since the conver-
sations started, nothing concrete has as yet been evolved.
The representatives of the foreign oil companies are not
discouraged, however, and believe that a workable agreement
will eventually be reached.
The Manchurian oil monopoly has not been seriously dis-
cussed as yet, although mention of the monopoly has been
made in the course of the conversations.
Respectfully yours,
K.1tC
Joseph C. Grew

I have the honor to refer to the Emoassy's despatch
176
No. 1145, dated January 25, 1935, in regard to the diffi-
culties now being experienced y American and other foreign
oil interests in Japan and to the conversations now being
held in Tokyo between representatives of those interests
and representatives of the Japanese Government, and to re-
port that the conversations have been progressing steadily,
although as yet the results thereof have not been embodied
in any final form.
FILED
Progress

=== Page 79 === (confidence 0.98)
-2-
Progress of the Conversations.
The memoranda of the conversations are lengthy and
deal largely with intricate technical details, and it is
believed that no useful purpose would be served in having
then all copied and transmitted to the Department.
If the
Department so desires, however, this will be done.
During the conversation of January 23, 1935, it was
decided that, as the various points of the Petroleum In-
dustry Law and its enforcing reguletions, as well as the
methods of application of the Law, ha been thoroughly
discussed in the meetings already held, the foreign oil
companies should draw up a nemorandum embodying their views
in regard to the difficulties stil confronting them in
connection with the Law and its application. A draft memo-
randum was therefore p epared and handed to the Vice Minister
for Commerce and Industry and to Mr. Kurusu, of the Foreign
Office, on January 25, 1935. A copy of this memorandum,
which will explain the progress of the conversations up to
1/
that time, is enclosed herewith. On January 30, 1935, the
representatives of the forcign oil inte ests met the Japan-
ese officials for the purpose of discussing the memorandum
and of deciding upon any alterations which might be necessary
to make it acceptable to the Japanese Government. As this
conversation was rather important, as indicating the wide
divergency of views which still exists between the foreign
oil companies and the Japanese Government, a copy of the
21
memorandum of the conversation is enclosed. As is indicated
in the memorandum of the conversation, the two sides are not
yet agreed on the basis for allotting quotas, on the stock-
keeping

=== Page 80 === (confidence 0.99)
-3 -
keeping question and on the price-fixing problem.
On February 4, 1935, the Vice Minister for Commerce
and Industry delivered to the two foreign oil companies
his draft reply to the draft memorandum. A copy of the
3/
reply is enclosed herewith. It vil? be noted th:t, ac-
cording to the draft reply, the Ministry of Commerce and
Industry is not willing to give a guarantee of a percentage
of the oil tra e; that while the Department of Commerce and
Industry does not intend to deprive the foreign oil com-
panies of their vested interests, the legislators of Japan
intend to encourage refining by giving more favoreble cuo-
tas to domestic refiners than to importers; that the De-
partment of Comerce and Industry will con uct a study of
the compar:tive advantages of refining in Japan and of im-
porting refined products, with thc result that (1) if the
importation of refine products is foun to be more advan-
tageous, the foreign oil companies wil? be relieved of their
anxieties, and (2) if refining in Japan is found to be more
advantageous, the present importers cn protect their in-
terests by refining in Japan; that after the slack Japanese
refining capacity is filled "the Department will not make
any unfair treatment arbitrarily as between the refiner
and importer"; that the foreign oil companies cannot hope
for any subsidies for stock-keeping or for any postponement
of the application of the regulations governing stock-keeping,
but that the Department of Commerce and Industry will be as
lenient as possible in this regard within the scope of the
existing regulations; and that in the future the Department
of Commerce and Industry will take the cost of imported oil
into

=== Page 81 === (confidence 0.99)
into consideration in fixing prices of oil.
It is unnecessary to state that the foreign oil in-
terests consider this reply as most unsatisfactory.
More disturbing than the unsatisf ctory character of
the reply itself, however, was a vorbal message delivered
with the draft reply. This, as translated by the Rising
Sun Petroleum Company, was as follows:
" This document is a draft of the official
answer to your reuircnent,made by Mr.
Yoshino. If you are satisfied with its con-
tents Mr. Yoshino wil get the approval of
the Minister and issue the official letter
in due course. He is ready to make an ap-
pointment to discuss your amendments in
this draft if they are small points, like
wording or other minor points, but if you
are not satisfied totally an on the whole,
he would not find any necessity to make an
appointment of meeting."
This appeared to constitute an ultimatum. The foreign oil
interosts could either accept the draft reply substantially
as it was, or the conversations would cease. The represen-
tatives of the oil interests consequently conferred on
February 5 with Mr. Sansom of the British Embassy, Count
von Rechteren of the Netherlands Legation and är. Dickover
of this Embassy. It was decided that the only possible
course of action consisted of an attempt to ascertain exact-
ly what Mr. Yoshino meant by his message. Mr. Sansom had
an appointment with Mr. Kurusu of the Foreign Office for
the following morning, and volunteered to make inquiries
regarding the message. On February 6 he informed Mr. Kurusu
in regard to the message and stated that the foreign companies!
feelings with regard to Mr. Yoshino's reply were that the
amendments which they wished to propose were more than small
points, and therefore they were called upon to decide whether
or

=== Page 82 === (confidence 0.99)
- 5 -
or not Mr. Yoshino's message closed the door to further
discussion. At the same time he expressed the opinion
that the reply of Mr. Yoshino, plus the oral amplifications
given before, would provide sufficient basis for further
discussions.
Mr. Kurusu was greatly surprised that such a message
had been sent by Mr. Yoshino and immediately instituted
inquiries regarding its authenticity. Later he informed
Mr. Sansom that the message which Mr. Yoshino hac intended
to convey with his reply was that Mr. Yoshino, as an offi-
cial, would find it very difficult to put any more in writ-
ing than was contained in the reply; that he did not want to
stop th conversations but on the contrary was anxious to
have them start again as soon as poss ble; and that while
he could not put more into writing, he thought that some
way could be found to have such oral agrecments as might be
reached confirmed in some way.
It is the general opinion among the oil interests and
the dipiomatic representatives concerned that Mr. Yoshino's
original message was a form of "bluff" - that ne tried by
this means to coerce the forcign oil companies into com-
pliance with the desires of th Japanese Government. When
his "bluff" was called, he promptly receded from his posi-
tion, indicating that the Japanese stand on the oil question
is not as firm as they would have us believe.
The conversations were therefore resumed on February 7.
According to the American representatives, there was a very
distinct change for the better in the atmosphere of the con-
versations when resumed. The Japanese officials intimated
that they would like to alter the oil regulations in favor
of

=== Page 83 === (confidence 0.99)
- 6 -
of the foreign oil companies, but that they could not do
so while the Diet is in session, because of the severe
criticism which would be directed against them. They
the efore asked for more time in which to endeavor to meet
the wishes of the foreign oil com anies. This sudden change
in attitude may have been due to a conviction that the
foreign oil firms cou'd not be "oluffed", or may have been
due to orders from above. At any rate, the situation now
app ars more hopeful than at any time since the conversations
started.
Quotas for 1935.
It will be remembered that the gasoline cuota granted
the Standard-Vacuum Oil Company for the last half of 1934
was somewhat under its actual sales for the first half of
the year, while the Rising Sun Petroleum Company was allotted
approximately the same amount as it ha sold during the first
six months and the Japanese refineries were allotted the
whole of the natural increase in trade (estimated at from
12 to 15 per cent annual1y). For 1935, however, the whole
of the natural increase of the trade was not given to the
Japanese refiners. It vas estimated that the increase in
the trade in 1935 would amount to 150,000 kilolitres and of
this amount 105,000 kilolitres were allotted to the Japanese
refiners and 45,000 kilolitres to the foreign importing
firms. This was undoubtedly done in order to quiet the com-
plaints of the foreign oil interests, at the same time
giving the bulk of the increase to the Japanese refiners
in accordance with the declared policy of the Japanese Govern-
ment. As omplained by Professor Yoshitaro Wakimura of the
Tokyo

=== Page 84 === (confidence 0.99)
- 7 -
Tokyo Imperial University in the JAPAN TIMES of February 6,
1935, Japan is somewhat afraid of antagonizing the foreign
oil producers:
" In the quota for the last half of 1934,
the natural increase was entirely given to
refiners, but in the quota for 1935, a
portion had been given to foreign firms.
In this respect the Japanese oil companies
are much disappointed. But as Japan does
not have the sources of crudc oil supply,
the Government cannot take a very strong
policy. The Government is to avoid the
conflict between the Japanese and foreign
firms as much as ossible, while fostering
the devclopmont of refin ng industry in
the country."
The obvious fact that the Japanese Government is only
permitting the foreign oil companies t, continue in business
because the country lacks oil resources and fears to an-
tagonize the supplying countries does not augur wel! for
the future of the American oil tradc in Japan.
The Search for New Sources of Suuply.
The HOCHI SHIMBUN of January 30, 1935, published an
article to the effect that Mexico, Peru, Colombia and other
countries hav: offered oil fields to Japanese oil companies
but that the Japanese oil companies, owing to lack of funds,
have been unable to take advantage of the offers. According
to the newspaper, the low price of gasoline and the obliga-
tions of the Petroleum Industry Law have caused financial
difficulties to the Japanese oil companies, who desire
either that a subsidized company be formed to exploit oil
fields abroad or that the price of gasoline be raised in
order that they may obtain funds to exploit fields themselves.

Your Excellency:
We wish to express to you our thnks for the great
courtesy that has been shown us and the assistance g ven us by
your Ministry in permitting us to discuss so fully as we have
been able to do during the past few weeks the difficulties
that appear to confront us in connection with the application
of the Petroleum Law No. 26 of 1934.
We have ventured to draw up a statement of its chief
difficulties as we see them, and as we have endeavoured to
present them to His Excellency, Mr. Yoshino, during our
conversations with him. We subnit it herewith in order that it
may be clear to Your Excellency wherein our anxiety lay, and
still does on some of the points to which reference is made.
Mr. Yoshino's kind explanations have gone a long way
towards relieving the anxieties held concerning the future,
but we hope that it will be possible for them to be removed
completely as a result of the replies which we have asked you
to be good enough to make to the cuestions covered by the
Memorandum.
Memorandum

=== Page 86 === (confidence 0.99)
DRAFT
MEMORANDUM TO BE SENT TO
THE MINISTRY OF COMMERCE
The Standard Vacuum Oil Company and the Rising Sun
Petroleum Company, Ltd. are very much concerned in that
certain features of the Petroleum Law of 1934 are ad-
versely affecting their interests. They fear that unless
these are corrected during the early stages of the operation
of the Law precedents may be created which it will be diffi-
cult to overcome at a later date. They are therefore
raising these cuestions now to enable the Department to give
definite rulings.
QUOTAS.
Prior to the new Law the joint share of the Gasoline
Market in Japan Proper enjoyed by these two Companies was
Gasoline
Japan materially greater than that allotted to them from time to
Proper.
time since the Law came into effect, and it appears to them
that a different measure has been used in judging the claims
of the local Refiners as compared with those of the Import-
ing Companies. To such an extent has this difference been
applied that these Importing Companies' (i.e. S.V.C. and
R.S.P.Co.) share of the total Gasoline trade has been
reduced by approximately 9% per annum, (30,000,000 A.G.
approximately in 1935) and given to Japanese interests.
The share of the trade enjoyed by the S.V.O.C. and the R.S.P.
Company prior to the date on which the Government first
requested them to restrict their deliveries for the benefit
of local Refiners was 54%, so that if their interests are
not to be sacrificed permanently it is to 54% that the two
Companies' trade should eventually return.
It

=== Page 87 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 =
It has been explained that the policy behind the
increase in the share of the trade given to Japanese
Refiners has been the necessity of finding an outlet
for surplus Japanese refining capacity:
Further:-
(a) That at the end of 1935 about 190,000
kilolitres slack capacity will remain to be filled
(b) That the Government does not intend to grant
permits for new refineries nor to allow extensions
that will increase the capacity of existing re-
fineries, until such extensions become necessary
to meet the existing Refiners' relative share of
increased consumption.
(c) That it is definitely the policy of the
Government not to discriminate gainst any of
the Companies now marketing Petroleum products
in Japan, and they particularly will not dis-
criminate as between local Japanese Companies
and the two Importing Companies referred to in
this Memorandum.
It has been further indicated:-
(d) That any reduction in consumption in the
future will be apportioned over all Companies in
relation to their respective shares of the trade.
It has been pointed out that not only is there a
substantially greater percentage of refining capacity shut
down in practically every other refining country as com-
pared with Japan (See Memo marked "Exhibit A".), but that as a
matter of fact it is a practical impossibility to operate
any

=== Page 88 === (confidence 0.99)
-3 -
any refinery up to its theoretical full capacity because
variations in operating conditions and seasonal fluctuations
in the demand for certain products necessitate a margin of
safety beyond which refineries cannot efficiently operate.
This margin is generally accepted as being at least 10%
of the capacity. If general practice in this connection
were accept d, then it is clear th't Japanese Refiners
will, as far as it is vossible to determine, be operating
at full practical capacity by the end of the current year.
However, even if this were not admitted, and assuming that
the consumption of Gasoline during the next few years will
increase at the rate of 12% per annum (which is substantially
less than the average of the past three years, which approx-
imated 14%) nd llowing Japanese Refiners 46% share of this
increase, then the full 100% of refining capacity will be
absorbed by the end of 1037. A Memorandum, marked "Exhibit
B", is attached, showing the calculation in detail.
If, therefore, as explained to the Companies, the
position cannot be disturbed for the year 1935, then by
stabilising the position of the various Companies concerned
on the basis of the quotas allowed them for 1935, but
allowing the present Importers to absorb 54% of the incr ase
in consumption in subsequent years, the Japanese Refiners
will not only find sufficient outlet to take care of their
present capacity by early 1938, but by about the middle
of the year 1937 will need to begin extending their
refineries in order to take care of their share of the
increasing consumption. Furthermore, in view of the
Japanese officials' statement that in the event of a
decrease

=== Page 89 === (confidence 0.99)
decrease in consumption the necessary sacrifice would be
spread proportionately over the Companies concerned, it
seems just that the same principle should apply in the
event of an increase in consumption and that all interest-
ed Companies should benefit proportionately to their share
of the trade.
Therefore it is desirable to make it clear that in
the judgment of the Companies the most practical solution,
both in fairness to all the Oil Companies and in the in-
terests of smooth auministration, is to establish quotas
on the basis of a percentage of the total trade.
It will be fully appreciated that, unless the Depart-
ment can reassure the two Companies on this as well as on
the other points set forth in this Memorandum, the present
position can but cause the most lively concern and give
rise to the gravest of doubts as to their ability to
continue to fulfil their share in the smooth and un-
interrupted supply of Petroleum products to Japan, which
is one of the main objects of the Law, and in fact as
to the ultimate value of their position in the Japanese
Gasoline market.
QUOTAS
As the Companies' quotas have been seriously reduced
Gasoline.
for 1935 it is understood that for these areas quotas based
Chosen
and
on the percentage of trade during the year 1934 will be
Taiwan
restored.
QUOTAS.
In connection with other products it is understood that
Other Pro-
ducts
existing shares of the trade will be accepted as the basis
and that all existing Companies will participate proportion-
ately in any increase or decrease in consumption.
With further reference to quotas it has been discussed
and, we believe, agreed that, should one Petroleum product
in

=== Page 90 === (confidence 0.99)
-5 -
in the future be developed in such a manner that it
eventually replaces another Petroleum product, the quota
originally held for the former product shall be trans-
ferred to the latter proauct. As an example, if Gaso-
line for the propulsion of automobiles, trucks, etc.
is displaced by nother Petroleum product, such as
Diesel Oil, the quota of each Company applying to
Gasoline should apply also to Diesel Oil for this
ourpose.
STOCK-KEEP-
In connection with the regulations relat ng to
ING.
stock-keeping, figures have been compiled with the
assistance of the Mining Bureau showing the esti-
mated cost to the Companies concerned. The initial
cost, anounting to bet=n ¥70,000,000 and ¥75,000,000.
for the whole Industry, an additional annual capital
cost of between ¥8,400,000. nd ¥9,000,000. to provide
for additional stocks and storage obligations arising
from increased consumption, as well as an annual
carrying charge amounting to between ¥7,100,000. and
¥7,600,000., even after making due allowance for all
stocks in the country and afloat, is so severe to both
local Refiners and Importers alike that without assist-
ance either monetary, or by the postponement, limitation
or a more practical interpretation of this part of
the regulations, the Petroleum Industry cannot hope to
put it into effect within the bounds of any sound
economic requirement.
REFINING.
It has already been explained that it is extremely
difficult to balance refinery out-turns with the demand
for products in any one market, which difficulty is not
apparent

=== Page 91 === (confidence 0.99)
- 6 -
apparent where several markets are supplied from the
same centre. An approximate balance is at present held
between local refining and imports. It is an accepted
principle that local refining without the equivalent amount
of local production and/or protective duties is uneconomic,
and has always been found to lead to hi her prices for
the consumer.
Further, producing countries re showing sins of
uneasiness th t crude vhich has hitherto been produced
and refined there is now being refined in markets where
little or no crude roduction exists to the detriment of
the producing country, and even restrictive measures in
some countries are being contemplated to compel refining
in the country of origin. This uneasiness would be
greatly increased should two international companies be
compelled as a result of th ir entering the refining
business in Japan to re-export distress surplus products
to other markets at present supnlied direct from producing
centres.
The two Importing Companies furnishing finished
products have therefore no plans at present for erecting
refineries. They uncerstand, however, that should they
desire to go into local refining permits will accordingly
be granted.
If the Industry is to realize the benefits intended
by the Law and stressed by the officials it is necessary
to have not only stabilization of prices but also a balance
of products. There should therefore be a closely appro-
ximated balance between the sales quotas granted to
Refiners and quotas for the importation of crude; other-
wise a surplus of crude will be run with resulting excess
of

=== Page 92 === (confidence 0.99)
-7 -
of refined products, and it will be impossible to
maintain the price structure sanctioned by the Government.
It is therefore felt by the two Importing Companies
that the Government should give the Industry some
assurances on this point.
PRICE-
Further, under the regulations the Government have
FIXING.
the right to control prices generally and to take over
stocks in case of emergency. It is clear that unless some
recognized cost and selling price formula, applying to all
Companies and to be used for the purpose of aetermining
a remunerative price basis for the whole Industry, as
well as the price at which stocks will be taken over in
an emergency, be adouted, great confusion will ensue
and complete uncert inty xist as to the return which the
Industry is to receive from time to time for its products.
It is pointed out that no Inaustry can hope to progress
and plan for the future unless it is given reasonable
assurance that the price it is to receive for its
products is at least equal to their cost plus a reasonable
profit. In this connection a sub-committee was appointed
to examine this cuestion and it is understood that the
Government representatives were in agreement with the
representatives of the Oil Companies that the formula
presented was acceptable in principle but were unwilling
at that time to comnit themselves to any detailed figures
or rates for the formula. It is submitted that the formula
loses its value if no rates are included. The Oil In-
dustry has sustained very substantial losses during the
past three years in Japan and the foreign Oil Companies
consider it of the greatest importance to know definitely
what

=== Page 93 === (confidence 0.99)
-8 -
what the Government has in mind as permissible prices
for the future. Therefore the Government is respectfully
requested to state what rates and detailed figures they
are willing to recognize in the above formula, the re-
sults of that forimula based n today's costs for both
Refiners and Importers, and to indicate when said formula
can be made fully effective to the Industry.
SECURITY
Security o tenure is ecually important ana necessary.
OF
TENURE。
Organizations built u? at great expense with the anproval
and acquiescence of Governments have just as much right to
claim protection for their position and to look upon their
interests as established rights as though a written contract
existed. The buruen of international trading would become
intolerable if Governments at will were to penalise one
class of capital in favour of another. It would destroy
the whole fabric of industrial security and trading.
Without security of tenure, no industry can prosper;
indeed it is abundantly clear that industry could not
exist at all under such circumstances. It is because
the two Companies fear that this and other fundamentals
are being withdrawn that they have respectfully requested
a statement from the Government as to its present and
future intentions.

Mr. Yoshino said he understood that larger quotas had

to aespalch
88.193m the
E.... bassy at Tokyo.
Reply of the Department of Commerce and
Industry to the Memorandum submitted by
Foreign Oil Compunies concerning the
Petroleum Industry Law.
1. While the Memorandum submitted by the foreign oil
companies seems to cover most of the item: discussed at our
meeting, it appears to this Department that in some part
it takes for granted such matters as the guarantee of trade
by percentage bout which  com lete gr ement of views
has not yet been attained. Therefore it is with regret
that this Department cannot accept the emorandum as it
1s.
2. The Department hopes that it is fully understoo by the
two foreign companies concerned that the object of the Petro-
leum Industry Law is not to deprive then of their vested
interests. It is true that the Law places certain new
burdens on the companies such as the maintenance of oil
stock, but, at the same time, it respects to the fullest
possible extent the established position of the companies
in the petroleum trade. The Department, therefore, in
compliance with the above principle, intends to exercise
the utmost fairness in cllotting the cuota to the com-
panies. On the other hand, our legislators desired, as is
natural in an oil consuming country like Japan, to en-
courage the refining in this country, and accordingly, so
long as no serious conomic dificulties intervene, it was
their intention to give a more favorable quota to the local
refiners as compared with the importer in the allocation
of any increase of oil consumption. The Department, however,
intends

=== Page 114 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
intends to conduct a careful c mparative study between the
advantage of encouraging the oil rufining in Jupan und that
of importing refined products from abroad, taking into
consideration the present world market condition as well :s
the situation now prevailing in J pan. There should be no
cause for a prehension on the art of i port rs, if the re-
sult of the said study indicates that it is more advantageous,
as the Memorandum states, t k   certain bal nce betleen
importation ana local rufining, by the mai: tenince of i-
portation of a cert in cmount of refined oil from abroad.
But eve: if the r sult of the study is to the contrary, there
will be a way left for the iaporters to protect their interests
by starting the refining business in Jupan. In suc : cuse,
the Department is ready t, grant the neces ary  rmits as
has already been co municated through the Denartment of Foreign
Affairs.
As repeatedly ex lained, as lon, as there exists slack
capacity in local refin ri s, special consideration in favor
of them will have to be made in the allotment of the cuota
until such cap&city is fully utilized, but thereafter the
Department will not make any unfair treatment arbitrarily
as between the refiner and i porter. Of this point, the
interested parties should be rest assured, in view of many
other precedents which exist in Japen in the administration
of similar cases.
3. As to the legal obligation of stock-holding, it is
inevitable that the said requirement will entail an increase
in

=== Page 115 === (confidence 0.98)
- 3 -
in expenditure, but this is a problem which both the
importer and refiner are equally confronted with and, since
the local refiners are now und rtaking the construction
of more tanks in complience with the Law, the Department
cannot but hope that the two foreign oil co p:nies will :lso
prepare, as soon as os: ible, a concrete plan for th legal
storage.
In this connection, the Department thinks that the
provision of subsicies or the postponement of a) lication of
these regulations cin hardly be c ntem lated. But it is
the intention of the Department that, in the interpretation
as well as ap lication of the regulations concerning the
legal stock-holding, lenient consideration vithin the scope
of the said regulations, will be accorded in actual cases,
in order that the require ents of the Law may be carried out
satisfactorily with the least possible :acrifice to the
business of the i  orter.
4. As has already been explained the Petroleum Indusiry
Law does not aim at the establishment of the system of rice-
fixing by the government. But a b : y fall in price on
account of severe competitions amon the oil companies in
the past and their subsecuent attempt to raise the price
sudcenly gave rise to a social problem involving a multitude
of consumers. Under the abnormal circumstance thus brought
about which does not allow the raisin of price, however
reasonable, to be sudden and sharo, the Government is at
present forced into a position to interfere in the decision
of the prices. But it is the earnest desire of this Department
that the time will soon come hen prices will naturally adjust
itself

=== Page 116 === (confidence 0.98)
- 4 -
itself in accordance with the condition of the oil market.
The Department recognizes that in case the Government has to
resort to its authority, provided in Article 7 of the Petro-
leum Industry Lav concerning th adjustment of prices, it is
necessary to calculte the cost of oil nd to devise a certain
formula as the basis of the s id calculation. The Depariment,
however, is of the opinion that the basic figures or percentages
to be used in such a formula should be determined from time
to time vhen such cases prcsent th ms Ives.
This department holds the view that, by raising the
price of gasoline by 3.5 sen per gallon, as recuested by the
refiners, the local refiners t present are able to carry on
their undertaking at a profit if their business is reasonably
oper:ted. Should the price of the imported gsoline be higher
than this, the Department expect the importers to adjust
their price accordingly. If reverse is the case, it would be
unnecessary for the Governnent to take into consideretion
the price of the imported gasoline. This is the reason why
this Department did not, for the time being, take into account
the price of the imported gasoline. In future, however, the
Department intends to calculate, for reference in finding
a reasonable price, the price of the imported oil according
to a certain formula.

example. In that connection, Mr. John R.

foreign interests operating in Japan with
Japanese interests should be determined, it
seems to me, from the point of view of
whether or not such association would be
profitable to foreign interests concerned
and not, as Dickover apparently suggests,
by the question of control. The foreign
oil interests are operating in Japan as
distributors and not as manufacturers, and
it is obvious that for them the question of
being associated with Japanese involves
considerations that do not apply in the
case of Americans and other foreigners
operating factories in Japan. However, the
implication of Dickover's statement, that
American
