The Honorable
The Secretary of State,
Washington.
Sir:
I have the honor to refer to the Embassy's despatch
1181
No. 1182, dated February 21, 1935, and to previous corres-
pondence in regard to the difficulties being experienced
by American and British-Dutch oil interests in Japan under
the new Petroleum Industry Law, and to report the follow-
ing developments in the situation.
FLLED
Conversations.
As reported in my despatch No. 1l82, the special re-
presentatives of the foreign oil interests proposed a draft
F
memorandum
/C

=== Page 138 === (confidence 0.99)
-2 -
memorandum embodying their interpretation of the results
of the conversations and their desiderata in regard to
the Government's operation of the Petroleum Industry Law.
This draft memorandum was delivered to the Japanese offi-
cials on February 14, and was studied by them for some time
before any comments were nade thereon. On February 25, Mr.
Kurusu asked Mr. Neville of this Embassy and Mr. Sansom of
the British Embassy to meet him at the Tokyo Club to discuss
the matter. A copy of the memorandum of their conversation
1/
is enclosed. From this it will be noted that Mr. Yoshino,
the Vice Minister for Commerce and Industry, could not ac-
cept the preamble, which read as follows:
"This memorandum covers questions relating
to the Petroleum Law which have been discussed
between Mr. Yoshino and the two companies, and
it is understood that Mr. Yoshino agrees with
the conclusions reached and will use his best
efforts to secure the agreement of the Minister
of Commerce and all other Departments concerned.
Mr. Yoshino feared that this statement would place him in
an embarrassing position with! his colleagues in the Government,
would make it appear that concessions were being made under
pressure from the foreign firms rather than spontaneously,
would be likely to antagonize officials of other Departments,
and would thereby injure the cause of the foreign oil com-
panies. Mr. Neville and Mr. Sansom agreed to convey this point
of view to the foreign oil companies, with a view to having
the preamble withdrawn. Mr. Kurusu also brought up the sub-
ject of the postponement of the discussions. This was asked,
he said, for two reasons. One was the obvious one of avoid-
ing questions in the Diet, and the other was to give Mr.
Yoshino time to discuss the question of stock-keeping with
the Japanese oil companies, who up to the present had been
letting

=== Page 139 === (confidence 0.99)
-3 -
letting the foreign firms fight their battles for them.
Mr. Yoshino proposed to ask them to state definitely
whether or not they would store six months' supplies of
oil; if they would, the present regulations would stand, the
foreign oil companies would probably withdraw from Japan,
and the Japanese oil companies would have to carry the ad-
ditional burden that was to have been borne by the foreign
companies; if they would not carry six months' stocks, let
them say so and join with the foreign oil companies in a
petition for a change in the regulations.
Mr. Kurusu's message was transmitted to the represen-
tatives of the foreign oil interests, who consented to delete
the preamble from the memorandum.
On February 27 and 28 the special representatives of
the foreign o1l interests discussed the memorandum with Mr.
Yoshino and Mr. Kurusu. The meetings were not successful
from the point of view of the foreign oil companies. The
Japanese officials "backed water" on several of the points
upon which the foreign oil men understood agreoments in prin-
ciple had been reached, and proposed numerous important
alterations in the memorandum - changes which would have had
the effect, had they been accepted, of emasculating the
document. Mr. Yoshino and Mr. Kurusu also claimed that they
had no authority at this stage of binding any of the depart-
ments of the Government, and that they would have to refer
the whole question to other officials and ascertain their
views before committing themselves. This procedure would
take a week or two, according to Mr. Yoshino, and he there-
fore asked that the conversations be suspended for a time.
The

=== Page 140 === (confidence 0.99)
-4 -
The special representatives of the foreign oil com-
panies readily consented to the postponement of the dis-
cussions, as they saw clearly that they were making no
progrees and as they believed that (1) no concessions
could be obtained from the Japanese while the Diet was in
session, and (2) their absence for a month would give the
officials of the Department of Commerce and Industry an
opportunity to arrange with other Government Departments
(principally the War, Navy and Overseas Departments) and
with the Japanese oil companies for some alteration in
the stock-keeping and other objectionable regulations.
They therefore arranged to leave Japan on March 2 and to
return (if the Japanese officials still desired to continue
the conversations) on April 1.
On March 1, before the departure of the special repre-
sentatives from Japan, Mr. Kurusu of the Foreign Office had
separate talks with Mr. Sansom of the British Embassy and
Mr. Neville of this Embassy, in which he stated that Mr.
Hashimoto, the President of the Nippon Oil Company,had ap-
proached the Department of Commerce and Industry with a view
of obtaining some alteration in the stock-keeping require-
ments. The Department of Commerce and Industry had suggested
that the Japanese oil companies get in touch with the foreign
oil companies and present a joint petition, and therefore Mr.
Kurusu wished the Embassies to transmit this information and
suggestion to the American and British oil interests before
the special representatives left Japan. A memorandum of Mr.
21
Kurusu's conversation with Mr. Neville is enclosed herewith.
The information was duly transmitted to the American oil in-
terests, and before their departure, the special representatives
of

=== Page 141 === (confidence 0.99)
- 5 -
of both the Standard and Shell interests sent replies
through their respective Embassies to Mr. Kurusu to the
effect that they, or their local ropresentatives, would
be glad to talk to r. Hashimoto at any time. The spe-
cial representatives of the oil companies, both American
and British, left Yokohama on March 2 and there have been
no further developments in the situation to the date of
writing.
The Embassy has endeavored to obtain a copy for the
Department of the memorandum which the oil interests pre-
pared and left with the Japanese officials, but the repre-
sentatives of the oil interests felt that the situation was
still too nebulous and that the memorandum was subject to
too many changes to render it of any value as a reference.
A copy of the menorandum was, however, shown to a member of
the Embassy staff for his information. It asked that, in
addition to their present quotas, the foreign oil companies
be granted 50 per cent of the natural increase in the oil
trade, after the idle refining capacity in Japan hac been
filled; it agreed to store three months' supplies of oil by
April 1, 1935, provided that all oil in Japan, in whatever
packages contained, and all oil in transit to Japan, be con-
sidered as part of the stock; it suggested, however, that
the foreign oil interests were not prepared to go further
than this in the matter of oil storage; and it asked that
in the matter of price-fixing the formula devised by the
foreign oil companies be taken into consideration.
(This last desiderata perhaps requires some explanation.
The import tariff of Japan imposes a low duty on crude oil
and

=== Page 142 === (confidence 0.99)
-6 -
and a much higher duty on refined oils, the differential
being around five sen per gallon in the case of gasoline.
That is, gasoline distilled from imported crude oil should
cos about five sen less per gallon than gasoline imported
with the import duty. There would be no objection to this
under ordinary conditions - it is a form of protection of
domestic industries com on throughout the world. In Japan,
under the new petroleum law and regulations, however, there
is no free market and open competition. The quantities which
can be imported and the price at which they can be sold are
fixed by the Government. The Government can, therefore, if
it desires, fix a maximum price somewhere within the five
sen differential, which will permit a reasonable profit to the
domestic refiners while causing a steady loss to the impor-
ters. The foreign oil companies' contention is that, if
the Government insists upon fixing maximum prices for oil
products in Japan, in fairness to the licensed importers,
(who, moreover, are required under penalty to carry out fully
any business plan approved by the Government), it should take
into consideration, in fixing the maximum price at which they
can sell, the importers' costs plus the import duty.)
The special representatives of the foreign oil interests
left with the Japanese officials their memorandum, without
accepting the alterations proposed by the Japanese officials.
The representatives of the oil interests believe that, during
their absence from Japan, the officials will work sincerely
to obtain some modifications in the regulations which will
ameliorate the conditions under which the foreign oil com-
panies must operate in Japan, but they are not at all
optimistic

=== Page 143 === (confidence 0.98)
- 7 -
optimistic in regard to the probable results, inasmuch as
they are convinced that tie law and enforcing regulations
are largely designed from a national defense point of view,
and that therefore it will be difficult, if not impossible
under present conditions, t) obtain the consent of the Army
and Navy to any substantial modifications. They believe
that before such modifications are consented to, it will
be necessary for them to give the Japanese Government de-
finitely to understand that, unless some substantial al-
terations are made in the enforcing regulations, the foreign
oil interests will withdraw from Japan, with all the impli-
cations of shortage of supply that such withdrawal will
cause. The special representatives of the oil interests
have on several occasions implied to the Japanese officials
that, as it is not comnercially feasible to operate in
Japan under present conditions, they might not be able to
continue in business here, but they have not yet definite-
ly come out into the open and stated that they would with-
draw from Japan if some amelioration of the situation can-
not be obtained. They have decided to retain this warning
for use if, after their return to Japan, they find that the
officials have been unable to obtain any substantial modi-
fications of the regulations. It is probable that the
Japanese oil interests would welcome the withdrawal of the
foreign oil companies from Japan, but it is believed that
more powerful interests would oppose such a move, because
of the international ill-feeling which it would occasion and
because of the threat to the nation's oil supply.
Representations.
Upon the departure from Japan of the special representa
t1ve

=== Page 144 === (confidence 0.99)
- 8 -
representatives of the oil interests, I took occasion to
confer with the British Ambassador regarding the probable
outcome of the negotiations. He concurred in my opinion
that, as the negotiations had reached a point where the
officials had to approach other Governmental departments
for their approval of alter t ons in the regul tions, it
would be helpful if we asked the Minister for Foreign Af-
fairs, in an informal way, to use his good offices with
his colleagues in the Cabinet to thc end that a solution
of the difficulties facing the foreign oil coapanies
might be found. Acting under authorizations previously
received (especially the Department's telegram No. 210,
December 20, 8 p.m.), therefore, I mentioned the matter
to Mr. Hirota informally on March 2, 1935, stating that the
special representatives of the foreigr oil interests, who
had left Japan but vho would return in about a month, hoped
that some concrete solution of their difficulties might be
found during their absence, to the end that they might be
in a position to proceed with their business in Japan in
the future with some degree of confidence. I also expressed
the hope that the Minister for Foreign Affairs would be kind
enough to endeavor to accomplish that purpose by using his
good offices with the interested members of the Cabinet. The
Minister replied that the whole question of oil is an im-
portant subject in the Diet - he used the words "burning
question" without, I believe, any attempt at a pun - and
for this reason he felt that it would be better if the
question were allowed to lie dormant until the Diet rises.
After that, he thought, it would be possible to arrive at
some satisfactory agreement in regard to the matter. He
then

=== Page 145 === (confidence 0.99)
- 9 -
then proceeded to comment on the fact that Japan is depen-
dent upon the United States for its supplies of oil, indi-
cating his appreciation of the fact that it would be un-
wise to antagonize the large American oil interests.
The British Ambassador will take similar action the
next time he sees r. Hirota.
Supplies of Oil to Japan.
Both the Standard and Shell interests, resentful of
their treatment in Japan and Manchuria and possibly fear-
ful of the rise in the Far East of strong Japanese compe-
tition, are apparently determined to prevent, if possible,
the acquisition by Japan of new and adequate sources of
supply of crude oil. The Standard interests are working
slowly, it appears, toward shutting off supplies from in-
dependent producers in California, the principal one of
these, strange as it may seem, being the Standard Oil Com-
pany of California, which apparently has no connection with
the New York and New Jersey companies, except that a small
percentage of the stock of the California company is owned
by the Rockefeller interests. Recently a report was re-
ceived by the foreign oil interests, through the Netherlands
Legation, to the effect that a Dutchman was in Japan for
the purpose of ped ling to the Japanese oil companies cer-
tain prospecting rights in Portuguese Timor, in the East
Indies. The special representatives of the Standard and
Shell interests promptly telegraphed to their representa-
tives at The Hague to acquire jointly these prospecting
rights, in order to shut out any possible Japanese purchase.
Strictly

=== Page 146 === (confidence 0.98)
-10-
of 52064 f1 uald 1. Simm, State Dapl
Dowle3///6
STRIOTME SORIDENTIAL
Prospects of the Negotiations.
While thc special representatives of the foreign oil
interests are inclined to be pessimistic with regard to
the outcome of their conversations with the Japanese author-
ities, it is my opinion that the situation is not so hope-
less as might appear on the surface. First, it is not an
unusual procedure for Japanese negotiators to give the
appearance of intransigence until they ascertain that the
other side is obdurate, when they will suddenly shift their
position and make the concessions necessary for an agreement.
Second, it is obvious that the Japanese authorities can make
no concessions under pressure from the foreign interests
without bringing severe criticism upon themselves from mem-
bers of the Diet. No conceesions can the efore be expected
while the Diet is in session. Third, it is my opinion that
the Japanese authorities realize that it would not be the
part of wisdom for a country poor in oil resources to an-
tagonize the two greatest oil interests in the vorld - an
opinion which has becn strengthened by Mr. Hirota's remark
to me on March 2, quoted above. For these reasons I believe
that the special representatives of the oil interests may
find the path to an agreement easier upon their return to
Japan than it was during their recent conversations.
Respectfully yours,

Memorandum of Conversation of
February 25, 1935
Mr. Kurusu
Mr. Neville

to despatch
Mr. Sansom
No /193 of anc 8,1935 from the
Embassy at Tokyo:
011
Mr. Kurusu asked Mr. Neville and Mr. Sansom to meet
him at 5 p. m. at the Tokyo Club.
He wished to speak to them about the Japan oil problem.
The companies had put in a (draft) memorandum stating their
understanding of the position, their own requirements, and
the methods by which those requirenents could be met by the
Japanese Government. This memorandum was prefaced by a
preamble stating:
"This memorandun.....covers questions relating to
the Petroleum Law which have been discussed
between Lir. Yoshino and the two companies, and
it is understood that Mr. Yoshino agrees with
the conclusions reached and will use his best
efforts to secure the agreement of the Minister
of Commerce and all other Departments concerned."
Mr. Kurusu said that this preamble (which he understood
Mr. Godber to have drafted) could not be accepted in its
present form by Mr. Yoshino. This was not because he
disagreed with the memorandum, though it was true that it
contained a number of points of detail which he could not
say that he accepted. But any categorical statement in
writing of the kind suggested would place him in an
embarrassing position with his colleagues, particularly
those in the Overseas Department who have jurisdiction in
Formosa and Korea.
Mr. Kurusu explained that therefore, not only from the
point of view of Mr. Yoshino, but also in the companies'
own interests, it would be better not to risk antagonizing
other

=== Page 148 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
other offisials; and in general it was best to make it
appear as far as possible that conoessions were being made
pontaneously by the Japanese authorities, and not under
pressure.
Mr. Neville and Mr. Sansom stated that they understood
this point of view. Mr. Neville urged that it was natural
and proper for the companies, before their representatives
left, to desire some assurance that they had got to a
definite stage in the negotiations and that an endeavour would
be made to meet them on the basis which they had reached after
protracted discussions. They did not want to come back and
find that they must start all over again.
Mr. Sansom asked whether, in default of Mr. Yoshino's
acceptance of the preamble, Mr. Kurusu could give to Mr.
Neville and himself an oral assurance that he and Mr. Yoshino
would work for the acceptance of the memorandum. If Mr.
Kurusu would give them such an assurance they might feel able
to recommend to the companies that the preamble should be
withdrawn.
Mr. Kurusu said that naturally Mr. Yoshino and himself
were prepared to work for general acceptance of the points
raised; stock keeping, price fixing, or "Control", tenure of
business and quota allotments. He said that he could not,
nor could Yoshino, bind himself in matters of detail.
In
general, however, he could assure us that the memorandum
would be the basis on which Mr. Yoshino would negotiate with
his colleagues. He said that the oil men had come from a
great distance, had been very patient and tactful and that
they could not be requested to suspend the negotiations without
some assurance that the work already done would be resumed
pere they had left off, and not thrown overboard. He said
that neither Yoshino nor himself wanted to sit through another
series

=== Page 149 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
series of meetings going over ground already covered. The
oil men could be sure of that and of Mr. Yoshino's desire to
obtain the agreement of his colleagues to a fair arrangement
along the lines of the memorandum. To commit himself in
advance, however, would weaken him with his colleagues, and
in case of disagreement with some of them, place him in a
position where he might have to resign, according to Japanese
ideas. Mr. Neville said that he would be willing to place
this point before the oil men, and ask them to reconsider
Mr. Yoshino's attitude. Mr. Sansom s aid that he would do
the same.
Mr. Kurusu then said that the object of postponement of
the disoussions at this time was two-fold. One, of course,
was to avoid questions in the Diet, and the other was to deal
with the stock-keeping situation. He said that the Japanese
oil interests were very quiet now because they thought that
the foreign oil companies would get the stock keeping require-
ments reduced and that they would benefit from it without any
effort on their part. It was Mr. Yoshino's intention to say
to them that the foreign oil men had gone away feeling that
the stock-keeping requirements were too onerous; that they
felt that they might have to suspend operations on that
account; that the Government wanted to know whether the
Japanese oil interests could comply with the law and regulations
as they stood; if so the Government would make no concessions
and the regulations would not be changed; further, the Govern-
ment would have to play fair with the foreign oil companies
and if they were told that the regulations would not be
changed, the regulations would remain as they are now, and
the Japanese companies held to their word with the additional
burden that was to have been borne by the foreign companies;

=== Page 150 === (confidence 0.98)
- 4 -
if, on the other hand, the Japanese interests felt that the
regulations were too onerous, they should speak up, join
with the foreign interests, and come to some understanding
as to what changes should be made. He said that this was
the line which Mr. Yoshino intended to take with the Japanese
oil interests in regard to stock keeping; stock-keeping and
price control he felt were matters common to both the Japanese
oil refiners and the importers. Tenure of business and quotas
primarily, he said, affected importers. In regard to the
latter, it was intended to have the importers participate
in future increase of business, as well as retain their
present quotas, on a basis that would assure the operation
of the refineries in Japan.

to despatch

Conversation
March 1, 1935
Mr. Neville and Mr. Kurusu.
011.
Mr. Kurusu called me on the telephone and asked me
to come to see him at 5 P.M. When I reached his office
he told me that he and Mr. Yoshino had told the Japanese
oil companies that they could not expect to obtain any
favors which were not accorded to foreign oil companies
in the administration of the oil control laws, and had
spoken to them along the lines that he had spoken to me.
He said that Mr. Hashimoto, of the Japan Oil Company, had
been to the Department of Commerc: and Industry and had
told the Vice Minister that the Japanese companies would
have to have a reduction in the storage requirements. Mr.
Yoshino had suggested to Mr. Hashimoto that he should get
in touch with the foreign oil companies and if there was a
joint petition of all the important oil concerns, it would
be easier for the Department of Commerce and Industry to
take the matter up sympathetically. Mr. Kurusu asked me
to pass this information on to the American oil men, saying
that he had already spoken to Sansom who had agreed to tell
the Shell people. I said that I should do so.
He then referred to previous conversations, saying
that prices and storage requirements were matters that
were the common interest of all persons in the oil business.
I said that the first thing that I should be asked was
whether

=== Page 152 === (confidence 0.96)
- 2 -
whether Joining with the Japanese companies would alter
the status of the discussions that had taken place with
Mr. Yoshino. He said it would not; that it would improve
matters considerably, in his judgment.

RANDUM OF MATTERS DISCUSSED BETWEEN THE MINISTRY
COMMERCE, THE STANDARD-VACUUM OIL COMPANY AND THE
RISING SUN PETROLEUM COMPANY
The object of the Petroleum Law No.26 of 1934
being to provide for a smooth and uninterrupted supply
of Petroleum Products to Japan it is recognized that to
achieve this object it is important for Japan to place
no undue restrictions on the importation of Petroleum
products nor to deprive the two Compani es of their vested
interests. It follows therefore that the esteblished
position of the Companies in the Petroleum Trade is to
be respected.
Further it is definitely not the policy of
the Departcent to discriminate against any of the
Companies now marketing Petroleum and its products in
Japan,Chosen and Taiwan, and in particular they will
not discriminate against the two Companies.
JOTAS
Gasoline quotas have been provided for in the
casoline
Japan Proper letter to His Excellency the Minister of Commerce da ted
9th February l935. That letter provides for the fol-
lowing:
GASOLINE
The two Companies will receive in the first
Japan Proper
place the quota already granted for the year l935.
Taking into account the slack refining capacity
existing in the country and sanctioned to dete, the
Department will decide beginning lst January 1936 and for
the three years subsequent thereto to allocate the first
66,500 Kilolitres increase in the consumption of Gasoline
to the Refiners. The balance of the increase in
consumption during that period will be allocated pro rata

=== Page 165 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
to the importers.
Should Matsukata give up his quota or part of his
quota it is understood that the quantity so rendered aveilable
will be allocated pro rata to the two companies as they have
already given up a greater share or the trade than is repre-
sented by Matsukata's quota.
Beginning 1st January 1939 the tonnage
equivalent to the consumption of 1938 shall be divided
in accordance with the division of trade in l938 and
of the increase in consum tion during 1939 and subsequent
years 5o% will be alloceted pro rata to the importers.

EFINING.
Although this undorstanding does not contem-
plate the provision of Refineries by either the Stendard-
Vacuum Oil Company or the Rising Sun Company,it is recog-
nized that should these two Importers desire to construct
Refineries in the territories referred to the Department
is ready to grant the necessary permits within the
limits of the two Compnies' quota whenever the two
Companies request them to do so.
It is further understo d that to re&lise the
benefits intended by the Law it is necessary to secure a
balance between refined products imported,those refined
locally and quotas for Crude importations, and to this
end the competent Authorities concerned will closely
approximate the sales quotas granted to Refiners and
their Cuotas for the importation of Crude,thus avoiding
such a surplus of Crude end/or products as would endanger
the whole scheme of stabilization and price control.
RICE-FIXING
The Compenies have been informed that the
Petroleum Industry Law does not aim et the establishment
of a system of price fixing by the Government.
It is clear,however, thet as the Government
has the right to exercise its authority under Article ?
and take over stocks in time of emergency, a certein

=== Page 170 === (confidence 0.99)
5.
mormula must be devised so that the Industry
know the return it will receive for its products
such an event.
As regards the price currently quoted in
the market for Petroleun: Products, although it has been
stated that the Department has no desire to make a
practice of interfering in the fixing of prices, on
those occasions in the pest when they were forced to do
so they base their consideretions entirely on the cost
to local Refiners end did not take into account the
price of imported products. In view, however, of the
fact that the competent Authorities concerned reslize
that the importation of the necessary quantities of
Refined Products should not be discoureged, it is under.
stood that it is the intention of the Department in
finding a reasonable price in future to permit prices for
imported Cil as well s locally produced products
which will meke possible a fair p ofit to ll concerned.
A formula for these purposes has therefore been devised
and it is atteched to this Memorandum,merked "Exhibit
B".
At the suggestion of the Department a
memorandum is ettached (merked Exhibit C") bringing
out more clearly why the Compenies feel that present
prices are too low and also whya price formula should
be adopted.
Parallel with the necessity for a smooth
and uninterrupted supply of Petroleum Products is the
desirability of enabling all Compenies to plan ahead
so that an efficient and economic supply of these
products remains available at all times to the
consumer. Further, the Law did not intend to
encourage destructive competition or uncertainty

=== Page 171 === (confidence 0.98)
6.
as to the future but that the Industry in the
peaceful pursuit of its development should receive
a fair return on its investment. Such being the case
those concerns who have been responsible for the
initiative of pioneering development,particularly
when for the good of the community the Government
decides to submit that Industry to restrictive
regulations, seem to be entitled to some measure of
ecuriy of tenure. The Companies are satisfied
that with these understandings confirmed they will
be able to continue to play their full part with
the balance of the Industry in its continued growth
and along with them develop ways and means of securing
to the public its supolies of Petroleum Produc ts
efficiently and economicelly. It is suggested there-
fore that a period should be placed upon licenses
granted to the various sections of the Industry and
that a minimum period of ten years should be allowed.

=== Page 172 === (confidence 0.99)
EXHIBIT "A"
STOCK-KEEPING.
In connection with the regulations relating to stock-
keeping figures have been compiled with the assistance of
the Mining Bureau showing the estimated cost to the industry.
To maintain a cleer six months" stock the initiel cost in
equipment and products for the whole industry is expected
to amount to between ¥70,000,000 and ¥75,000,000, details
of which are available. To maintain these stocks in re-
lation to increasing consumption the industry will heve to
provide an additional annuel capital sum of between ¥8,500,000
and ¥9,000,000. In addition the annual carrying cost of
these stocks will amount to at least ¥7,000,000 annually.
The cost of products has been taken at today's value,
but it is lo compared with the past. If in couse of time
the value of products rises the additional annual capital sum
and carrying cost would correspondingly increase. For this
reason the industry will never be able to celculate in edvance
the final cost of these regulations. Rarely has an industry
been placed in such an uncertain position. For normal stocks
it is a risk which cennot be avoided, but for excess stocks it
becomes a speculation which cennot be jus tified on commercial
grounds.
The stock is not needed for purely commercial purposes,
The history of the Companies here shows that stock ranging be-
tween 1 to 2 months is all that is necessary to protect indus-
try. The average stocks in other consuming countriesare
certainly not in excess of three months and in France, the
only country which is at present operating a Control Law, the
regulations call for three months' stock only. An excess
above this figure therefore is non-commercial and may be looked
upon as a dead asset.

=== Page 173 === (confidence 0.99)
2.
A six months' obligation costs the industry annually a
minimum of 15,000,000 quite apart from the enormous initial
capital expenditure of ¥75,000,000.
Therefore it is urged
thet not only lenient consideretion within the scope of the
regulations is necessary, but that the sacrifice of the in-
dustry could be limited and sound economic requirements fol-
lowed if it is admitted that the consumer and the industry
cannot pay for this expenditure.

3. (a)

(b)

economic than refining at producing point the Compenies
realise that there are times when owing to crude prices
being unduly depressed locel refining mey be temporarily
cheaper than importing refined products. The Compnies

at a reasonable price when the formulae for Refined Prod uwr ts
and Crude show different results. They suggest in view of
the officials' desire to let normel economic laws operate
so far as possible that the Government should not interfere
unless prices rise above the higher of the two price formulae.
(c)
In considering the economics of Refining locally versus
the Import of Refined products from a national point of
view, it is necessary to teke into account the revenue
derived from both operations,otherwise by manipulating
the tariffs almost any result may be obtained.

DEPARTMENT OF STATE

tre cmnutim nx Apri1 9, 1935.
ROM：
Tokyo's 1207 of March 22, 1935, in
regard to the Japan oil situation:
The principal representatives in Japan
of the Standard and Shell interests had a
conversation with the president of the
Nippon Oil Company. A copy of that
memorandum is attached. Although both the
native and foreign groups are willing to
make a concerted request to the Japanese
Government that the conditions which would
be imposed under the new petroleum law
be ameliorated, the two groups did not seem
to be able to agree in regard to what the
Japanese Government might be asked to do.
The reason for such disagreement is, of
course, the obvious one that although the
Japanese oil concerns can be confident that
the Japanese Government will protect their
interests, the foreign oil concerns have
no assurance that their interests in
Japan will be permanently safeguarded by
the Japanese Government.
It is also to be noted that the Japa-
nese Navy Department is reported to be
opposed to any amelioration of the require-
ments of the new law in regard to the
quantity of reserve stocks to be maintained
by

=== Page 195 === (confidence 0.98)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
- 2 -
by the oil companies.
A Japanese paper reports that efforts
to exploit an oil field in Borneo are to
be abandoned and that it is intended to
prospect for oil in Formosa.
The present despatch is an ad interim
report to cover the relatively unimportant
developments during the temporary absence
from Japan of the special representatives
of the foreign oil concerns.
894.6363/189.

=== Page 197 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
Conversations.
As reported in despatch No. l193, before leaving
Japan the special representatives of the foreign oil in-
terests agreed to a suggestion which came through Mr.
Kurusu, of the Forcign Office, that the forcign oil com-
panies should discuss the stock-keeping question with the
Japanese oil companies, with a view to taking joint steps
in an endeavor to obtain some amel oration of the stock-
keeping requirements. In compliance with this suggestion,
on March 12, 1955, Mir. Goold of the Standard-Vacuum 011
Company and Mr. Malcolm of the Rising Sun Petroleum Com-
pany had a conversation with Mr. Hashimoto, President of
1/ the Nippon Oil Company. A memorandum of the conversation
is enclosed herewith. It will be noted from this memoran-
dum that Mr. Hashimoto is of the opinion that the foreign
and Japanese oil companies should present a joint petition
asking either that a subsidy be granted to cover the cost
of stock-keeping, or that the price of gasoline be raised
for the same purpose, while the foreign oil companies wish
the stock-keeping requirements modified to, say, three months'
stocks instead of the present six months' stocks. A subsidy,
at least for alien firms, is out of the question, and the
foreign oil companies feel that the allocation of all or a
part of an increase in the price of gasoline to cover the
cost of stock-keeping would be unsatisfactory, partly because
it would not relieve them of the heavy initial cost of build-
ing tanks and filling them with oils, and partly because the
future value of any increase in the price of gasoline would
be

=== Page 198 === (confidence 0.99)
-3 -
be uncertain, due to the fluctuations in the cost of crude
oil and in the exchange value of the yen. The importers
of refined oils and the Japanese refiners stand in entire-
ly different positions in regard to the question of stock-
keeping. The refiners are reasonably certain of their fu-
ture, as thc Japanese Government will do its best to de-
velop the industry and therefore will protect the interests
of the refiners, while the importers are in no way certain
of their future in Japan. The investment of capital by the
refiners in more tanks and other storage facilities is not
unreasonable, as such tanks wil! certainly be required by
the future expansion of the industry, even if not required
for the compulsory storage of stocks, while the importers
of refined products cannot, as a conmercial proposition,
invest further capital in a market which seems certain to be
restricted, as far as they are concerned. The foreign im-
porters and the Japanese refiners therefore take different
views of the situation. They both desire amelioration of
the present circumstances, but they desire amelioration in
different ways. The Japanese refiners are willing (to some
extent) to carry the stocks required by the Government, but
wish the Government to bear the carrying costs or to permit
the cost to be passed on to the consumer. The foreign im-
porters are not willing for the reasons given above, to store
the stocks required and wish the Government to modify the re-
quirements. There seems to be no basis for joint action by
the Japanese refiners and the foreign importers.
On March 20, therefore, the local representatives of
the Standard-Vacuum Oil Company and the Rising Sun Petroleum
Company

=== Page 199 === (confidence 0.99)
- 4 -
Company, acting on instructions received from the special
representatives, who are now in HongKong, called on Mr.
Kurusu, of the Foreign Office, and informed him that the
basis for a joint petition proposed by Mr. Hashimoto of
the Nippon Oil Company was in their opinion impracticable
and unacceptable. After some discussion of the matter Mr.
Kurusu said that he would consult Mr. Yoshino, of the De-
partment of Commerce and Industry, in regar  to further
steps. The project for j int action by the Japanese and
foreign oil companies there rests at the moment.
The Japanese Government's Attitude.
In accordance with the agreement reached with the spe-
cial representatives of thc foreign oil interests before
their departure fron Japan, the Department of Commerce and
Industry has taken up with other governmental departments
the questions which have been discussed by the representa-
tives of the foreign oil interests. On March 1l Mr. Yoshino,
Vice Minister for Commerce and Industry, had a conference
with representatives of the Departments of War and Navy.
Nothing is yet definitely known of the results of the conference,
but from a casual remark of Mr. Kurusu (who did not attend
the conference but who is in close touch with Mr. Yoshino)
it appears that the Army and Navy representatives were not
willing to accede to the foreign firms' desires in regard
to the question of the mitigation of the stock-keeping re-
quirements. It is believed that the Foreign Office and De-
partment of Commerce and Industry representatives are really
convinced of the impracticability of the present regulations
and

=== Page 200 === (confidence 0.96)
- 5 -
and fearful of their effect upon Japan's foreign relations,
but that the Army and Navy constitute a stumbling block
to any modification of the requirements.
The Search for Qil.
The KOKUMIN SHIMBUN reports, under date of March 16,
that the Nippon Oil Company, wh c! has been working jointly
with the Mitsui Bussan Kaisha to exploit an oil field in
Borneo, has decided to abandon the Borneo project and to
concentrate its efforts on oil fields in Formosa. The
Japanese Navy has two reserve oil fields in Formosa, at
Takao and Kosen, which they have asked the Nippon Oil Com-
pany to exploit. The Company also intends to conduct pros-
pecting operations in the Hozan field in Formosa.
Respectfully yours,
)
Joseph C. Grew

Meeting at Tokyo Kaikan. March 12.1935
Present:
Mr. Hashimoto, President of the Nippon Oil Company
Mr. Horie
Sales Manager "
"(interpreter)
Mr. Malcolm
Rising Sun Petroleum Company
Mr. Goold
Standard-Vacuum Oil Company
The following is a synopsis of the important points
made by Hashimoto:
1. In view of the remarks made to Hashimoto by Messrs.Godber,
Walden and Parker (at the luncheon given by Mr. Shigemitsu)
concerning the advisability of cooperation between the
foreign and Japanese companies, Hashimoto wanted a frank
talk with Malcolm and Goold and had therefore suggested
the meeting.
2.
He knew of no discussion between himself and Yoshino or
any other Government official at which any suggestion
had been made that the foreign and Japanese companies
should unite in petitioning the Government to reduce
the stock-holding requirements.
(Note: Such a message had come to Goold on March lst
from Kurusu through Neville, 3 months having
been mentioned).
3.
Hashimoto stated that the Nippon Oil Company, possibly
with minor exceptions would meet the stock-holding re-
quirements of the law as of April lst (at a cost of
¥5,200,000 borrowed from the Banks) and that the Nippon
Oil Co. also intended to comply as of October 1st, 1935
(at a cost of about ¥12,000,000) having already commenced
construction of the tanks. He also stated that the price
for crude oil had as yet not been fixed but the necessary
*quantity" arrangements had been made. He stated the
Nippon

=== Page 202 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
Nippon Oil Co. might be "a little late but not much"
in having the tanks filled. As far as he knew the
other Japanese companies had made similar plans and
would also be able to meet the stock-holding obliga-
tions on the requisite dated. Hashimoto said, however,
that he fully realized the growing burden imposed by
stock-holding in future years, as business increased,
and was exceedingly worried about it.
4.
Hashimoto suggested a petition from all the oil companies
to the Government to grant a subsidy to pay for the cost
of stock-holding or failing this, authorization to ad-
vance the price of gasoline for such purpose. Hashi-
moto admitted there was no chance for a subsidy this
year at least, because the Co unerce Minister had dis-
cussed the question with the Finance Minister prior to
the presentation of this year's Budget and had learned
no funds could be appropriated.
5.
Hashimoto said the three party cartel (Rengo Kai) was
composed of the Nippon, Ogura and Mitsubishi and was
working smoothly and that Hayama and Aikoku had agreed
to join although Matsukata had been gradually drifting
away from the meetings of the Japanese companies. Hashi-
moto thought it was advisable for the foreign companies
to join in arranging a new cartel to include all the
oil companies, in which case he thought Matsukata might
come into line and market prices could be strengthened.
He suggested that the petition referred to in Par. 4
could be sent in the name of the new cartel which would
include all the oil companies.
6.
He promised to give the foreign companies a copy of the
Rengo

=== Page 203 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
Rengo Kai agreement for study saying that each parti-
cipant had to post guarantee funds, and that in general
it was similar to the old sixty party cartel which
involved exchange of figures fines for overtrading,
net price agreements, audits, etc.
7.
Hashimoto stated that existing gasoline prices returned
little or no profit to the Japanese companies who were
very dissatisfied with the price level.
8.
He suggested that second parties and dealers might join
in petitioning the Government for higher gasoline sell-
ing prices.
9.
He promised to let us know the amount of duty which the
Nippon allocated to gasoline when calculating gasoline
costs.
10. Hashimoto said he had rocently cabled Lon on, Berlin,
New York, etc. to ascertain the relationship between the
price of gasoline and the prices of meat, bread, etc.,
this for use in asking the Government to approve higher
gasoline prices in Japan.
The following is a synopsis of the principal points made
by the foreign oil company representatives:
1.
They wished to cooperate with the Japanese companies
as far as consistent but were surprised to learn that
the Japanese companies had no intention of petitioning
the Government to reduce the stock-holding requirements
say to 3 months or less.
2.
They thought that the gasoline selling price question
and the compulsory stock-holding question should be
kept separate and distinct in all cost calculations
and

=== Page 204 === (confidence 0.98)
- 4 -
and in any approach to the Government, pointing out
that apart from the added cost per gallon, arising
from stock-holding, was the question of whether or
not the foreign companies could consi er the enor-
mous capital investment required.
3.
They would like to have an opportunity of studying
the draft of the proposed petition which was to be
prepared by Hashimoto before they could state their
willingness to subscribe to it.
4.
They could meet the stock-holding requirements as of
April lst only as the result of the Government's
having placed a liberal interpretation on some sections
of the regulations.
5.
They favored in principle the establishment of a new
gasoline cartel embracing all companies,but before
committing themselves definitely wanted to know details,
and assuming the agreement would in the main take the
form of the existing three party cartel (Rengo Kai)
would first like an opportunity to study its text.
(Note: Hashimoto promised to furnish a copy of the
Rengo Kai agreement)
6.
They pointed out that the foreign companies had had their
gasoline business curtailed the last half of 1934 and
also for l935 and that therefore if they joined a cartel
they did not wish to be confronted with a suggestion
that all companies voluntarily restrict their sales on
a pro-rata basis should it be considered by the cartel
that the total gasoline sales plans approved by the
Government for l935 were in excess of actual consump-
tion.
7.

=== Page 205 === (confidence 0.99)
- 5 -
They thought the cost of selling and distributing should
be exchanged between al? companies and thought also
that the Government when authorizing a higher selling
price level would be more influenced by actual costs
than by conditions in other countries.
8.
That they had presented a memorandum to the Government
pointing out that the selling price of gasoline in
Japan was the lowest in the world and presenting argu-
ments why prices should be adv nced, and would be glad
to show Hashimoto a copy if he thought it would be of
any benefit.
(Note: Hashimoto replied that he would be very pleased
to receive a copy)
9.
They pointed out that if the foreign comnpanies found
the conditions imposed by the new control law were too
onerous nd had to withdraw from Japan, that this would
add immeasurably to the burden the Japanese oil com-
panies would have to bear in respect to stock-holding.
They said they somewhat doubted the wisdom of following
Hashimoto's suggestion to have second parties and deal-
ers petition the Government for higher prices which
might create unlooked for difficulties in view of the
difference in selling systems of the various companies,
etc., etc., however, they would like to think over the
suggestion.

The Honorable
The Secretary of State,
Washington.
Sir:
I have the honor to refer to the Embassy's despatch
FLED
189
No. 1207, dated March 22, 1935, and to previous corres-
pondence, written and telegraphic, in regard to the diffi-
culties being experienced by foreign oil interests in
F/FG
Japan under the Petroleum Industry Law, and to report that
there have been no developments of consequence during the
past two weeks.
Conversations.
The special representatives of the Standard-Vacuum
and

=== Page 222 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
and Shell oil interests returned to Japan on April 2, 1935,
to resume their conversations with the Japanesc officials
in regard to the position of the foreign oil interests in
Japan. The first conve sation was scheduled to take place
on April 4, but due to the illness of the Vice Minister
of Commerce and Industry, was postponed until today (April
5). The special representative of the Shell interests must
leave Japan on April 13, while the special representatives
of the Standard-Vacuum interests wish to leave Japan on or
about April 16; consequently they are asking the Japanese
officials to push the conferences to a conclusion.
Attitude of the Foreign Oil Interests.
As gathered from conferences with the special representa-
tives of the forcign oil interests, they arc determined not
to invest further funds in Japan under the present circum-
stances. If therefore, the Japanese insist upon the carry-
ing out of the obligation to keep six months' stocks (which
would require the investment of large sums for tanks and oil),
the foreign oil companies will probably propose, as a tempo-
rary expedient, to cut their sales quotas to half or less,
in order to build up six months' stocks, based on the re-
duced quotas. This amount of oil could be carried with their
present storage facilities. But as the foreign oil companies
are selling about 46 per cent of the gasoline sold in Japan
today, the sudden reduction of their sales would create a
gasoline shortage, which would probably cause considerable
disturbance among the gasoline consumers and might precipate
an

=== Page 223 === (confidence 0.98)
- 3 -
an alteration in the regulations. At the same time, the
special representatives of the oil interests will inform
the Japanese officials that they will return to their head-
quarters to consult with their directors regarding the ad-
visability of closing out their Japan trade entirely. It
is hoped that this more aggressive attitude on the part of
the foreign oil interests will bring about a reconsidera-
tion of the law by the Japanese.
Respectfully yours,
Joseph C. Grew

Apr11 13th, 1935.
Senator Tom Connally
Washington, D. C.
Dear Sir:-
I notice from press reports that Japan
intends to require American oil companies doing business in
89
Japan to maintain a six months supply of oil or a specified
minimum amount, in storage at all times. I presume they are
4.6
desirous of having a stock of oil on hand as a means of
preparedness in case of war.
36
On the other hand, we should have a
supply of silk on hand in this country for the same purpose
0.
and for a similar period. Would it not be just as logical
11
to require Japanese companies doing a silk business in the
U S A, to carry in stock a large quantity of silk? While
L6
this may be logical, there may be some practical objection
with which I am not familiar.
Yours very truly,
W A WILLIAMS

(c)
Discrimination in the Application of the
"Manchukuoan" Customs Tariff against American
and British .erosene in Favor of Japanese
Light Oil.
Under the authorization contained in the Department's
telegram No. 20 of February 15, the Embassy joined with
the British Eibassy on February 19 in making informal,
oral representations to the Japanese Government on the
discriminatory duties levied by the customs of "Manchukuo"
on American and British kerosene as opposed to Japanese
"light oil".** In making these representations, Mr.
Dickover of this Embassy and  r. Sansom of the British
Embassy invited Mr. Kurusu's attention to the fact that
on December 19 the American and British Consuls General
at Mukden had made informal representations to the
"Manchukuoan" authorities on the same subject.
The
representatives of the two Embassies further statcd that
the "Manchukuoan" authorities had failed to eliminatc
the discrimination and expressed the hope that the
Japanese

Tokyo's 1378 of June 28, 1935, in

states that a news item in a Japanese

that Japanese oil companies have been experi-

storage provisions of the Petroleum Industry
Low, but that they have now succeeded in com-

three months' supplies of oil. Storage for
six months' supplies must be installed by

The Embassy believes that the

the face of opposition from taxicab companies,

ification of the storage requirements. The

I have the honor to refer to the Embassy's despatch
No. 1253, dated April 19, 1935, and to previous correspondence
in regard to the difficulties being experienced in Japan by the
foreign oil interests under the Petroleum Industry Law, and to
report the following recent developments in the situation.
F/B
On June 26, 1935, the JIJI SHIMPO published an article
giving the cost to the oil companies, Japanese and foreign, in
1935, of providing for and carrying six-months' stocks of oil
a around Yen 86,000,000. This figure is composed of the
following items: Cost of tanks and equipment, Yen 16,086,000;
Cost of oil. to be stored, Yen 57,552,000; Cost of land, Yen
3,331,000;

=== Page 253 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
3,331,000; Cost of interest, insurance, etc., Yen 8,892,000.
The figures, it is stated, were supplied by a large Japanese
oil company. The article proceeds to state that the Japanese
oil companies have been experiencing difriculty in obtaining
funds for the construction of equipment and the purchase of
the oil to be stored, but that they have succeeded now in
completing the facilities for the storage of three months:
supplies of oil. Storage for six months' supplies must be
installed by October 1, 1935.
It is presumed by the foreign oil interests in Japan that
the above was an inspired news item, issued to prepare the way
for some concession which will have to be made by the Japanese
officials in the not distant future to satiefy the demands of
the foreign oil companies and the desires of the Japanese oil
companies. It is obvious that the Japanese oil industry can-
not carry this heavy burden without some compensation. The
Japanese and foreign oil companies some time ago filed a joint
petition for permission to raise the price of gasoline. There
was immediate and strong opposition from the taxicab and other
commercial motor transport companies, accompanied with threats
of cessation of motor transport should the inorease in gasoline
price be granted. It is believed, therefore, that the authori-
ties , finding it dangerous to sanction an increase in the price
of gasoline, are preparing the way for some modification of the
storage requirements. What form this modification will take is
not yet known, but the foreign oil companies are of the opinion
that it will consist of an indefinite postponement of the second
three months' storage requirements. In other words, the authori-
ties will probably require the carrying of three months: stocks,
the

=== Page 254 === (confidence 0.98)
-3 -
the facilities for which were supposed to have been completed
by April 1, 1935, but will postpone indefinitely the time by
whieh the compenies must be propared to carry six monthst
stooks.
Korean Oil Company Organized.
On June 25, 1935, the Chosen Oil Company was formally
inaugurated. This company has its head offices at Seoul,
Chosen, and will establish a refinery at Gensan, on the
eastern coast of Korea. The capitalization is Yen 10,000,000,
of which the bulk has been subscribed by the Nippon Oil com-
pany, the Mitsui interests, the Sumitomo interests, the
Japan Mining Company, the Chosen Nitrogen Company, the Oriental
Development Company, and Chambers of Commerce in Chosen. It is
expected that Mr. Noguchi, the President of the Chosen Nitrogen
Company and one of the promoters of the new oil company, will
be president of the new company. Mr. Hashimoto, President of
the Nippon Oil Company, also was interested in the organization
of the new firm.
According to the JIJI SHIMPO of June 26, 1935, the new
oil company is to monopolize the refining and sale of oil
in Korea. According, however, to the information obtained by
the special representatives of the foreign oil companies in
their conversations with the Japanese officials in April last,
the new company is only to refine and sell as much oil as would
be sold by the Japanese oil companies subscribing to the stook
of the new company, plus a part of the natural increase. That
is, the Japanese oil companies were simply to transfer their
Korean business to the new refinery, while the foreign oil
companies were to continue to maintain their previous position in
the Korean business.
what

=== Page 255 === (confidence 0.99)
-4-
What the eventual result will be cannot be predicted at
present, as much will undoubtedly depend upon the success or
lack of success of the Japanese in restricting the operations
of the foreign oil companies in Japan Proper, and upon the
outcome of the experiment in oil monopolies in Manchuria.
A rumor has been current for some time among petroleum circles
in Japan that the monopoly in Manchuria was in the nature of a
trial, and that if it should prove successful, the monopoly
principle would be extended to the Japanese colonies and per-
haps eventually to Japan. It is believed by some of the foreign
oil representatives that the establishment of the Chosen oil
Company is the first step toward the establishment of a monopoly
in Korea.
Respectfully yours,
Joseph C. Grew.

Sir:
I have the honor to refer to this Embassy's despatch
202
No. 1378,dated June 28, 1935, and to previous corres-
pondence in regard to the difficulties being experienced
in Japan by the American and other foreign oil interests
under the Petroleum Industry Law, and to report the fol-
lowing recent developments in the situation.
dated April 19, 1935, in the agreement arrived at between
the special representatives of the foreign oil interests
and

=== Page 260 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
and the Japanese officials on April 13, 1935, the following
clause was included:
Stock-keeping obligations will be three
months including working stocks. To be
arranged as soon as possible but not later
than 0ctober 1, 1935.
It now being well on in July, and the Japanese officials
having as yet made no announcement of their plans for al-
tering the oil storage regulations, the local representatives
of the foreign oil interests have been feeling some anxiety
in regard to the matter, especially as they must shortly
file with the Department of Commerce and Industry their
import, storage and business plans for 1936. A report
has, however, reached the Embassy from unofficial sources
to the effect that the Vice Minister for Commerce and In-
dustry has been discussing the question of the altering
of thestorage regulations with the Vice Ministers of the
other governmental departments concerned.
.Mr. Yoshino,
the Vice Minister for Commerce and Industry, at first pro-
posed to the other Vice Ministers that the oil storage
requirements should be definitely and finally fixed at
three months' stocks, but this proposal did not meet the
approval of certain other Vice Ministers (presumably those
of the War and Navy Departments). Mr. Yoshino next pro-
posed, according to the report, that the foreign oil com-
panies shpuld be required to carry only three months' stocks,
while the Japanese oil companies should be required to carry
six months' stocks and that a subsidy should be given the
Japanese companies to compensate them for the additional
three months' stocks which they would have to carry. This
proposal

=== Page 261 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
This proposal also, it is understood, did not meet the
approval of some of the governmental departments. Mr.
Yoshino is now working on a plan to fix the oil storage
requirements definitely at four months' stocks for all
companies, foreign and Japanese, without subsidy. The
Embassy has not learned whether or not this plan is likely
to be acceptable to the other governmental departments
interested, but has reason to believe that it will be
acceptable to the foreign oil companies.
Respectfully yours,
Joseph C. Grew

August 17, 1935.
Tokyo's 1408 of July 25, 1935,
recounts recent developments in the oil
situation in Japan. No action is required.
Before leaving Tokyo the Ambassador
called on the Foreign Minister and Mr.
Kurusu and discussed the petroleum question.
Memoranda of conversations with these
gentlemen are appended. From these two
conversations it appears that the question
is not being allowed to die, and that the
Japanese officials are working slowly toward
a solution of the storage and price problems.
Two major difficulties which face them are
(1) the insistence of the military on the
conservation of large stocks of petroleum,
and (2) the necessity for "saving face" for
the Minister of Commerce and Industry who
sponsored the Petroleum Industry Law.
Mr. Hirota stated that the matter had
been discussed recently in the Cabinet
when it was arranged that a conference be
held between the Ministers of War, Navy,
Commerce, Finance and Foreign Affairs.
He stated that there were obviously divided
opinions on the subject but he hoped that
a favorable settlement could be reached in
due course. He did not, however, express
either optimism or pessimism as to the
result.
Mr. Kurusu

=== Page 263 === (confidence 0.98)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
- 2 -
Mr. Kurusu emphasized the divergent
viewpoint of the various interested
ministries. He stated that the whole
matter must be settled before October 1.
He did not commit himself as to the
probable result, but said that he was
doing his best. His principal card was
the fact that Japan had to have oil and
this factor ought to be sufficiently
strong to bring the conflicting interests
into line.
An informative article in regard to
the enforcement of the Petroleum Law
which appears to have been written by a
Japanese is appended to the despatch.
It may be read if time permits.

The Honorable
The Secretary of State,
Washington.
Sir:
I have the honor to refer to the Embassy's despatch
24
No. 1385,dated July 10, 1935, and to previous correspondence
in regard to the difficulties being experienced in Japan
by the American and other foreign oil interests under the
Petroleum Industry Law, and to report the following recent
developments in the situation.
F/FG.
Conversations with Japanese Officials.
In compliance with the Department's instruction No. l10,
July 17, 4 p.m., the Ambassador, before leaving Tokyo, took
advantage of his farewell call on the Minister for Foreign
Affairs

=== Page 265 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
Affairs on July 18, 1935, to bring up casually and in-
formally the question of tho present status of the American
petroleum interests in Japan and to ask the Minister's
opinion in regard to a favorable settlement of the question
along the lines of the tentative agreement reached last
April between the special representatives of the foreign
oil interests and the Japanese officials. The Minister
replied that the matter had been considered by the Cabinet
and that the Ministers of War, Navy, Finance, Commerce
and Foreign Affairs had been asked to confer in regard to
the question. A memorandum, dated July 18, 1935, of the
conversation between the Ambassador and the Minister for
1/
Foreign Affairs is enclosed herewith.
The Ambassador also called upon Mr. Saburo Kurusu, the
Chief of the Bureau of Commercial Affairs, to say "au revoir
and brought up the petroleum question. Mr. Kurusu said that
he was working on the question and recounted in detail the
various difficulties being experienced in attempting to re-
concile the viewpoints of the various Ministries concerned.
He felt that his principal card in forcing a satisfactory
settlement would be the fact that Japan had to have foreign
oil. A memorandum, dated July 18, 1935, of the conversation
2
between the Ambassador and Mr. Kurusu is enclosed.
From these two conversations, it appears that the question
is not being allowed to die, as apparently was feared by the
American oil interests. The Japanese officials are working
slowly toward a solution of the stock-keeping and price
problems, but are faced with a number of difficulties,
foremost

=== Page 266 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
foremost among which are (1) the desire of the military
to conserve large stocks of petroleum and petroleum pro-
ducts in Japan, and (2) the necessity of "saving the face"
of the Minister for Commerce and Industry, who sponsored
the Petroleum Industry Law.
Mr. Kojiro Matsukata, the importer of Soviet gasoline
into Japan, stated on July 23, in a conversation with Mr.
Dickover, that the Japanese oil refiners are in a quandary
as to their future course of action. They have reccived
no answer to their petition for an increase in the price
of gasoline (a part of which was intended to cover the cost
of storing oil), and they do not know if they will be re-
quired to store six months' stocks of oil or less. They
are therefore reluctant to start the construction of tanks
and to make forward contracts for the supply of oil. The
Japanese oil companies are, as a result, also pressing the
Government for definite decisions on the various problems
connected with the oil question.
Newspaper Comnent.
A clipping from the OSAKA MAINICHI AND TOKYO NICHI-NICHI,
English Edition, of July 18, 1935, on the subject of the
operation of the Petroleum Industry Law, written by P. D. Q.
3/
(real name unknown to the Embassy), is enclosed herewith.
This article points out that the foreign oil companies will
not store six months' stocks of oil under present conditions,
while the Department of Commerce and Industry cannot decide
to allow a heavy increase of price. The only answer would
seem to be to alter the law, which would reflect upon the
dignity

=== Page 267 === (confidence 0.98)
dignity of Japanese law. Moreover, the article points
out, the law has not yet succeeded in controlling prices,
while the Government has had to abandon, in part at least,
its plan of assisting the domestic refining industry by
allotting the natural increase in demand to the local re-
finers. The author's conclusion is that more attention
should be paid to the development of Japanese sources of
fuel, such as the liquification of coal and the exploita-
tion of local oil fields.
The Embassy has no means of ascertaining whether or
not this article was published as a method of preparing the
way for a change in the law and regulations, but in view
of other articles of a similar nature which had been published
previously, this conclusion seems reasonable.
Respectfully yours,
Sdaim I. haill
Edwin L. Neville
Chargé d'Affaires ad interim.

Enclosure No. 1 to despatch
No. 1408 of July 25, 1935, from the
Embassy at Tokyo.
Conversation
July 18, 1935.
The Minister for Foreign
Affairs, Mr. Hirota.
Trade and Commerce
Mr. Hirota spoke cf the economic situation between
the two countries and thought by cooperation, conciliation
and compromise most of the difficulties could be removed.
I told him of my talk with Mr. Kurusu yesterday and we
agreed that the general policy of our two governments in
matters of trade seemed to be very similar. He said that
the Canadians had made the mistake of taking arbitrary
action and raising their tariff without sufficient opportunity
for conciliation and that this had caused trouble.
In this connection I said that the Department of
State would no doubt ask me questions about the present
status of the American petroleum interests in Japan and
I would be glad to know quite casually and informally
and without making official representations how the
Minister felt about the probability of favorable settlement
along the lines of the tentative agreement reached between
the Standard Vacuum Company, Messrs. Kurusu and Yoshino.
Mr. Hirota said that he had only recently discussed this
matter at Cabinet where it was arranged that a conference
should be held between the Ministers of the Navy, War,
Commerce, Finance and Foreign Affairs. He said that there
were obviously divided opinions on the subject but he hoped
that

=== Page 269 === (confidence 0.98)
-2 -
that a favorable settlement could be reached in due
course. He did not however express either optimism or
pessimism as to the result.
J.C.G.

=== Page 270 === (confidence 1.00)
Enclosure No. 2 to despatch
No. 1408 of July 25, 1935, from the
Embassy at Tokyo.
Conversation
July 18, 1935.
Mr. Kurusu, Chief of the
Commercial Bureau.
Trade Relations
In calling on Mr. Kurusu today to say goodbye, we
discussed again the trade relations between the two
countries on the basis of my talk with Mr. Hirota. Mr.
Kurusu said that he expected a favorable outcome of the
negotiations concerning the apportionment of trade with
the Philippines. He obviously had a good many factors
to contend with in Japan but fortunately the textile
interests were in a less exacting frame of mind than
they were a year or two ago owing to the general im-
provement in business and he thought that a satisfactory
settlement would be reached.
As for the petroleum situation, he was working with
the representatives of the other interested ministries
each of which looked at the matter from a different point
of view and it was extremely complicated to bring them
all into line. The War and Navy Ministries cared nothing
about the matter except the conservation of adequate stocks.
The Minister of Commerce had unfortunately committed himself
in the Diet to the effect that the price of gasoline would
not be raised. The Minister of Finance was flatly opposed
to a subsidy. The problem was how to settle the matter
without raising the price of gasoline especially as the
political

=== Page 271 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
political reputation of the Minister of Commerce was at
stake. Mr. Kurusu said he had recently talked with various
taxi drivers who said that if the price of gasoline were
raised two or three sen it would be very difficult for
them because it would not justify their raising their
fares, but if the price were raised to as much as five
or ten sen then their fares could be raised and all would
be well. Mr. Kurusu said that this whole matter must be
settled before the first of October. He did not commit
himself as to the probable result but said that he was
doing his best. His principal card was the fact that Japan
had to have oil and this factor ought to be sufficiently
strong to bring the conflicting interests into line.
J.C.G.

FILED
I have the honor to refer to the Embassy's
5
despatch No. 1408, dated July 25, 1935, and to previous
correspondence on the subject of the difficulties being
F/B
experienced in Japan by the American oil interests under
the Petroleum Industry Law, and to report that it now
appears that events are rapidly moving toward some
solution of the problem, although it is yet too early
to predict the final outcome.
According

=== Page 274 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
According to the newspapers of August 2, 1935,
the Minister for Commerce and Industry, Mr. Chuji
Machida, after the Cabinet meeting on August 1, 1935,
consulted with Premier Okada, General Hayashi, the
War Minister, Admiral Osumi, the Navy Minister, and the
Minister for Foreign Affairs, Mr. Hirota. Mr. Machida
pointed out that the Petroleum Industry Law was un-
satisfactory and that it should be re-examined, with
a view to altering the law or taking other steys which
would relieve the oil companies, Japanese and foreign,
of the excessive burden of stock-keeping. The issues,
as explained by Mr. Machida, were whether or not the
Government would compensate the oil companies for the
cost of providing storage facilities and storing the
oil, whether or not the Government would modify the
oil storage regulations, and whether or not the Govern-
ment would allow an increase in the price of gasoline
to compensate in part for the cost of storing gasoline.
It was decided that a conference of officials of the
Commerce, War, Navy, Finance and Foreign Ministries
should be held to decide upon these issues, according
to the newspapers.
The JIJI SHIMPO of August 7, 1935, reported that
Mr. Yoshino, the Vice Minister for Commerce and In-
dustry, had been active in endeavoring to reconcile
the conflicting ideas of the Ministries interested in
the question. According to this newspaper, the War and
Navy

=== Page 275 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
Navy Ministries were opposed to any compromise on the
oil storage question, while the Finance Ministry was
opposed to the payment of compensation for the storage
of oil, with the result that the Department of Commerce
and Industry was placed in a most difficult position.
After Mr. Machida explained the situation to Admiral
Osumi and General Hayashi on August 1, however, the
latter two Ministers realized that the matter involved
international complications, and as a result thc at-
titude of the Army and Navy toward the question has been
modified considerably. On August 6, therefore, Mr.
Yoshino called on Mr. Machida, explained the present
status of the storage of oil by the oil companies and
further explained the views of the Army and Navy. Mr.
Yoshino assured the Minister that there were increased
prospects of arriving at a settlement of the question.
Next, according to the JIJI, Mr. Yoshino called on
Mr. Tsushima, the Vice Minister for Finance, to consult
him regarding the question of compensation for the storage
of oil, and Mr. Tsushima will proceed to the country
residence of Mr. Takahashi, the Finance Minister, in a
day or two to obtain a definite decision in regard to
this question.
From the above, it appears that developments are
working toward a solution in the form of a subsidy to
compensate for the cost of storing oil. It is probable,
however, that this subsidy will not apply to the foreign
firms

=== Page 276 === (confidence 0.97)
-4-
firms, as the Japanese Government never subsidizes
foreign firms. It therefore appears that a compromise
plan, rumors of which have reached the Embassy, is
under consideration. This plan is said to be that
the foreign oil companies will be required to carry
only three months' stocks of oil, without subsidy,
while the Japanese oil companies will be required to
carry six months' stocks, with a compensating subsidy.
Respectfully vours,
Edim L. recille
Edwin L. Neville,
Chargé d'Affaires ad interim.

=== Page 278 === (confidence 0.99)
I. RELATIONS WITH THE UNITED STATES.
(a) American Oil Interests in Japan.
Although there were no new developments during the
month in the situation created by the application of the
894.6363
Petroleum Industry Law to A erican and other foreign oil
interests in Japan, it appeared that the Japanese officials
concerned, particularly Mr. Kurusu, Chief of the Commercial
Bureau of the Foreign Cffice, were working slowly toward
a solution of the problems of stock-keeping and prices.
The foremost obstacles standing in the way of a settlement
of the matter were the desire of the Army to have large
stocks of petroleum and petroleum products in Japan and
the necessity of avoiding action detrimental to the
reputation of the Minister for Commerce and Industry, who
sponsored the Petroleum Industry Law.*
In compliance with the Department's telegraphic
instruction No. 110 of July 17, the A.:bassador took
advantage of his farewell call on the Minister for Foreign
Affairs on July 18 to bring up casually and informally the
question of the present status of the American petroleum
interests in Japan and to ask the Minister's opinion
concerning a favorable settlement of the question. Mr.
Hirota was noncommittal in his reply, stating that he
hoped that a favorable settlement could be reached in due
course but not expressing either optimism or pessimism
in this regard.

=== Page 279 === (confidence 1.00)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
September 13, 1935.
Tokyo's 1443 of August 23, 1935, re-
ports that the Japanese authorities are
working toward a solution of the difficulties
incurred under the Petroleum Industry Law
by the American oil companies.
Mr. Machida, the Minister for Commerce
and Industry finds himself in a difficult
place by reason of the law requiring the
companies to carry a six months' stock.
The law was sponsored by his predecessor,
and he himself is left "holding the bag",
unable to recind a law which has been
found partially unworkable without causing
loss of face to his Department. Moreover,
a foolish promise made in the Diet that
the price of gasoline would not be raised
through the operation of the law has
prevented him from offering compensation
for the cost of storing oil. But he
realizes that the oil companies must be
compensated in some way or the oil will
not be stored.
Mr. Kurusu stated to Mr. Dickover
that he could promise that "there would
be no law-breakers on October 1, 1935".

will be altered by that time.

The Honorable
The Secretary of State,
