Sir:
I have the honor to refer to the Embassy's despatch
No. 1421, dated August 7, 1935, and to previous correspon
FLED
dence on the subject of the difficulties being experienced
in Japan by the American oil interests under the provisios
of the Petroleum Industry Law, and to report that it appears
that the Japanese authorities are working steadily on the
problem of finding a solution of the difficulties.
The JIJI of August 16 reported that conferences between
the Ministries of Commerce and Industry, Navy, War and Finance
F/FG.
have

=== Page 282 === (confidence 0.99)
-2 -
have as yet failed to yield a satisfactory solution,
but that the oil companies anticipate that a solution
will be found by permitting a small raise in the price
of gasoline and either reducing the oil storage require-
ments for all companies, or reducing the requirements
only for the foreign firms, while compensating the Japa-
nese companies for carrying the full requirement of six
months' stocks. The YOMIuRI of August 20 reported that
Mr. Machida, the Minister for Commerce and Industry, had
approached Mr. Takahashi, the Finance Minister, with a
plan for reducing the requirement for foreign oil com-
panies to three months' stocks and for compensating
the Japanese companies, which would carry six months'
stocks. According to the newspaper, however, Mr. Taka-
hashi rejected this plan and suggested that Mr. Machida
reconsider the Petroleum Industry Law and its enforcing
regulations in the light of present conditions. Mr.
Machida is thus placed in a very awkward position, the
YOMIURI states. The newspaper also bluntly stated that,
should the Japanese authorities go so far as to fine the
foreign oil companies for their refusal to make prepara-
tions for the storage of six months' supplies of oil, the
foreign oil companies are determined to withdraw from Japan
and may institute a boycott on supplies of crude oil to
Japan.
Mr. Dickover, of the Embassy, on August 20 asked Mr.
Kurusu, Chief of the Bureau of Commercial Affairs of the
Foreign Office, how the adjustment of the difficulties
of the oil companies was progressing. Mr. Kurusu con-
firmed various statements in the newspapers to the effect
that

=== Page 283 === (confidence 0.99)
- 8 -
that Mr. Machida, Minister for Commerce and Industry,
was in an embarrassing position. He said that the
Petroleum Industry Law was sponsored and introduced
into the Diet by Mr. Machida's predecessor in office,
Mr. Yamamoto, that the law has now been in operation
for a year and has been found to be partially unworkable,
and that Mr. Machida has "been left holding the bag".
Mr. Machida himself, according to Mr. Kurusu, is a
liberal of the old school, who believes in freedom
of trade and as little government as possible in busi-
ness, and, moreover, he is the President of the Minseito,
the party which advocates such principles. Therefore
he secretly is opposed to the Petroleum Industry Law,
but he cannot make his predecessor in office and the
permanent offcials of his Ministry "lose face" by de-
nouncing the law. Moreover, he, or one of his sub-
ordinate officials, foolishly made a statement in the
Diet to the effect that the price of gasoline would
not be raised through the operation of the law. There-
fore the price of gasoline cannot be rised to com-
pensate for the cost of storing oil. But Mr. Machida
realizes that the oil companies have to be compensated
in some way for storing oil, or the oil will not be
stored. Consequently he is in a most difficult position.
Mr. Kurusu concluded by stating that, despite the
difficulties of Mr. Machida, he could promise that "there
would be no law-breakers on October 1, 1935". By this
he

=== Page 284 === (confidence 0.95)
-4-
he evidently meant that the foreign oil companies,
which have not and do not mean to store six months'
supplies of oil in Japan, will not be breaking the
law on October 1 (when, according to the law, the full
amount of oil must be stored), because the law will be
altered by that time.
Respectfully yours,
Edieion
Edwin L. Neville

MOMH:
Tokyo's 1465 of September 6, 1935,
disousses in some detail press reports
of the deliberations of the authorities
in regard to enforcement of the oil law.
Press reports, which the Embassy has
been unable to confirm, state that the date
of enforcement originally set for October 1,
will have to be postponed; that the Govern-
ment will compensate the companies for the
cost of erecting storage facilities; and
that a moderate increase in the price of
gasoline will be allowed.
This solution would prevent the
foreign companies from becoming lawbreakers
on October 1, and would enable the Govern-
ment to save face. But it would not
satisfy the military and it would leave
the foreign oil companies in an insecure
situation.
This despatch is largely repetitious
of previous reports.

For
The Honorable
The Secretary of State,
Washington.
sir:
I have the honor to refer to the Embassy's despatch
No. 1443 dated August 23, 1935, and to previous cor-
FILED
respondence on the subject of the difficulties being
experienced in Japan by the American and other foreign
oil interests under the provisions of the Petroleum
Industry Law. In previous despatohes it was reported
that the Japanese Government authorities were working
F/FG.
toward a solution of the problems confronting the
Government in connection with the operation of the
Law.
Recent

=== Page 287 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
Recent newspaper accounts of the results of the
deliberations of the authorities have been pessimistic.
According to the vernacular newspapers, the committee
appointed from among officials of the Departments of
Commerce and Industry, Finance, War and Navy held a
second conference on August 28, at which time it was
decided that (1) the provision of the regulations re-
quiring oil traders to carry six months' stocks of oil
should not be changed, but as it will be impracticable
to enforce this provision on October 1, l935, the date
originally set, the date of enforcement will have to be
postponed for some time, (2) the Government should con-
sider compensation of the oil firms, both Japanese and
foreign, for the cost of erecting storage facilities and
the storing of oil, and (3) a moderate increase in the
price of gasoline should be allowed, as partial compen-
sation for the expense of storing oil.
The newspapers point out that there are several
objections to the solutions enumerated above. In regard
to (1) , it is pointed out that the foreign oil companies
positively refuse to store six months' stocks of oil, and
that therefore the mere postponement of the date of enforce-
mont is not a final and definite solution of the difficulty.
On the other hand, the newspapers state, the Japanese oil
companies have proceeded with preparations for the fulfillment
of the storage requirements by October 1, 1935, and if the
date of enforcement is to be indefinitely postponed, or if
the requirements are to be altered to three months' stocks,
thoy

=== Page 288 === (confidence 0.98)
- 3 -
they want to be compensated for the expenses already
inourred. In regard to (2), it is stated that the
Finance Ministry is definitely opposed to any sort of
State compensation to the oil companies, and, in case
compensation is given, the question arises if the foreign
firms should be compensated at all or be compensated at
the same rate as the Japanese firms. Likewise, the au-
thorities are opposed to the granting of a higher price
for gasoline, on the ground that such a step would be
against the interests of the consumer, whose interests,
it was stated before the Diet when the law was under
consideration, would not be affected by the law. For
these reasons, according to opinion in oil circles, the
newspapers state, a solution of the difficulties satis-
factory to all parties will be almost impossible, if not
impossible,of attainment.
The Embassy has not been able to gather any direct
information in regard to the outcome of the deliberations
of the authorities concerned, but it appears probable
that the temporary solution which has been discussed is
simply that of postponing indefinitely the date of enforce-
ment of the requirement that oil companies must carry six
months' stocks in storage. This solution would, for the
time being, accomplish the following results:
1. The foreign oil companies would not be law-
breakers on October 1, 1935, and would therefore not be
subject to fines or cancelation of their licenses to
operate in Japan.
2.

=== Page 289 === (confidence 0.99)
- 4 -
2. The oil companies would not be required to
invest further large sums for tanks and oil, and there-
fore an increase in the price of gasoline would not
be immediately indicated.
3. The Japanese Government would "save face",
as it would not have to repeal a provision already pro-
mulgated.
The Army and Navy, however, would undoubtedly not
be satisfied with this solution, as they would not ac-
quire the large reserves of oil which they desire. It
is doubtful also if the foreign oil companies will be
satisfied with the solution, as it is not a fundamental
solution and leaves them in a position of uncertainty.
Newspaper Comment.
Some of the newspapers have commented editorially
upon the difficulties which the Government is erperiencing
in enforcing the Petroleum Industry Law. The ASAHI on
August 26 stated that the fact that the oil storage
scheme had not yet been carried out was a serious re-
fleotion on the Government, and must be due to defects
in the law itself. It stated that the only explanation
is that the Diet passed the law upon the insistence
of the military without considering the implications
of the law, and suggests that the best way out of the
difficulty would be for the Government to compensate
the oil companies for the extra expense which would
be inourred because of the law. The JIJI on August 31
stated that the Petroleum Industry Law was not operating
smoothly

=== Page 290 === (confidence 0.99)
- 5 -
smoothly, because the Government authorities failed
to realize the obvious fact that the oil companies
would object to the expense of providing storage
facilities and buying oil for storage; that the plan
of raising gasoline prices would be unsatisfactory
because it would squeeze the public in order to pay
for a scheme which should be financed by the Govern-
ment or the companies or both; that the best solution
would be to lower the quantity to be stored; that this
step would lose face for the Government but that the
problem is such that the question of face should not
be taken into consideration. The JAPAN TIMES AND MAIL
of September 2, l935, outlined the difficulties being
experienced in enforcing the Petroleum Industry Law
and stated that it would seem that * drastic modifi-
cation is called for before it can be enforced". It
pointed out that the law was essentially a national
defense measure, but stated that *even those most
concerned with the technicalities of defense can hardly
kill the goose which lays the golden egg of Japan's
oil needs". The editorial concluded:
"It is hoped that the Government au-
thorities will act wisely in revising the
provisions of the present Oil Law so that
the oil companies both foreign and Japanese
may be able to do business on a reasonable
basis. Such a revision should, of course,
not be detrimental to the interest of the
nation. Unless the present impasse is solved,
the National Oil Industry Policy will do littio
good for the Nation."
The Search for o1l.
According to the KOKUMIN SHIMBUN of September 2,1935,
the Japanese Navy has worked out a program to improve
Japan's

=== Page 291 === (confidence 0.98)
- 6 -
Japan's limited oil resouroes. The program,which
really contains nothing now, consists of further
exploitation of Japan's domestic oil fields, ac-
quisition of fields abroad, enforoement of the storage
provisions of the Petroleum Industry Law, and the
development of oil substitutes.
The Navy believes, according to the KOKUMIN, that
many areas in Japan have not yet been sufficiently
explored for oil, including Aomori, Akita, Niigata
and Nagano Prefectures, Hokkaido, Formosa and South
Saghalien. The Navy believes also that fields already
yielding could be made to produce greater quantities if
drilling were carried to deeper strata.
The South Seas (meaning Java, Borneo and Sumatra)
are ideally located as fields for exploitation by Japa-
nese oil interests but unfortunately most of the island
fields have been acquired by British and American in-
terests. It is therefore necessary to look elsewhere
for foreign fields for Japanese exploitation, but com-
panies which obtain oil concessions abroad can count on
the support of the Navy. More positive support will
also be given by the Navy to the Kita Karafuto Sekiyu
Kaisha, which is exploiting oil fields in the Soviet
half of Saghalien, and the Navy will endeavor to obtain
an extension of time during which new drilling can be
undertakon.
The Navy regards it as essential that oil be stored
in times of peace for use in time of emergency, and
therefore

=== Page 292 === (confidence 0.97)
-7-
therefore will insist that the six months' oil
storage provision of the Petroleum Industry Law be
enforoed .
The Navy is also to give further attention to
the study of oil substitutes, to supplement the supply
of oil from the sources enumerated above. The Navy
developed, at the naval laberatory at Tokuyama, the
system of liquification of coal which may be commer-
cialized by the South Manchuria Railway, and further
efforts will be made along those lines, according
to the KOkUMIN, as well as the study of alcohol,
animal and vegetable oils, and other petroleum sub-
stitutes.
Respectfully yours,
Ein Phille

w.wairl
Roference Tokyo's telegran No. 187 of
Soptember 28, noon, in regard to recent de-
velopments in the oil situation in Japan.
In brief, it would appear that the
Japanese Ministries of Commerce and Industry
and of Foreign Affairs have failed in their
efforts to obtain from the other concerned
ministries, particularly those of War and of
Navy, approval of the informal agreement
reached last April, which agreement, as a
culmination to protracted discussions be-
tween the special representatives of the oil
interests and Japanese officials, provided
for a reduction from six months to three
months in the stockholding requirements of
the new oil law. As the situation now
stands, the Japanese Government, although
postponing for nine months the date upon
which the new regulations become effective,
will, "for reasons of national defense and
to provide for emergencies", insist that
on and after June 30, 1936, all oil dealers
in Japan carry in stock a six-months' sup-
ply of oil. According to the Vice Minister
of Commerce and Industry, the Japanese
Government will endeavor to provide com-
pensation equal to the carrying charges
of

=== Page 294 === (confidence 0.98)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
- 2 -
of three-months' stocks of oil and "next
year" will give consideration to the ques-
tion of permitting a price advance. How-
ever, the Embassy's telegram under reference
states that it is yuestionable "whether and
to what extent" the Finance Ministry und
the Diet will approve expenditures in con-
pensation for carrying charges on three-
months' stocks of oil.
Although the decision of the Ja nese
Government, as indicated above, is not in
accord with the infommal understanding ar-
rived at last April and although, in the
absence of adequate compensution, it, in
effect, may cause American, British und
Dutch oil interests to withdraw from Japan
after having spent many years of hurd laburwrk
in developing in Japan, for the benefit of
the Japanese as well as for their own
profit, adequate services of supply of
petroleum products, it does not aprear that,
with the possible exception of Article 1 of
the American-Japanese Treaty of 19ll, the
Japanese Governient has in this instance
violated any treaty prcvision agreed to by
it and by the United States. The concluding
paragraph of Article 1 of the aforementioned
treaty reads as follows:
"They

=== Page 295 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
- 3 -
"They (the subjects or citizens of
the United States and of Japan) shall,
however, be exempt in the territori cf
the other fron compulsory military serv-
ice either on land or sea, in the recu-
lar forces, or in the national guurd, or
in the militia; frcn all contributions
imposed in lieu of personal service, and
from all forced loans or military exac-
tions or contributions."
In view of the fact that the storue require-
ment of the oil law is unquestionably a
measure of national defense and that it has
been so termed by the Vice Minister of Com-
meroe and Industry, it would seem to be in
direct conflict with the spirit and, in all
probability, the letter of that portion of
the treaty quoted above. Thus, unless the
stated intention of the Japanese Government
to provide compensation equal to the carry-
ing charges on three-months' stocks of oil
is put into effect, it would seem that the
American Government appropriately might
make known to the Japanese Governnent its
opinion that implementation of reculations
requiring American oil interests to maintain
at their own expense for purposes of Japa-
nese national defense stocks of oil in ex-
cess of those which the concerned company

may wish to carry for its normal business

oxaotion" and thus a violation of Article 1

should the British Government wish to adopt
similar tactics by referring to Article 2 of
the British-Japanese Treaty of 19ll, which
article guarantees to British subjects under
certain conditions freedom from military re-
quisitions and contributions, it would find
its position materially weakened by the
qualifying clause appearing in that article
which reads as follows: "unless imposed on
them equally with native subjects". In this
connection, it will be recalled that the
proposed stockholding requirement applies to
Japanese as well as to foreign oil interests.
Notwithstanding this phase of the situation,
it is believed that the American Government
should be prepared to make to the Japanese
Government representations along the line

"The foregoing stipulation [right
to appear in courts] has no bearing upon
the question whether a company or associ-

tries will or will not be permitted to
transact its business or industry in the
other, this permission remaining always
subject to the laws and regulations en-
acted or established in the respective
countries or in any part thereof."

October 14, 1935.
? No action reguined. eus
The Embassy at Tokyo reports that
tho temporary solution of the oil storage
problem, effected by postponing the date
of enforcement of the mandatory regulation,
is unsatisfactory to the foreign oil
companies. Although the Japanese
authorities have kept their promise that
the foreign companies would not become
law-breakers by reason of their failure
to store six months' supply of oil
after October 1, 1935, the solution is
only temporary and does not offer any
fundamental settlement of the dispute.
It is reported that the Ministry
of Commerce and Industry will offer to
compensate the oil companies for carry-
ing oil stocks to the extent of half the
carrying charges with no compensation
for the erection of storage facilities
and the costs of the oil. The foreign
companies object on the score that they
are being coerced into contributing to
a national defense measure, and because
if they consent to this plan, with in-
adequate compensation, other countries
will adopt similar measures. They also
suspect

=== Page 304 === (confidence 0.98)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
- 2-
suspeot that the postponement scheme will
operate to give preferential quota treat-
ment to those Japanese oil companies
which elect to carry six months' stocks.
The Embassy admits that the situa-
tion is discouraging but is inclined to
believe that, if the much heralded crisis
of 1935-36 fails to materialize by
June 1936, it is possible that there
will be something of a revolt against
the excessive demands of the military,
which may be extended to cover the oil
storage demands. In this connection,
the HoCHI has editorially expressed the
opinion that the Petroleum Industry Law
has lost its raison d'etre as there
appear to be no signs at all of the
crisis.
The Standard-Vacuum and Rising Sun
companies are about to protest to the
Chosen Government against the quotas
allotted them under the Chosen Petroleum
Industry Law.

The Honorable
The Secretary of State,
Weshington.
sir:
I have the honor to refer to the Embassy's despatch
209
No. 1465,/dated September 6, 1935, and to previous cor-
D
respondence on the subject of the difficulties being
experienced in Jepan by the American and British oil
interests under the provisions of the Petroleum Industry
Law, and to report the following recent developments
in connection with the matter.
As was reported in despatch No. 1465, the Japanese
officials concerned with the oil problem were considering
F/FG.
the

=== Page 306 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
the plan of postponing the date by which the oil companies
would be required to have in storage six months' supplies
of oil. This plan was carried into effect on September 18,
1935, by Imperial Ordinance No. 272. As published in the
OFFICIAL GAZETTE of September 19, 1935, cnd translated
by the Embassy, the ordinance reads as follows:
"With regard to the quantity of petroleum oils
to be kept in storage by refiners of petroleum or
importers of petroleum, this may be determined
by special order or ordinance until June 30, 1936,
irrespective of the provisions of Paragraph 1,
Article 6, of the Regulations perteining to the
operation of the Petroleum Industry Law.
Additional Rule: This Ordinance becomes effective
on October 1, 1935."
According to RENGO, the Depertment of Commerce and
Industry will shortly issue regulations implementing
the above ordinance. These regulations will provide that
oil companies which obtain permission from the Minister of
Commerce and Industry may postpone the storage of oil es
provided in the Petroleum Industry Lew and its enforcing
regulations from October 1, 1935, to June 30, 1936.
On September 17, 1935, the Department of Commerce
and Industry issued the following statement to the press
(translation by the JAPAN ADVERTISER):
"iccording to the regulations of the Oil Business
Law, oil refiners and importers must assume the obli-
gatien of keeping six months' supply of oil in storage,
commencing October 1. Alive to the prevailing status
of the oil business in Japan, however, the Ministry
has recognized the inadequacy of enforcing the regu-
lations entirely for the time being. This has prompted
the Ministry to postpone the date of enforcement to
June, 1936."
In the opinion of the foreign oil compenies, this
temporary solution of the storage problem is not satis-
factory, although by it the Japanese offioials have kept
the

=== Page 307 === (confidence 0.98)
- 3 -
the promise made to the special representatives of
tho Standard end Shell interests last April, to the
effect that some ad justment of the situation would
be made before October 1, 1935, in order that the
foreign oil comp nies, who are making no preparations
to store six months' stocks of oil, might not be law-
breakers on that date. The solution reached, how-
ever, is somewhet discouraging, primarily because it
indicates that the officials of the Department of
Commerce and Industry and of the Foreign Office have
not been able to overcome the insistence of the military
that six months' stocks of oil be carried in Japan by
the oil companies. Moreover, the solution is only
temporary and does not offer ny fundamental settle-
ment of the dispute. The Ministry of Commerce and
Industry announced, when the Ordinance was presented to
the Cabinet for approval, that during the next nine
months the Ministry expects to work out the problem
of oil storage smoothly and uninterruptedly by adopting
proper measures. It is reported, however, that the
measures which the Ministry will seek to have adopted
will consist of compensation to the oil compenies for
the carrying of stocks of oil. The amount of compensa-
tion suggested is only half the carrying charges, with
no compensation for the erection of storege facilities
and the original purchase of the oil. The Japanese
officiels apparently arrive at this scheme of compen-
sation on the ground that the oil companies are already
carrying three months' stooks, and that therefore the
Government should compensate them only for carrying
the ertra three months' stocks. This compensation will
not

=== Page 308 === (confidence 0.98)
- 4 -
not be satisfactory to the foreign oil companies,
however, which object primarily to the investment
in this country of large sums for storage facilities
and the purchase of oil, believing that if such storage
is required for national defense purposes, the original
cost and all the carrying charges should be borne by the
government. If the foreign oil companies consent to
carry large quantities of oil for the Japanese Govern-
ment, with inadequete compensation, every country in
the world which lacks supplies of oil will iumedietely
adopt storage laws similar to those adopted by the Japa
nese Government. The foreign oil compenies therefore
feel that they must continue to refuse to gree to the
storage of six months' supplies of oil, even though
some compensetion is offered, as a metter of general
principle.
The foreign oil companies also suspect that the
postponement scheme will be operated to give preferential
quota treatment to those Japanese oil companies which
elect to cerry six months' stocks. Some of the Japenese
oil companies, notably Mitsui, have made preparations
to carry six months' stocks, and it is presumed that
recognition of their ready compliance with the lew will
take the form of lerger quotas for l936 end following
years, at the expense of the companies which have not
complied with the regulations. It is believed that it
was for this reason that the word "postponement" was
not used in the Imperial Ordinance promulgated on
September 18, 1935. If the requirement of carrying
81x

=== Page 309 === (confidence 0.99)
- 5 -
six months' stocks should be postponed indiscriminately
for all companies, no preference could be given those
companies which comply with the requirements. But
by making the postponement applicable only to those
which apply for permission therefor, the Government
might have some reason to grant preferential privileges
to those which do not apply for postponement and which
carry the full six months' stocks. The foreign oil companies
have received an intimation that this scheme may be made
permanent, as a special Departmental regulation may be
issued allowing the reduction of the storage quantity
to three months' stocks provided formal permission is
obtained. Presumably those companies which elect to
carry six months' stocks will receive, in addition
to such compensation as the Diet may approve, pre-
ferential annual quotas.
While the situation, as outlined above, is dis-
couraging, the possibility exists that the postponement
of the date for the carrying of six months' stocks
may work out satisfactorily. The principal reason behind
the sudden demend of the military that six months' stocks
be carried is believed to be the necessity of having
adequate supplies of oil to tide the nation over the
expected "1935-36 Crisis". If the crisis fails to develop
by June 30, 1936, it is possible that the military will
consent to reduce their demands for the storage of oil.
Moreover, the question of compensation for the companies
carrying six months' stocks of oil will have to come before
the Diet, and there are indications that there will be
something

=== Page 310 === (confidence 0.98)
- 6 -
something of a revolt against the excessive demands
of the military, which may be extended to cover the oil
storage demands.
As the Jap nese Government, as indicated above,
has to e degree carried out the informal promises made
to the special representatives of the foreign oil com-
panies last pril, it does not appear that any formal
action is called for at the present time. The situation
is still somewhat nebulous, and until definite plans
are formulated for presentation to the Diet, it is
difficult to see in whet way the Embassy could inter-
vene for the assistance of the American oil interests.
Chosen Quotas.
The Standard-Vacuum Oil Company and the Rising Sun
Petroleum Company are ebout to protest to the Government
General of Chosen (Korea) against the quotas allotted
them under the Petroleum Industry Law (which is administered
in Chosen independently of the central government) for
1935. It appears that in the early part of the year
they were informed by the Government Generel that they
could count on an increase in their quotas of 15 per
cent above their sales in l934, end they have been
selling at that rate since. When the quotes were finally
fixed recently, however, they found that they had been
granted increases of only ten to eleven per cent above
their sales in 1934. As a result they are now unable
to supply their dealers with all the petroleum pro-
ducts demanded without exceeding their quotas for the
year. Moreover, during the conversations between the
Japanese officials and the special representatives of
the Standard and Shell interests in Tokyo in April
last, the foreign companies were assured that they
would

=== Page 311 === (confidence 0.99)
-7 -
would be granted probably better than one-third of the
natural increase of the demend in Chosen, while the
shere of the natural increase granted in 1935 is only
about 30 per cent of the totel. They are therefore
protesting to the Chosen Government General and sending
copies of their protests to the Ministry of Commerce and
Industry and the Foreign Office in Tokyo.
The Search for Oil.
The HOCHI of September 13, 1935, reported that the
total shipments of crude oil from North Saghalien to
Japan in 1935 will amount only to 174,000 tons, in-
cluding some 64,000 tons purchased from the Soviet-
operated part of the fields. It had been planned to
obtein around 220,000 tons from North Saghalien, but
the production of the Japanese-operated fields dropped
from the 147,000 tons obtained last year to 110,000 tons
this year, while the Soviet Government this year was
unwilling to sell any large amount of oil, its refinery
in Khabarovsk now being in full operation and requiring
nearly all the oil obtainable from the Soviet-operated
North S&ghalien fields. The North Seghalien Oil Com-
pany (the Jap:nese company to which the concession
in North Saghalien was given) will therefore in the
future concentrate on drilling new wells in the attempt
to increase production, rather than relying upon pur-
chasing oil from the Soviet-operated wells.
Newspaper Comment.
The HOCHI of September 17, 1935, editorially ex-
prossed the opinion that the Petroleum Industry Law has
lost its raison d'atre. It was drafted, according to
the newspaper, with the idea in mind that Japan would
have

=== Page 312 === (confidence 0.97)
- 8 -
have to moet a great crisis in 1935-36 and that an
emergenoy supply of oil would be necessery. But sub-
sequent developments have falsified the expectations of
the Government, there being no signs et all of the crisis.
Moreover, the oil companies are refusing to store six months'
supplies of oil, causing the lew to lose much of its authority.
There is enclosed a clipping from the JAPAN DVERTISER
of September 19, l935, containing an editoriel ably reviewing
the oil controversy. The editorial concludes with the opinion
that, as the military have not seen fit to find themselves
the funds for compensating the oil companies for the storage
of oil, they cannot believe the storage so very necessary,
thus indicating that the "crisis" no longer greatly disturbs
them.
Respectfully yours,
Edam  hairlle

Tokyo's 1506 of October 4, 1935, re-
ports further developments in the oil situa-
tion in Japan. The following is a brief
digest of a very detailed report.
On September 23, the Department of
Commoree and Industry notified the foreign
oil companies that there had been no change
in the law in regard to storage obligations;
that the recent ordinance of September 18,
1935, provided merely for a postponement of
the date of enforcement; that those companies
whieh have already completed arrangements
for storage will be encouraged to carry stocks
and will be assigned larger sales quotas; and
that the question of price increases was
still under discussion.
The Standard-Vacuum and Rising Sun
companies consider the decision of the
Japanese Government to be entirely unsatis-
factory, and have informed the Vice-Minister
of Commeree and Industry that their directors
will be surprised and disappointed to learn
that the position of the foreign oil com-
panies has received so little consideration.
In the opinion of the foreign oil companies
there has been a departure from the five-
point agreement arrived at in April of this
year

=== Page 319 === (confidence 0.96)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
- 2 -
year. The companies consider that the
outlook in regard to storage obligations,
import and sales quotas, and prices is less
favorable now than at the time of the April
agreement.
The oil companies have referred the
matter to their home offices.

Sir:
I have the honor to refer to the Embassy's despatch
211
No.1482, dated September 20, 1935, in regard to tho dif-
ficulties being experienced by the imerican and other
foreign oil interests in Japan because of the provisions
FLED
of the Petroleum Industry Law and its accompanying re-
gulations, and to report the following developments in
the situation.
On September 23, 1935, Mr. Kojima, the Chief of
F/FG
the

=== Page 321 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
the Mining Bureau of the Department of Commerce and
Industry, summoned the local representatives of the
Standard-Vacuum Oil Company and the Rising Sun Petro-
leum Company to the Ministry, and there informed them
that there had been no change in the law in respect
either to the obligation to hold six months' stocks
in storage or in the obligation to carry increasing
stocks in storage as the demand for oils grows; that
the new Ordinance (that of September 18, 1935; Embassy's
211
despatch No. 1482) provided merely for a postponement
of the obligation to store six months' stocks; that the
Government in the meentime will consider meusures to
enable all the companies to carry six months' stocks
without difficulty, but that the plans had not yet
sufficiently matured to be discussed officially; that
those companies which have already completed arrange-
ments to carry six months' stocks will be encouraged to
carry such stocks, notwithst: nding the postponement
provided for in the new ordinance; that the injustices
resulting from the fact that some companies will thereby
be carrying six months' stocks and others only three
months' stocks will probebly be correcteu by ssigning
larger quotas to those companies carrying six months'
stocks; and that the question of an increase in the
price of gasoline was still under consideration.
After the meeting referred to above, Mr. Kurusu,
the Chief of the Bureau of Commercial Affairs of the
Foreign Office, telephoned to Mr. Mocrao of the British
Embassy and Mr. Dickover of this Embassy, stating that
Mr.

=== Page 322 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
Mr. Kojima had not been in a position to discuss the
oil question thoroughly with the representatives of the
foroign oil companies; that it was therefore feared that
the oil companies' representatives might convey n erro-
neous impression to their head offices; end that, before
they had telegraphed their head offices, if they so de-
sired, Mr. Kurusu would arrange with the Vice Minister
for Commerce and Industry to discuss the whole question
confidentially with the ropresentatives. This suggestion
was immediately conveyed to the representatives of the
foreign oil companies, who, however, referred the matter
to their head offices, at the same time quoting briefly
the gist of Mr. Kojima's statements. The head offices
approved of the conferences with Mr. Kurusu and the Vice
Minister for Commerce nd Industry and instructed the
local representatives to st nd firmly on the basis of the
five-point agreement reached last April between the Japa-
nese officials and the special representatives of the
foroign oil interests.
On September 27, 1935, the representatives of the
two foreign oil companies met Mr. Kurusu and Mr. Yoshino,
the Vice Minister for Commerce and Industry at the Foreign
Office. Mr. Yoshino informed the two representatives that:
1. In accordance with its undertaking with the
special representatives of the oil companies last April,
the Japanese Government has considered the question of
reducing the storage obligation to three months' stocks,
but for reasons of national defense and to provide for
emergencies, the Government concluded that it would be
necessary

=== Page 323 === (confidence 0.99)
- 4 -
necossary to store six months' stocks. This decision
is subject to modirication only in case a plentiful
supply of crude oil is discovered in Japan or success
is obtained in the liquefaction of coal.
2. The cost of carrying three months' stocks
must be borne by the oil companies, they having carried
nearly this amount in the pest as working stocks. The
Dopartment of Commerce and Industry will endeavor to obtain
the approval, by l te October or early November, of the
Finence Ministry to the inclusion in the budget, for
adoption by the Diet, of en emount for compensation re-
presenting six per cent on the capital employed in erecting
the storage tanks and the purchase of the oil, plus other
carrying charges, for the additional three months' stocks.
It is possible that either the Finance Ministry or the
Diet will approve only part of this compensation, say
two months' storage charges only, in which case the cost
of carrying one month's stocks will have to be covered
by an increase in the price of gasoline.
3. The matter of an increase in the price of gasoline
has become & social question and consequently the Govern-
ment must continue for the present to fix prices. With a
view to minimizing any unfavorable reaction on the part
of the public and in order not to prejudice the Finance
Ministry against compensation for the storing of oil,
the Department of Commerce and Industry desires that no
advance in the price of gasoline be made until the
question of compensation is settled by the Finance
Ministry

=== Page 324 === (confidence 0.99)
- 5 -
Ministry, when the Department of Commerce end Industry
will approve an increase in the price of gasoline
covering en advance on account of economic factors s
well as an advance for any uncovered portion of the cost
of carrying the second three months' stocks.
4. The compensation mentioned cannot start until
the beginning of the next fiscal ye r on April 1, 1936,
and therefore other means must be found for compensating
those companies which will have completed their six
months' storege obligation by October 1, 1935. The
Government consecuently intends to permit the absorption
of the slack refining capacity in a shorter period then
that formerly contempl: ted, which will result in tempo-
rarily largerquotas for such companies. The importing
(foreign) companies therefore will receive something
less than the one-third of the netural increase pre-
viously greed upon, and at worst will receive none of
the increase.
5. The Government recognizes that the foreign oil
c ompanies were not in a position to qualify for equal.
consideration with the Japanese companies on October 1,
1935, becaus• of the understanding arrived at last April
that they should not be forced to incur any expenditures
for stock-keeping until their position had been made legal.
Therefore the new ordinance was so designed as to permit
them to qualify for the larger quotas by bringing their
storage up to six months' stocks as soon as possible,
and if the foreign companies signify their intention of
so doing, the Government will allow a reasonable time,
8ay

=== Page 325 === (confidence 0.98)
- 6 -
say three or four months, for building the tanks,
without forfeiting their claims to the quota benefits.
Mr. Kurusu asked the representatives of the foreign
oil companies to keep the above information confidential.
He stated that the Japanese oil companies had not been
given the information, and that any publicity at the
present time might creste difficulties in accomplishing
the plan for compensation for stock-keeping.
The representatives of the foreign oil companies
informed the Vice Minister for Commerce and Industry
that their directors would be greatly surprised and
disappointed that the position of the foreign oil com-
panies had received so little consideration, because
the outlook as regerds the storage obligations, import
and seles quotas, and prices was much less favorable
than was indicated in the five-point greement(Embassy's
despatch No. 1253, pril 19, 1935), which the special
representatives had regarded as the minimum which the
Japeneso Government would accomplish for their relief.
Mr. Yoshino explained that the departure from the five-
point agreement was due to the subsequent decision of the
Government to adopt compensation measures to cover the
cost of stock-keeping.
Mr. Kurusu and Mr. Yoshino added that, in their own
opinion, the plan of holding increasing stocks as the
demand inoreases is impracticable end must eventually
be altered in some way. Mr. Yoshino also stated that the
Kwantung Government had approved the use of the Dairen
tanks of the two foreign oil companies, temporarily, at
1oast

=== Page 326 === (confidence 0.98)
-7 -
least, for the purpose of storing oil as a part of
the stock-keeping obligation for Japan Proper. The oil
companies presume that this means that they can use
their D: iren tanks indefinitely, unless some unusual
emergency should impel the Kwantung Gover ment to take
over the tanks for its own use.
The Standard-Vacuum Oil Company and the Rising Sun
Petroleum Company consider the decision of the Jap nese
Government, as given above, as entirely unsatisfactory.
The present plan does not relieve the companies from
the burden of investing further large sums in Japan
for the erection of storage facilities and the purchase
of storage oils, end in the present uncertain status
of the Japanese merket the oil companies have consistently
refused to consider the investment of more capital in
the country. Moreover they do not consider that any
part of the carrying charges of stored oil should be
borne by increased prices, as this method of compen-
sation is too uncertain to be of value. Also, the com-
panies were given to understand, last April, that they
would be granted a portion of the natural increase in
demend for petroleum products (about one-third) and et
the same time were assured that they would not be re-
quired to incur any expenditures for stock-keeping
until the regulations had been altered and their position
(that of holding only three months' stocks) legalized.
The componies consider it most unfair for the Japanese
Government now to take advantage of the fact that they
are carrying, under the above agreement, less than
81x

=== Page 327 === (confidence 0.96)
- 8 -
six months' stocks, to give them less than one-third
of the natural increase previously agreed upon.
The local representatives of the foreign oil com-
panies have referred the matter to their head offices,
end it is expected thet representatives of the compenies
will confer with the Department of State in Weshington
and the Foreign Office in London. The Embassy will keep
the Department informed of any further developments
in the situation.

Pressed by the necessity of discovering and developing sources of oil
to support the national policy, the Department of Commerce and Industry has
decided upon the formation of an overseas oil field development corporation,
and are pressing the  ippon Oil, Okura,  itsui, itsubishi and other private
companies to jointly furnish the capital for such an organization with the
government agreeing to furnish an annual subsidy equal to one half the ex-
penses of operation; and it is expected that the  reasury opartment will
agree to the overnment em pendi tire imolvec by inclu in the necessary
amount in the supplementary bulget for next year. (Fiscal year 1936 - 1937).
The plan of the bepartient of Jonmerce un. Industry onvisaces oil sur-
veys in Central and South nerice and in th region of th: South eas by the
new corporation and the acquisition by it of romising oil  earin: areas.

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
November 27, 1935.
Tokyo's despatch No. 1534 of November 1,
1935, antedates by two weeks the Embassy's
latest report on the oil situation in Japan
as embodied in its telegram No. 2ll of
November 15, 5 p.m.
The Embassy's despatch under reference
amplifies reports previously received by
the Department in regard to this subject
but adds little of importance except state-
ments to the effect that (a) the Japanese
Government has sanctioned an increase of
2-1/2 sen per gallon in the price of gaso-
line and (b) the Shell, Standard and Texas
Companies are contemplating the acquisition
of a Chinese oil company.
The statement (see concluding paragraph
of despatch under consideration) in regard
to item (b) is far from clear. Apparently
it envisages relieving Japanese oil companies
of surplus stocks which stocks would in turn
be used to assist in supplying that portion
of the Japanese market served by the foreign
oil interests. Just what bearing this de-
velopment has on the principal item at issue,
1.e., the Japanese Oil Control Law, is not
made clear.

I have the honor to refer to the Embassy's despatch
No. 1506, of October 4, 1935, in regard to the operation
FTED
of the Japanese Petroleum Industry Law, and the dissatis
faction of the foreign oil companies with the arrange-
ments made by the Government authorities in this con-
nection, end to report further developments.
On Ootober 26, the Embassy is informed, the represen-
tatives of the domestic and foreign oil companies were
called
F/FG

=== Page 351 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
called to the Department of Commerce and Industry and
informed that the Government had sanctioned an advance
of 2-1/2 sen per gallon in the price of gasoline, to be
effective on November 1. The coapanies were told by the
Vice Minister that, in reaching its conclusion, the Govern-
ment had in mind the encouragement of domestic refining;
that this price increase was considered under present
conditions sufficient to cover increased refining costs,
and that, unless the Government plan for compensation
fails to cover the costs of the second three months
storage requirements, or unless other unforeseen events
intervene, it should be adequate. Apparently this is
the increase promised conditionally on September 27, last,
and reported in the despatch under reference (items 2 and
3 on page 4). No further information seems to have been
given the oil companies as to the plans of the Ministry
of Finance for compensation for storage costs.
The foreign oil companies telegraphed this inform-
ation to their head offices, and also asked their opinion
regerding the Government oil plan as set forth in the
interviews of September 27 and October 26. The oil com-
panies, or at least the American oil company, are now
in receipt of a reply from their head offices to the
effect that the directors have given careful and serious
consideration to the Japanese Government's latest
change in control regulations, but that they are relying
on officials to carry out the program embodied in the
five point memorandum(Embassy's despatoh No. l253 of
Apri1 19, 1935).

=== Page 352 === (confidence 0.99)
- 8 -
The Acting Manager of the Standard Vacuum Oil
Company came to the Embassy on October 31 and conferred
at some length on the oil situation. He said that he
proposed to inform the Department of Commerce and Industry
in  riting of the Directors' reply and to await develop-
ments; that he had no other course open to him. He said
that the British company would do the same.
He then told me that the Japunese companies had sur-
plus stocks of by-products from their refining operations,
some of which were being exported; that the Standard,
Texas and Shell interests were contemplating the acguisition
of the Kwanghwa Company, a Chinese concern that seems to
have some dealings, actual or prospective, with Japanese
oil companies who wish to dispose of such surplus stocks
by exporting them to china. He said that it might be
possible for the Shell and Standard Companies to pur-
chase for their Japanese trade the surplus kerosene and
other refining by-products of the Japenese companies;
that he thought a profitable arrangement might be made
along these lines. Such arrangement would relieve the
Japanese companies of surplus stocks which they cannot
now dispose of to advantage in the face of outside com-
petition. It would also relieve the foreign companies
in Japan of the necessity of importing these products,
and might so operate as to enable them to ask for higher
gasoline quotas because of reduced kerosene and other
petroleum imports. He said that he was instructed not
to

Subjoct: Oil situation in Japan.
894.6363/22
The above-mentioned representatives of the Standard-
Vaouum Oil Company called at the Department and from
3 p.m. to 6:15 p.m. there were discussed various problems
which now confront the foreign oil companies in Japan as
a result of the recent announcement that on and after
July 1, 1936, all oil interests in Japan will be required
to carry in storage stocks equivalent to a six-months'
supply.
Mr. Parker stated that after consultation with Mr.
Agnew of the Shell oil interests, who, following a visit
to New York, is due to arrive in London this evening, the
F0
Standard-Vaeuum informed its senior representative in
Japan to the effeet that it was relying upon the Japanese
authorities to carry out the five-point program agreed
upon
F/G

=== Page 355 === (confidence 0.98)
一 8 -
upon last April; that an official of the Japanese Govern-
ment had indicated that such a statement was not an ade-
quate reply to the Government's announcement of the re-
vised regulations; that he (Mr. Parker) and his associ-
ates did not know whether to "sit tight" and thus foroe
the Japanese Government to make the next move or whether,
oither through Mr. Goold, who will shortly return to
Japan, or a company director, who would proceed to Japan
from New York, or through the Department and the Embassy
at Tokyo, they should inform the Japanese Government to
the offeet that, under the conditions now envisaged by
the Japanese authorities, the Standard-Vaouum would not
be prepared to continue operations in Japan. Mr. Parker
stated that a conference had been arranged to take place
today between representatives of the Japanese Foreign Of-
fice and of the Department of Commeroe and Industry, on
the one hand, and of the foreign oil interests, on the
other hand, and that he had hoped that the Embassy at
Tokyo had informed the Department by telegraph in regard
to the results of that conferenoe. Mr. Parker was in-
formed that the Department had not received frem Tokyo a
report of the conference. Mr. Parker added that even a
definite offer from the Japanese Government to provide
compensation equal to the carrying charges on three-
months'

=== Page 356 === (confidence 0.99)
一3-
months' stooks would not settle the question as the
Standard-Vaouum could not, in view of its world-wide
interests, afford to set such a precedent; and that in
any event the company was not prepared to make additional
outlays of capital in Japan. Mr. Parker also stated that
the Shell interests were similarly minded.
Mr. Parker then referred to the recent visit to New
York of Mr. Agnew, who, on the day of his departure for
London, and following a conference with the British Ambas-
sador in Washington, suddenly switched from his original
position of agreeing with views of the Standard-Vaouum
that what had happened in Japan would very likely happen
in North China, to a position in whioh he believed that
"China would remain for the Chinese". Mr. Parker expressed
the opinion that Mr. Agnew's change of view must have been
caused by the receipt from London of news of important de-
velopments in China and that, as Mr. Agnew would, upon ar-
rival in London, consult immediately with the British
Foreign Ofrice, the British Embassy in Washington would
in all probability be instructed to approach the Depart-
ment in regard to the general situation under discussion.
Mr. Walden referred to the oral statement of the
Japanese authorities that the new regulations for the
storage of oil will be made effective "for reasons of
national

=== Page 357 === (confidence 0.98)
一 4 -
national defense" and expressed the view that, under the
conditions named, the new regulations are in contraven-
tion of that part of the American-Japanese Treaty of 19ll
which accords to the citizens of both countries freedom
from "military exactions or contributions". Mr. Mackay
replied that the treaty provisions cited by Mr. Walden
would seem to afford a basis for representations to the
Japanese Government, but added that the conoluding para-
graph of Artiole 7 of the treaty under reference (this
paragraph was read to Mr. Walden) may complicate the situ-
ation and that in any event a further approach to the
Japanese Government on purely legalistic grounds would
seem to hold forth little hope of attaining the desired
onds. Mr. Walden and Mr. Goold agreed that a charge of
treaty violation would in all probability prove to be
ineffeotive.
Mr. Parker stated that the Standard Oil Company of
Now Jersey holds in the United States and, in conjunotion
with the Shell interests, throughout the rest of the
world, except in Germany, the patent rights for the manu-
facture of oils by the hydrogenation process; that Japa-
nese interests have long desired to acquire these patent
rights for use in Japan; that, although it is probable
that the Japanese will, within the next few years,
aoquire

=== Page 358 === (confidence 0.98)
- 5 -
aoquire sufricient knowledge in regard to the hydrogena-
tion process as to render unnecessary the aoquiring by
them of patent rights, the Standard oil interests are re-
luotant to offer the process to the Japanese Government
as a quid pro quo for the elimination of the objectional
features of the Oil Control Law. Mr. Parker indicated,
however, that an offer of the process to the Japanese
Government might cause the Japanese military authorities
to agree to a modification of the existing regulations.
The conversation then turned to the question whether
it would be advisable to cease negotiations with the Japa-
nese civil authorities and to go direot to the military
authorities with a full erplanation of the situation as
viewed by the foreign oil interests and with a statement
that, if satisfactory remedial action is not taken by the
Japanese Government, the Standard-Vacuum reluotantly will
be forced to cease operations in Japan. (No deeision on
this point was reaohed.) Mr. Dooman at this point of-
fered the suggestion that the Standard-Vacuum might care
to consider the advisability of associating itself with
such Japanese interests as may be prepared to shoulder
the costs incident to the maintenance of six-months' sup-
plies in return for an appropriate participation in the
Standard-Vacuum's business in Japan. Mr. Parker replied
to

=== Page 359 === (confidence 0.98)
-6 -
to the effeot that, prior to the promulgation of the Oil.
Control Law, consideration was given to the question of
Japanese participation in the company's business in Japan
but that under existing conditions such partieipation
would seem inadvisable.
Mr. Parker stated that, with a view to avoiding fur-
ther capital outlays and at the same time continuing to
do business in Japan, it might be possible for the
Standard-Vacuum to reduce their operations in Japan to a
point where the company's existing storage facilities
would be considered by the Japanese authorities as suf-
ficient to accommodate a six-months' supply of oil. He
added that suoh a procedure would entail a 30 per cent
out in the Standard-Vacuum's trade in gasoline and a 49
per cent cut in its trade in fuel oil and that, insofar
as the calendar year l936 is conoerned, it would mean an
almost complete stoppage of business on July 1, l936, as,
for all practical purposes, the company's quotas would at
that time have been exhausted.
Mr. Parker referred to the old question of embargo-
ing oil shipments to Japan as a step in retaliation
against the Oil Control Law and expressed the view that
it would be impossible to cause American exporters of oil
to adhere to such a policy unless pressure on the part of
the

=== Page 360 === (confidence 0.98)
一7 -
the American Government was brought to bear. (This view
was concurred in by all present.) There then ensued a
general disoussion of this phase of the situation in the
course of which reference was made to the conversation
which took place on August 23, 1934, between Secretary
Iokes, Sir Henri Deterding, Mr. Teagle and officers of
the Department of State. Mr. Hornbeck suggested that,
inasmuch as subsequent developments might alter decisions
previously reached, Mr. Parker and his associates might
care, during their stay in Washington, to call upon
Secretary Ickes. Mr. Parker replied that it seemed best
that he and his associates return immediately to New York.
He added that final decision in regard to his company's
future policy in Japan would have to await the receipt of
further reports, expected momentarily, from Japan and
also an opportunity to see what, if any, approach would
be made to the Department by the British Government.
Mr. Goold, after expressing the view that the oil
Control Law is "disoriminatory", inquired whether some
means might not be employed to bring pressure by measures
of retaliation. He mentioned in particular the possibili-
ty of a more drastio control by the United States of Japa-
nese shipping and the possibility of applying to Japan's
silk trade with the United States stockholding requirements
and

=== Page 361 === (confidence 0.97)
-8-
and other restrictions similar in effeot to those which
the Japanese are imposing on the Amerioan oil trade in
Japan. Mr. Hornbeck replied to the effect that, inasmuch
as the provisions of the Oil Control Law would appear to
apply equally to all oil dealers in Japan, it might be
difficult to show it discriminatory; that the placing of
any new and special restrictions on Japanese shipping
might result only in the placing by the Japanese Govern-
ment of similar restrictions on American shipping; and
that, in view of the fact that American exports to Japan,
particularly cotton exports, are in a vulnerable position,
retaliatory measures devised against Japan might easily
have boomerang effeots.
Mr. Parker and Mr. Walden and Mr. Goold, on taking
their departure, expressed thanks for the whole-hearted
and helpful cooperation which the Department and the
Embassy at Tokyo had accorded the Standard-Vacuum Oil
Company. They also expressed regret that their call had
extended far beyond office hours. Mr. Hornbeck replied
to the effect that office hours did not exist when the
furtherance of American interests was concerned and that
he wished Mr. Parker and his associates to know that when-
over possible the Department was prepared to afford such
assistance as might be not only appropriate but also
practicable.

Subject: Oil situation in Japan.
222
The British Ambassador called at the Department and
894.6363/222
was received by Mr. Hamilton who, on being informed that
8946363
the Ambassador wished to disouss the oil situation in
Japan, offered the suggestion, which was promptly agreed
to by the Ambassador, that Mr. Maokay be permitted to
take part in the ensuing disoussion.
The British Ambassador stated that, according to a
telegram received by him from the British Foreign Office,
the oil companies are at present engaged in negotiations
with the Japanese authorities; that the British Foreign
Office feels that the position of the oil companies in
these negotiations might be strengthened if the British
and the American Embassies at Tokyo would, in continu-
ance of the past practice of cooperation, make further
representations to the Japanese Government; and that the
objective which the British Foreign Ofrice has in mind is
to cause the Japanese authorities to revert to the program
embodied
F/G

=== Page 365 === (confidence 0.97)
s
一 2 -
embodied in the five-point memorandum of last April. The
British Ambassador added that, in the opinion of the
British Foreign Office, the precedent, which would be
established if the Japanese Government should be success-
ful in forcing the foreign oil companies to comply with
existing stockholding requirements, might prove most
unfortunate to the foreign oil interests in their aotivi-
ties in other parts of the world.
Mr. Hamilton stated that the Department would be
prepared to consider sympathetically the question of mak-
ing further representations to the Japanese Government if
1t should appear that any useful purpose would be thus
sorved; that representatives of the Standard-Vaouum Oil
Company had called recently at the Department to discuss
the entire question; and that the Department was awaiting
a statement of the company's decision and an indication
whether it desired the making on its behalf of further
representations to the Japanese Government.
Mr. Hamilton permitted the Ambassador to read the
most recent report of the Embassy at Tokyo in regard to
the subjeot under discussion (Embassy's telegram No. 2ll,
Novomber 15, 5 p.m.).
Mr. Maokay stated that, in the light of information
received by the Department, there would seem to exist
some

=== Page 366 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
some doubt whether further representations to the Japa-
nese Government would prove effeotive.
Mr. Hamilton stated that the Department would at
once communicate by telegraph with the American Embassy
at Tokyo with a view to obtaining a statement of the
Embassy's opinion in regard to the advisability of mak-
-27-35
ing further representations to the Japanese Government.
Mr. Hamilton also stated that he felt sure that the
American Chargé at Tokyo would wish, in forming his con-
olusions, to consult with the British Embassy at Tokyo
and with the senior representative in Japan of the
Standard-Vacuum Oil Company.
The British Ambassador, upon taking leave, stated
that he would expect to inform the Department of any new
developments in the situation under discussion and that,
if the Department wished to communicate with him, he
would of course be available.
NOTE: In the course of the above conversation the
British Ambassador stated that, with regard to the situa-
tion under discussion, the British Foreign Office "appreci-
ates that there are no legal or technical bases upon which
representations to the Japanese Government might be made".

After consultation and taking into consideration
the growing interest in Japan in the increased price of
gasoline caused by the stock keeping obligation and the
intarest exhibited in Japan in the New York conference
of last October on the control of supplies of oil the
American and British Embassies and the representatives
of the American end British oil interests unanimously
agreed that the present is an appropriate moment for
further representations. It was also the unanimous
opinion that a mere inquiry or request for reversion to the
program of the five point memorandum would avail little
FUED
or nothing however, and that the representations should
be strengthened by some indication of the steps which
will inevitably be taken by the oil companies if the
more onerous provisions of the law and regulations are
not modified. It was auggested that this reenforcement
of the representations might take the form of definite
statements

are seriously considering withdrawal from Japan or that

and substance of such statements naturally will have to
be determined by the head offices of the oil companies
in New York and London.

UNITED STATES

WASHINGTON
November 26, 1935.
Memorandum to Secretary Ickes:
In answer to your request of November 25 concern-
ing exports ofoil to Japan, I am pleased to send you here-
with two tables, one showing the quantity and declared value
of exports of crude oil, gasoline (in bulk), gas oil, and
fuel o1l by months for 1935 through October; the other show-
ing the domestic price range for those particular oils for
the period September 1934, througl October 1935. Comparable
information on exports and declared value for 1934 is not
available in Washington. Exports of the other classes of
oils to Japan are virtually negligible.
In general, California export prices, which are
based on quotations at Los Angeles, are lower than domestic
prices because domestic prices are based on quotatios at
San Francisco, which are higher than those at Los Angeles
by the difference in transportation costs. Furthermore,
the export values cover contract shipments in bulk whereas
the domestic prices generally cover tank-car lots, and may
not be representative.
You may be interested in knowing that Japan is
believed to have sufficient oil in storage to last at
least twelve months. It has been estimated that current
military stocks are in excess of 3,500,000 barrels.
JOHN W. FINCH,
Director.
Inclosure 409457

Mr. Broadmead called at the Department and was re-
revs
ferred by Mr. Hamilton to Mr. Mackay. Mr. Broadmead
stated that the British Embassy in Washington had re-
894.6363/225
ceived from the British Foreign Ofrice in London a re-
quest to oall at the Department to ascertain whether, in
view of the recent announcement of the Japanese Govern-
ment of its intention to insist upon the carrying in
storage by all oil dealers in Japan of a stook equiva-
lent to a six-months' supply, the American Government
would be prepared to instruct its Embassy at Tokyo to
make, in concert with the British Embassy at Tokyo,
"suoh representations to the Japanese Government as may
seem possible and desirable". Mr. Broadmead stated that
STLED
his instruction from London did not include any informa-
tion beyond a brief statement of the Japanese Government's
decision to insist on the storage of a six-months' supply
:of oil and that it offered no suggestion as to the basis
upon

=== Page 391 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
upon which representations to the Japanese Government
might be made.
Mr. Mackay replied that although he oould not speak
for the Department he believed that the Department would
wish, as in the past, to cooperate whole-heartedly with the
British Foreign Office in an endeavor, by the use of all
appropriate and practicable means, to arrive at a satisfac-
tory solution of the oil problem in Japan; and that, if the
British Government had any suggestions to offer with regard
to the basis, either legal or treaty or otherwise, upon which
further representations to the Japanese Government might
appropriately be made, such suggestions would receive
sympathetio consideration by the Department.
Mr. Broadmead stated that he was not very familiar
with the oil situation in Japan and Mr. Mackay, upon re-
quest, sketched briefly important developments which
oulminated in the recent announcement of the Japanese
Government, as indicated above, and the request of a
representative of the Japanese Department of Commerce
and Industry that the decision of the oil companies in
regard to storage requirements be conveyed to the Japa-
nese authorities as soon as possible.
Mr. Mackay also stated that representatives of the
Standard-Vacuum Oil Company had called recently at the
Department; that the possibility of further action on
the

=== Page 392 === (confidence 0.97)
- 3 -
the part of the Amerioan and British oil interests and
their respeotive Governments had been disoussed; that no
decisions of importance were reached; that the repre-
sontatives of the Standard-Vacuum Oil Company had stated
that they were continuing to cooperate and consult with
the representatives of the Shell interests; and that,
following the receipt of further reports from Tokyo,
they would endeavor to arrive at a final decision which
would be communicated to the Department. Mr. Maokay
added that it was the opinion of all present at the con-
ference under reference that, unless there seemed to
exist a real possibility of obtaining at least a partial
modification of the objectionable features of the Oil
Control Law, the question would arise whether any useful
purpose would be served by the making of further repre-
sentations to the Japanese Government.
Mr. Broadmead thanked Mr. Mackay for his explana-
tion of the situation and stated that if the British
Government had any suggestions to offer he would expeot
to inform the Department in regard thereto.

The Honorable
The Secretary of State,
DEC 88 1935
Washington.
FILED
874 2631
Sir:
I have the honor to refer to the Embassy's de-
spatch No. 1534, dated November 1, 1935, and to pre-
vious correspondence on the subject of the operation
of the Japanese Petroleum Industry Law and its effect
on American oil interests in Japan, and to report the
following developments in the situation.
/220
As was reported in despatch No. 1534, the local
representatives of the Standard and Shell oil interests
were informed, in the latter part of October, by their
head
1L

=== Page 397 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
head offioes that the directors of the Standard-Vacuum
Oil Company and the Rising Sun Petroleum Company had
given most careful and serious consideration to the
(Japanese) Government's latest change in the control
law regulations, and that, in line with the prolonged
negotiations last winter, the directors are relying
on the Japanese officials to carry out the program
then decided on, as embodied in the Five-Point Memo-
199
randum (Embassy's despatch No. 1253, dated April 19,
1935). After consultetion with their Embassies, the
local representatives of the oil interests conveyed
this reply of their directors to the Vice Minister
for Commerce and Industry under date of November 2,
1935.
On November 6, 1935, the local representatives
of the two oil companies were celled to the Ministry
of Commerce and Industry, where they met Mr. Yoshino,
the Vice Minister, Mr. Kojima, the Chief of the Mining
Bureau, r. Sakai, the Chief of the Fuel Section, of
the Department of Commerce and Industry, and Mr. Kurusu,
Chief of the Bureau of Commercial Affairs, and Mr.
Hogetsu, one of his assistants, of the Foreign Office.
The conversation had to do principally with the reply
of the directors of the companies, transmitted to the
Japanese officials on November 2. In brief, the local
representatives were informed that the Five-Point Memo-
randum did not constitute a promise to alter the law
and regulations, but merely represented what the ofri-
cials hoped to be able to accomplish in order to meet
the

=== Page 398 === (confidence 0.99)
-3 -
the wishes of the foreign oil companies; that the six
months' storage provisions of the law and regulations
would have to stand and vere beyond further negotiation;
that instead of altering the law to provide for three
months' storage they had introduced a comnensetion scheme
and had postponed the enforcement of the six months!
storage obligation in order to give the foreign com-
panies time to comply; and that it now devolves upon
the foreign oil companies to indicate whether or not
they desire to continue trading in Japen under the pro-
visions of the law. Unofficially the Japenese officials
pointed out that unusual consideretion was being given
to the foreign oil companies in that they ere being al-
lowed to participete in the compensation scheme, although
Japanese tredition and custom was entirely against paying
any sort of subsidy or compensation to alien firms. Mr.
Yoshino also advised the foreign companies to take ad-
vantage of the opportunity being presented of maintain-
ing their established positions in this market and not
to take any action which would jeopardise those posi-
tions. A copy of the minutes of the conversations is
enclosed herewith.
An examination of these minutes reveals the fact
that, although the Japenese officials would seem to pre-
fer that the foreign oil companies continue their business
in Japan (vide Mr. Yoshino's advice to the local repre-
sentatives on page 2 of the minutes), they did not appear
to be greatly perturbed at the prospect that the foreign
companies might decide not to continue in business here
under the onerous conditions imposed by the law and re-
gulations. This is implied in their direct request that
they

=== Page 399 === (confidence 0.99)
-4-
they be informed what the foreign companies intend to do.
It is believed that the Japenese authorities have come
to think that, as long as crude oil supplies continue
to be available in unlimited quantities, it vill not be
difficult for the Japenese oil refiners to expand their
cepacity rapidly in order to meet the situation should
the foreign oil companies decide to abandon the field.
It is reported that some of the Japanese oil companies
alreedy have plans made, but not officially approved,
for such expansion.
The gist of the conversations vas transmitted to
the head offices of the companies by the local repre-
sentatives on November 7, 1935. On Noverber 15, the
local representatives of the oil companies ere called
to the office of Mr. Kojime, the Chief of the Mining
Bureau of the Department of Commerce and Industry, who
informed them that it was desired that the foreign oil
companies indicate their intentions in regard to the
storage obligation at the earliest possible moment, as
it wes necessary for the Dep:rtment of Commerce and In-
dustry to compile the budget for compensation for oil
storage as soon as possible. This information was also
cabled to their head offices by the local representatives
of the Standard and Shell interests, and a reply was re-
ceived to the effect that an answer in regard to the
storage question would be despatched to the local repre-
sentatives in the early part of the week beginning Novem-
ber 18. At the time of writing this answer has not been
received.

=== Page 400 === (confidence 0.95)
- 5 -
The attitude of the Japanese Government being as it
is, it is difficult to see what further diplomatic action
can be taken in the premises. It appears to the Embassy
that the issue is now squerely placed before the oil com-
panies for final decision.
Respectfully yours,
Edui. .Thaitl

Department of State
Subject: Oil situation in Japan.
894.6363/227
Following an unsuccessful attempt made on November 30
to form contact by telephone with the New York office of
the Standard-Vaouum Oil Company, Mr. Hornbeok this morning
telephoned Mr. Parker.
Mr. Hornbeck stated to Mr. Parker the substance of
telegram No. 224 of November 30, 6 p.m., from the American
Embassy at Tokyo, which telegram, in brief, reports the
unanimous agreement of the American and British Embassies
and the American and British oil interests that the pres-
ont is an opportune moment for the making of further repre-
sentations to the Japanese Government in regard to the oil
FILED
situation in Japan.
Mr. Hornbeck then reverted to that portion of the
telegram under reference which states the opinion that
further representations to the Japanese Government would
be of little, if any, value unless coupled with a state-
ment to the effect that (a) the foreign oil companies are
considering seriously the question of withdrawal from
Japan;
F/G

=== Page 407 === (confidence 0.97)
- 2 -
Japan; or (b) that, as a commeroial proposition, the oil
companies cannot agree to the carrying of stooks equiva-
lont to a six-months' supply. Mr. Hornbock stated that
this appears to put squarely up to the concerned oil interests
the problem of deciding on what course they wish to proceed.
Mr. Hornbeck inquired whether Mr. Parker had seen an
article on the oil situation in Japan which appeared in a
reoent issue of the CHRISTIAN SCIENCE MONITOR and in which
the authoress, in addition to displaying considerable knowledge
of the subject, stressed the probability that the question
would be solved by means of a compromise. Mr. Parker said
that he would look up the article.
Mr. Hornbeck, in reply to an inquiry by Mr. Parker, said
that the British Ambassador at Washington had called recently
at the Dopartment to discuss the oil situation in Japan but
that at the time of that call neither the Ambassador nor the
Department was in possession of the information contained in
the Embassy's telegram of November 30.
Mr. Parker thanked Mr. Hornbeck for the Department's
cooperation and requested that the Standard-Vacuum Oil Company
be provided with a copy of the Embassy's telegram under
reference. Mr. Hornbeck stated that the substance of the
Embassy's telegram would be sent to Mr. Parker at once.
(NOTE: A paraphrase of the telegram was mailed to
Mr. Parker today).

had already sent to their representative in Japan their
apparently proposed telegram of December 4, Mr. Parkor

In the course of the conversation Mr. Parker stated

an instruotion to its representative in Japan along the
lines of the draft of cable to Agnew dated December 6.
He said that in the light of the statement that I had read
to him he would send an amendment.
With regard to the meaning of the expression in
Standard-Vacuum's draft cable "discriminatory tariffs",
Mr. Parker said that that referred to discrimination between
Japanese oil interests and foreign oil interests.
In supplement to our propared statement, I pointed out
that the course now in contemplation by the New York and
London offices of the companies differs from that recommended
by their representatives in Japan with the concurrence of
their respective Embassies. Mr. Parker replied that it
does but that they still have under consideration the other
course as an ultimate possibility. I then made the observa-

mediate step. Mr. Parker replied that that was correot.
I inquired whether Mr. Goold (Standard-Vacuum's Japan manager,
now in New York) had been consulted. Mr. Parker replied that
Mr. Goold had "been in on" their conferences.
Mr. Parker suggested that we instruct the Embassy in
