December 11, 1955.
.1√
Proposed Statement to be made to Mr. Parker of Standard-
Vacuum Company by Tolophone.
(Referenoe, Mr. Parker's letter of December 9.)
It was our understanding, on the basis of the tele-
gram from our Embassy in Tokyo whioh we gave Standard-
Vacuum last week, that the companies' representatives
and the two Embassies in Japan felt that the moment was
opportune for further official representations.
Are we correct in assuming that the telegram dated
London December 4 from the British interests has been sent
to Yokohama as their instruction to their representatives
in Japan?
If so, it would seem that the British interests, with
the conourrence of the British Government, do not now
desire that there be at this time further governmental
representation.
With regard to the question of trading in the
hydrogenation process, -- the Department feels that any
effort which the interests may desire to make to conolude
a trade with the Japanese Government should be entirely
on the responsibility or the interests. We feel that
foreign governmental agencies, especially the American
Government, should in no way be associated with the idea
or the action relating thereto. As to the political effeet
of such an offer and/or transaction, if concluded, we
feel

=== Page 418 === (confidence 0.97)
- 2 -
feel that it would strengthen Japan in certain oourses of
action by Japan of whioh other governments do not
approve. In that conneotion, we would like, with Standard-
Vacuum's authorization, to discuss this feature with our
Navy and War Departments. May we have such authorization?
In addition, we greatly doubt whether the offer, if made,
will get the results desired. If it so proves, the net
result would be to increase the self-confidence of the
Japanese and weaken the position of the companies.
If the companies wish that the Embassies give intro-
ductions for their representatives toward conference with
the Japanese military authorities, all that we could do
would be to instruct our Embassy to approach the Japanese
Foreign Office on the subject. We would not wish to go
direotly to the Japanese military authorities. We would
wish to make it understood to the Japanese Foreign Office
that any aotion we take must be with that office and with
its knowledge.
We have no objection to an effort by the companies
to take the matter up direotly with the Mitsuis and to
try to persuade the Mitsuis to arrange for the conferences
envisaged. We oannot advise with regard to choosing
between the Mitsuis and possible other companies. Ir the
matter is first taken up with the Mitsuis, and if that
approach should fail of satisfactory results, it might
possibly be found more difficult for us thereafter to take
the matter up with the Foreign Office.
WO

=== Page 419 === (confidence 0.97)
- 3 -
We are not very happy over a portion of the draft of
Standard-Vacuum's proposed cable to Agnew. We peroeive
no good reason why American representatives of an Amerioan
company should express "our sympathy with what we think
are intended to be the real objeotives they (the Japanese)
wish to attain; that we are offering our suggestion of
hydrogenation assistance with the thought that we would
thus be cooperating with them towards attaining such
objeotive . . . . in making their coal supplies readily
available for petroleum manufacture" whence "they would
always have under their own control during an emergenoy
a substantial source of constant supply". We do not think
that that sounds well, and we doubt whether the Japanese
would think highly of it.
In the nert sentence, we think there should be inserted
after the word "arranging" additional words "through the
Foreign Orfice".
We doubt whether it is advisable to say to the Japanese
that "the heads of the companies here will be prepared to go
to Japan to conolude the matter". We think that the companies
might weaken their position if they let it appear that they
are too greatly worried over the possible outoome.
We do not understand just what is meant, at the end of
the draft, by "disoriminatory tariffs".
WO

=== Page 420 === (confidence 0.97)
wreel that, all said and done, this matter is going
to be decided by the Japanese on the pasis of their
estimate of the relative advantages and disadvantages to
them of having the foreign oil oonpanies in or out of
Japan; and that the best chance that the companies have
of getting a decision favorable to them would lie in a
definite threat by them to yithdraw. Please refer back

received this morning, and told him that we had at once
informed Tokyo of its receipt and that I would now immediately
take up the matter of a reply to the newly arrived telegram
from Tokyo.

Reference Tokyo's telegram No. 246 of
December 19, 3 p.m., in regard to the oil
situation in Japan, --
In view of the indeterminate attitude
displayed by the New York representatives
of the Standard-Vacuum Oil Company in re-
gard to the question of possible withdrawal
of the company from Japan, Mr. Neville's
statement (see middle of paragraph one of
the Embassy's telegram under reference)
that the oil companies "could not comply
with the law . . . . and would be unable
to do business in Japan under its terms"
would seem unjustifiably categorical. How-
ever, Mr. Neville probably based his com-
ments on the statements contained in the
proposed letters (presumably later sent)
addressed by the oil companies to the Vice
Minister for Commerce and Industry which
contained the definite statement that "they
(the companies) cannot undertake any extra
capital expenditure beyond that envisaged
in the five-point memorandum" (see paragraph
one of Tokyo's telegram 240 of December 13,
6 p.m.). 228
In view of the detailed nature of the
Department's instructions to Tokyo in regard
to

=== Page 436 === (confidence 0.94)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
- 2 -
to recent developments in the situation
under reference, there would seem to be
room for improvement in coordinating the
offorts of the British and American
Embassies vis-à-vis the Japanese authorities.
However, under existing conditions
it would appear advisable to permit
Ambassador Grew to proceed in accordance
with the plan indicated in the concluding
sentence of his telegram No. 246 of
December 19, 3 p.m., in which event no
action is required of the Department.

Two. The British Ambassador on December 17,

mbassy's 240 and added that it was known that the

DEPARTMENT OF STATE

January 8, 1936.
Tokyo's despatoh No. 1590, Decomber 12,
19S5, AMERICAN OIL INTERESTS IN JAPAN,
comtains, in my opinion, no information
which would serve as basis for protest by
the American Government on the ground of
diserimination if the proposed tariff on
petroloum is enacted.
The"discrimination" in the proposed
potroleum tariff which is complained of
 the foreign oil company representatives,
as reported in the despatch, appears to
consist (if the allegations are correct)
in an advantage accruging to refiners
of petroleum products in Japan as against
importers of refined petroleum products.
It has net been shown that the law of
Japan discriminates against refining
operations in Japan by the foreign
companies (on the contrary the Japanese
authorities would probably favor such
refining operations by the foreign
companies). The fact that the foreign
companies import refined petroleum
products instead of refining petroleum
products in Japan appears to be the
result of their preference rather than
the result of any disability under
Japanese law which does not equally
apply to Japanese refiners.

=== Page 444 === (confidence 0.98)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
VISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
January 4, 1936.
RONT
Tokyo's despatch No. 1590, Decem-
bor 12, 1935, AMERICAN OIL INTERESTS IN
JAPAN, reports the developments to that
date in discussions between the oil
companies and Japanese officials with
regard to the new Japanese Petroleum
Law.
Representatives of the oil companies
have been given details of proposed
alterations in the Japanese petroleum
tariff which will actually favor
refiners of oil products over importers
of refined oil products. This advantage
will operate by reason of the fact that
the proposed tariff accepts, for customs
purposes, a theoretical gasoline content
of crude oil which is in fact exceeded
in modern refining methods. That is,
the Japanese refiner who imports a
shipment of crude oil which for customs
purposes is calculated to have a gasoline
content of 1000 tons will in fact be
able to refine from his importation more
than 1000 tons of gasoline. (The
Embassy's information in this technical
matter is presumed to derive from the
oil companies.) The proposed duty on
most lubricating oils 1s about 50% higher
than at present, but on kerosene there
is no increase.
on

=== Page 445 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
On November 30 the two foreign oil
companies presented a memorandum on the
subjeet of the proposed tariff alterations,
but in reply they were informed of the
improbability of any amendments.
The Embassy states that the proposed
tariff increases make the already gloomy
future of the foreign oil companies in
Japan still more gloomy.
In connection with the stock keeping
obligation of the new law, on December 3
the Standard and Rising Sun representatives
were read a statement by the Chief of the
Mining Bureau that the Vice Minister for
Commeree and Industry could no longer
continue the unofficial discussions and
must proceed to give effect to the Govern-
ment's policy as embodied in the law. It
is probable that this statement was for
the purpose of definitely disclaiming
the fivo-point memorandum as an obligation.
On December 10 and December 11 the
Rising Sun and Standard representatives,
respectively, received telegrams from
their head offices authorizing negotiations
with the Japanese Government with a view
to assisting the Japanese Government
to obtain an independent supply of oil
by use of their patent rights for the
hydrogenation of coal. The companies
/235
had in mind making this offer to the
Japanese Government in return for the
terms outlined in the five-point memorandum.
(A subsequent telegram [Tokyo's No. 251,
December 26, 2 p.m., paragraph one.],
however, reports that the Japanese
Ministry

=== Page 446 === (confidence 0.97)
- 3 -
mistry of Commerce and Industry has
fimitely stated that the matter of
liguefastion of coal has no connection
with the enforcement of the stook
keeping obligation.) The contention
of the oil companies would appear to
be that possession by the Japanese Govern-
ment of the process of hydrogenation of
coal would give the Japanese Government
a source of oil (by liquefaction of coal)
which could be used by the Japanese
Government in an emergency and would
consequently make unnecessary the pro-
visions of the Japanese Petroleum Law
requiring storage of several months
supply.

The Honorable
The Secretary of State,
UMT
Washington.
Sir:
I have the honor to refer to the Embassy's de-
226
spatch No. 1554, dated November 19, 1935, and to pre-
vious correspondence on the subject of the difficulties
being experienced in Japan by the American oil interests
under the Petroleum Industry Law, and to report the following
developments in the situation.
Proposed Increase in the Tariff on Petroleum.
In the latter part of November the newspapers pub-
lished reports to the effect that the Japanese Government
had
FA

=== Page 448 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
had decided to increase the tariff rates on crude and
refined petroleum, beginning with the next fiscal year
(April 1, 1936) in order to raise enough revenue to.
cover the compensation which the Government will pay to
importers and refiners for stock-keeping. It was also
reported that a larger proportionate increase would be
imposed on imported gasoline than on crude oil. on
November 21 the representatives of the Standard-Vacuum
Oil Company and the Rising Sun retroleum compeny called
on the Vice Minister for Commerce and Industry to as-
certain the truth of this report, and were told that the
Government did intend to raise the tariff rates on pe-
troleum, but that the increase was only for general re-
venue purposes and that there was no intention to dis-
criminate between importers and refiners. The repre-
sentatives of the oil companies vere invited to criti-
cize the draft of the proposed tariff changes, which
would be given them in confidence.
Later the representatives of the oil companies were
given the details of the proposed alterations in the
petroleum tariff rates by the Chief of the Fuel Section
of the Ministry of Commerce and Industry. Although the
oil companies were assured by the Vice Minister for Commerce
and Industry that there would be no discrimination in the
increased rates as between importers and refiners, the
foreign oil companies claim that there is in fact a very
substantial discrimination. The subject is highly techni-
cal, but briefly the situation is as follows: The avowed
purpose of the proposed changes is to apply an increase
of

=== Page 449 === (confidence 0.98)
-3 -
of 1.7 sen per gallon on gasoline, and to apply such in-
crease on crude oil as will correspond to 1.7 sen per gal-
lon of the gasoline content of the oil. This appears to
be reasonable and not discriminatory, but the discrimination
lies in the method of calculating the gasoline content of
the crude oil. The authorities propose to take as the gaso-
line content, for the purpose or calculating duty, one-
half of the volatile oils distilled off at 275 degrees
Centigrade. In actual practice, however, more than one-
half of the volatile oils distilled off at this tempera-
ture, when California crude oil is used, consists of gaso-
line, and the gasoline production is further increased
by the cracking method, which is used in all the larger
refineries in Japan. Whereas the duty on the gasoline
content of crude oil, therefore, is to be calculated ac-
cording to the obsolete fractional distillation method,
the gasoline will actually be produced by the cracking
method and a much larger amount produced(estimated in some
cases as nearly double) than was taken into calculation by
the customs. The increased cost to the refiners will
therefore be only about .8 or .9 sen per gallon, while
the importers will have to pay the full duty of 1.7 sen
per gallon. If an increase of 1 sen per gallon, calcu-
lated on the refiners' costs, is permitted in the price
of gasoline to compensate for the inoreased duty, the
American and British oil companies will be compelled to
bear an extra burden of .7 sen per gallon, or approximately
Yen 1,000,000 per annum for both companies.
The

=== Page 450 === (confidence 0.99)
- 4 -
The tariff rate on lubricating oils and greases
is also to be raised, but there is to be no increase
in the duty on the topped crudes from which the Japa-
nese refiners produce their lubricating oils. The oil
companies estimate that 83 per cent of the lubricating
oils which they import will have to pay a 50 per cent
higher rate of duty, totaling about Yen 232,000 per annum
for the two compenies. There is to be no increase in
the tariff rate on kerosene or on the kerosene content
of the crude oil, probably in order to assist the Japa-
nese refiners in their export trade in kerosene.
On November 30, 1935, the two foreign oil companies
presented a memorandum to the Chief of the Fuel Section
of the Ministry of Commerce and Industry, pointing out
wherein the proposed alterations in the tariff would im-
pose heavier burdens on the importers than upon the domes-
tic refiners. The oil companies' representatives were in-
formed, however, that the proposed alterations in the
tariff had already gone to the Tariff Investigation Com-
mission for examination, and that it was unlikely that
any amendments would be made.
In this connection there is enclosed a memorandum
of a conversation between Mr. D.D. MacGregor, of the
Mitsubishi Oil Company, which is affiliated with the
Associated Oil Company of California, and Mr. Dickover,
of this Embassy, in which Mr. MacGregor stated that the
proposed tariff increases were part of a plan to drive
the foreign oil companies out of the distributing and
retail business in Japan. Whether or not this is true,
the

=== Page 451 === (confidence 0.98)
- 5 -
the fact remains that the proposed tariff increases make
the already gloomy future of the foreign oil companies in
Japan still more gloomy.
Stock-keeping Obligation.
1.1
As was reported in the Embassy's despatch l.o. 1554, the
Japanese cuthorities on November 6 and November 15 requested
the foreign oil companies to inuic:te at the earliest pos-
sible moment their intentions in regard to the question of
carrying six months' stocks of oil in storage in Japan. On
December 3, 1935, the representatives of the Standard-Vacuum
Oil Company and the Rising Sun Petroleum Company were called
to the Mining Bureau of the Ministry of Commerce and Industry,
where the Chief of the Bureau expressed his disappointment
that no reply had as yet been received from the two companies
in regard to the stock-keeping question, and, reading from a
paper in his hand, informed the representatives that the
Vice Minister for Commerce and Industry regretted that he
could no longer continue the unofficial discussions which
had been inaugurated when the Directors of the companies
were in Japan last winter and which he had continued with
good-will, and that henceforth the Mining Bureau, although
still desirous of knowing as soon as possible the decision
of the companies in regard to stock-keeping, must proceed to
give effect to the Government's policy as embodied in the
law.
The Chief of the Bureau did not proceed to any explan-
ation of his remarks, and the foreign oil interests have
since been speculating as to the exact meaning of the re-
marks. It is believed that the Chief of the Bureau may
have intended his remarks as a warning to the oil companies
that

=== Page 452 === (confidence 0.99)
- 6 -
that they could no longer consider themselves as entitled
to special consideration because of the discussions which
had been in progress, and that they would in the future be
liable to the penalties provided for non-compliance with
the law, such as reduction of quotas and fines.
.The remarks
may also have been intended as a means of putting the Japa-
nese officials in a position where they would no longer
be bound, even loosely, by the five-point memorandum of
last April. Moreover, ne speper reports have uppeared in
Japan to the effect that Directors of the two companies
were again coming to Japan to resume their discussions with
the Jap nese authorities over the petroleum question. The
remarks of the Chief of the ining Bureau may have been
intended as a warning that such visits would not be velcome
and that the discussions would not be resumed.
On Decenber 10 the Rising Sun Petroleum Company
received a telegram from the head office in London, instruct-
ing the local representative "regretfully to inform the
Japanese that we cannot undertake any extra capital expen-
diture beyond that envisaged in the Five Point emorandum"
and proceeding to outline a plan for granting to the Japa-
nese Government, or to any private concern designated
by the Japanese Governnent, a license to use the patent
rights held by the Royal Dutch-Shell interests and others
for the liquefaction of coal by the hydrogenation process,
in return for a confirmation by the Japanese Government
of the Five Point Memorandum as originally negotiated. On
December 1l the Standard-Vacuum Oil Company received a
telegram from its head office in New York, concurring with
the telegram received by the Rising Sun Petroleum Company
and

=== Page 453 === (confidence 0.99)
- 7 -
and authorizing the local representatives of the Company
to negotiate with the Japanese Government with a view
to assisting it to obtain an independent supply of oil
by the use of the patent rights and technical assistance
of the Company in the hyarocenation of coa. Copies of the
2/3/
two telegrams received by the local orfices of the companies
are enclosed.
This plan, and the method of proceeding in its
execution, are now under discussion by the representatives
of the two oil companies and of the American and ritish
Embassies and the Netherlands Legation. It seems to be
the general opinion of those interested in the proposal
that the first approach should be to the Japanese officials
who have been handling the oil uestion, rather than to
private Japanese interests who in turn would approach
the Government. The question also arises of whether the
approach to the authorities should or should not be pre-
ceded by informal diplomatic representations designed to
prepare the ground for the proposal. Before any steps
are taken by this Ebassy, however, the Department will
be informed and instructions will be requested.
Some of the diplomatic officers interested in the oil
situation in Japan are not inclined to be optimistic in
regard to the new proposal. In their opinion, the Japa-
nese Government, if its purpose is the obtaining of a
supply of oil for emergency use without cost to itself,
could hardly be expected to be willing to spend large
sums to obtain patent rights and to build factories in
order to obtain the supply of oil from other sources.
Or if, as is sometimes claimed, the purpose of the recent
legislation

=== Page 454 === (confidence 0.97)
- 8 -
legislation is to drive the foreign oil companies out
of the domestic Japanese trade in order that Japanese
companies may have the business, any proposal which would
permit the foreign oil companies to remain in the field
would not be acceptable.
Respectfully yours,
Edum  harille
Edwin L. Neville
Chargé d'Affaires ad interim

MEMORANDUM
December 3, 1935.
Conversation: Mr. D.D. MaoGregor, of the Mitsubishi Oil
Company, Ltd. (Affiliated with the Associated
Oil Company).
Mr. Dickover.
I saw Mr. MacGregor last evening and discussed the
oil situation in Jupan, Manchuria and North China ith him
for some time. Mr. MacGregor, who is in Japan to watch the
Associated interests in the Mitsubishi Oil Company, is in
close touch with the Japanese engaged in the oil business
here and often has interesting and sometimes accurate and
valuable information.
In discussing the proposed t riff increase on crude
and refined oils, Mr. MacGregor s id that it undoubtedly
would work greater hardship on the imorters of refined oils
than on the Japanese refiners, due to the method of cal-
culating the available gasoline content of the crude on the
old fractional distillation method instead of the more
modern cracking method. He said that it was just a part
of the general attempt to drive the foreign oil companies
out of the distributing business in Japan. The foreign oil
companies had been losing money because of the low price
of gasoline in Japan for some years past; just recently,
due to the raise in the price allowed by the Department of
Commerce and Industry, the foreign oil companies were again
making a little profit. This would not suit the books of
the Japanese, so they had proposed a discriminatory tariff
increase, which would again force the foreign oil companies
into the red. In this way they hoped to induce the foreign
011

=== Page 456 === (confidence 0.97)
-2-
oil companies to leave Japan. hat the Japanese really
desire, and what they have been working toward for some time,
said r. MacGre or, was the eliination of the foreign oil
companies from the distributing and retailing trade in
Japan. They want the foreign oil companies to do simoly
a wholesale trade, selling to the Japanese c.i.f. Japanese
ports, and leaving the entire distributing and selling trade
to the Japanese. I said that I should not think that the
Japanese would want the trade, with all the restrictions and
burdens put upon the trade recently. Mr. acGregor said that
as soon as the foreign compenies are driven out, the bur-
dens and restrictions vill disa pear, or means will be found
to coipensate for them.
E。。D.

=== Page 457 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No. 2 to despatoh No.
1590 of December 13, 1935 from
the Embassy at Tokyo.
Copy of telegram dated December 9, 1935, from
London, received by the Rising Sun Petroleum
Jompany, Yokohama.
Re your telegram 30th September re your telegram 7th
November:
Our Directors have given most exhaustive consideration
to proposals of Japanese Government but we have regretfully
come to the conclusion that compensation suggestion does
not appear to contain any real alleviation of the situa-
tion in that we quite understand no Government can
bind itself for a long period on such a matter and there-
fore, in our opinion and entirely from commercial point
of view, we cannot see we are guaranteed an economic re-
turn on the extra capital expenditure required by the
six months' stock-holding rule, particularly under a
system of annual quotas which gives us no security for
trade for longer than twelve months.
Quite apart from this question of capital expenditure
there is also uncertain basis at all times for pa ment
for oil stocks held in the country, and here again from
a purely commercial point of view we feel that we are
placed in a position that no bona fide trader should
be asked to undertake.
Still another condition which no conmercial concern could
accept is the requirement for stocks to be replaced after
Government requisition, which in effect would give to the
Government an option on the whole of our production.
Therefore, although we are willing and anxious to supply the
needs of the country in the same manner as we have always
done under all circumstances in the past, we must ask
you regretfully to inform the Government we cannot under-
take any extra capital expenaiture beyond that envisaged
in the Five Point Memorandum, which Memorandum was drawn
up after prolonged discussions between parties and after,
as we believe, both Government and Oil Companies had fully
realized the desire and difficulties of the respective
parties and which was reached as an effective and satis-
factory compromise to meet conditions of today.
However, while we have come to the above conclusion after
mature deliberation, which conclusion we believe is the
only one which a commercial concern could consider under
the ciroumstances, we have one suggestion to make to
the Government which may enable them to confirm the Five
Point Memorandum as originally negotiated and at the same
time to achieve the aim which they have clearly shown they
have in mind, namely the safeguarding of the petroleum
supply position in time of emergenoy. The Government are
aware that we are part owners of the only satisfactory
patent

=== Page 458 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
patent for the liquefaction of coal by the hydrogenation
process and we can arrange with our partners in this
patent that license for its use shall be granted to the
Japanese Government or such conmercial or other body
as they might appoint to enable them to erect such plant
to produce petroleum products from native sup,ly of coal.
The terms and conditions of such license would include
expert advice for the erection of the plant and preliminary
running.
For your private guidance only, New York Friends have sug-
gested to us that erection of Hydrogenation lant almost
certainly would have to be undertaken by Japunese commercial
interest and for this purpose they would favour Mitsuis.
On the face of it we can see no objection to such an arrange-
ment and therefore we think you should carefully consider
with Friends, Dutch Embassy and British Embassy what form
of hydrogenation offer should be best made to the Govern-
ment. Possibly best way would be to make offer direct
as above and at the sane time suggest that the actual
license should be given to Mitsuis. Also it may be con-
sidered advisable to explain whole situation, including
hydrogenation offer, to Mitsui before approaching the
Government, with the idea that they may assist and use
their influence towards favourable settlement. As to this
we give you full discretion to act in accordance with
general opinion.

=== Page 459 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No. 3 to despatch
No. 1590 of December 13, 1935.
from the Embassy at Tokyo.
Copy of telegram from New York, received by
the Standard-Vacuum Oil Company, Yokohama,
on Dece: ber 11. 1935.
We and Friends have now exchanged views and we are advised
that London is today despatcning a cable with which we have
expressed concurrence. They in turn have expressed con-
currence with this message. It should be understood that
our following proposal is to be in exchange for the Govern-
ment's adherence to the Tive -oint emorandum.
Please arrange with Friends for negotiations vith Japenese
Government whereby we offer to assist them with p tent rights
and technical assistance for the installation in Japan of
hydrogenation plant to manufacture oil from coal. e suggest
that the uncommercial and unreasonable requirements of the
Control Law as it stends and our objections to compliance
should first be clearly stated, at the same time ex ressing
our sympathy with what we think are intended to be the real
objectives they wish to attain, that we are offering our
suggestion of hydrogenation assistance with the thought
that we would thus be co-operating with them towards at-
taining such obj ctives, as by using our experience and
patents in making their coal supply readily available for
petroleum manufacture they would always have under their
own control during emergency a substantial source of con-
stant supply.
The American and British Embassies are prepared to give you
assistance in arranging introductions to Naval and Military
officials if you require them.
It is our thought that if the hydrogenation suggestions were
accepted it would probably be necessary for some Japanese
commercial house to build and operate the plant(s) with special
Government subsidy and we think Mitsui would be the best for
that purpose: therefore we suggest, if Friends agree, that
the entire situation be explained to vistui and that you
consult them as to the most effective manner of presenting
the idea to the Japanese Government; that perhaps Mitsui
could arrange for introductions without the aid of the
Embassies and might in various ways be of great help in the
matter.
You may say to the Government, if they indicate willingness to
discuss the suggestion given, that the heads of the company
here will be prepared to go to Japan to conclude the matter
or will discuss the subject further with Japanese representa-
tives elsewhere if they desire.
With these observations we leave it to your discretion to go
ahead along these lines.
AB

your general consideration , we feel it advantageous to put
time limit on Governnent's ecceptance. e would like reply
about the end of the month but leave this entirely to your
judgment.

The Honorable
The Secretary of State,

Sir:
I have the honor to refer to the Embassy's despatch
237
No. 1590, dated December 12, 1935, and to previous cor-
respondence on the subject of the difficulties being ex-
perienced in Japan by the American oil interests under
the Petroleum Industry Law, and to report the following
dovelopments in the situation.
Stook-keeping Obligation.
As was reported in the Embassy's despatch No. 1590,
the Standard-Vacuum Oil Company and the Rising Sun Petroleum
Company
F/FG

=== Page 476 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
authorized their looal representatives to inform the
Japanese Government that they could not, as a commeroial
proposition, undertake the additional expenditure re-
quired for storing six months' supplies of oil, and to
offer the Japanese Government a coal liquefaction process
in return for a confirmation by the Japanese Government
of the program outlined in the Five Point Memorandum of
last April . This was done by the local representatives
on December 14, by means of a joint letter addressed
to the Vice Minister for Commerce and Industry, a copy
1/ of whioh is enclosed. This letter points out that the
stock-holding compensation plan does not contain eny
real alleviation of the situation, that the companies
are not assured of an economic return upon the addi-
tional capital expenditure required by the six months'
stock-holding, that there is an uncertain basis at all
times for payment for stocks held in Japan (which can
be requisitioned by the Government at prices set by the
Government), that the requirement that stocks be replaced
after requisition practically gives the Government an option
on the whole output of the compenies, and that therefore
the companies are unable to undertake any extra capital
expenditure beyond that envisaged in the Five Point Memo-
randum. The letter then proceeds to outline the offer
of the hydrogenation process.
On December 16 the local representative of the Stan-
dard- Vacuum Oil Company approached the Mitsui Bussan Kaisha
with the offer of the hydrogenation process, because it
was believed that the Japanese Government would want the
projeot

=== Page 477 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
projeot carried out by a commeroial organization rather
than by the Government, because it was known that the
Mitsui Bussan Kaisha had already exhibited an interest
in acquiring the rights to the process, and because it
was thought that the offer would be better received by
the Japanese Government if it were endorsed by a firm
of the standing of Mitsui. The proposal was received
somewhat coolly by the representative of the Mitsui Bussen
Kaisha, who pointed out that the Government had found diffi-
culty in finding the small amount of money required par-
tially to compensate the oil companies for the cost of
stook-holding, and would find it much more difficult to
obtain funds to finance a acheme of such magnitude as
that envisaged by the companies' offer. He also pointed
out that the process is not commercially profitable. How-
ever, he promised to explore the possibilities of the offer,
and suggested that the oil companies might be relieved of
their troubles by selling from "the other side", implying
that the companies should turn their Japan business over
to Japanese firms.
On December 22 Mr. Dickover of the Embassy had a
lengthy conversation with ir. Kurusu, the Chief of the
Bureau of Commercial Affairs of the Foreign Office, on the
subject of oil. A memorandum of this conversation is en-
2/olosed . Mr. Kurusu said that the Ministry of Conmerce and
Industry would send a "pretty stiff" reply to the companies'
letter of December 14 and suggested that the companies had
three alternatives, namely, to withdraw from Japan entirely,
to carry six months' storage with a Government guarantee of
minimum

=== Page 478 === (confidence 0.98)
- 5 -
to comply with the law; that administrative measures, such
as reduction of quotas to a point where the companies will
be complying with the law, will also be applied; and that
the matter of the process for the liquefaction of coal has
no connection with the obligation of stock-holding.
Representations.
The local representatives of the foreign oil com-
panies requested their respective Embassies, upon sub-
mitting their letter of December 14 to the Ministry of
Commerce and Industry, to support by diplomatic represen-
tations their refusal to carry six months' stocks and
their request for a reversion to the program of the Five
Point Memorandum. The Department approved of the repre-
sentations, provided they could be coordinated in sub-
stance and timing with those of the British Ambassador
and provided that the American Government not be asso-
ciated in any way in the representations with the offer
to provide the Japanese Government with the hydrogenation
process. The British Ambassador, acting upon his general
instructions, took up the matter orally and informally
with the Minister for Foreign Affairs on December 17, in
the course of a conversation on other matters. He said
that the oil companies had been greatly disappointed by
the recent course of events and had regretfully de-
cided that they could not, on purely commercial grounds,
undertake to keep six months' stocks. He added that he
was not speaking under instructions, but that he hoped
that the Japanese Government would seriously consider
the companies' letter to the Ministry of Commerce and
Industry

=== Page 479 === (confidence 0.99)
-6 -
Industry and the offer contained therein. He further
stated that it was known that the military were in faot
responsible for the present law, and thet it was unreason-
able to expect the oil companies to bear the burden of un-
economically storing oil for military purposes. Mr. Hirota
apparently had not seen the letter from the oil companies
to the Ministry of Commerce and Industry, but he gave it as
his opinion that the whole question would probably be cired
when the Diet meets, on the ground that the enforcement
of the law has been delayed until July 1, l936. He added
that it was possible that some solution of the difficulties
would be found either before or when the explanations are
given in the Diet.
In the course of a conversation which Mr. Neville
of the Embassy had with Mr. Kurusu of the Foreign Office
on December 18, Mr. Kurusu brought up the subject of oil
and asked Mr. Neville if the letter which the oil companies
had sent to the Ministry of Commerce and Industry meant
that the oil companies had the intention of defying the
Japanese Government. Mr. Neville stated that it meant
only that, purely as a commercial proposition, the oil
companies were unable to comply with the stock-holding
provisions of the law and that they could not therefore
do business in Japan under the terms of the law. Mr.
Kurusu then proceeded to state that the decision of the
oil companies was a source of considerable perturbation
to the Ministry of Commerce and Industry. He said that
it would be necessary to do something in regard to the
situation, because Japan would have to look elsewhere
and make other arrangements in order to obtain the oil
supplies

=== Page 480 === (confidence 0.98)
- 7 -
supplies necessary for the country if the foreign oil
companies should withdraw from Japan. He then ex-
pressed the intention of discussing the situation with
the local representatives of the foreign oil companies
before further developments took place.
As the British Ambassador took up the oil question
with the Minister for Foreign Affairs only incidentally
in the course of a conversation on other matters, this
Embessy decided that it would be best to follow the same
procedure, and instead of calling on the Minister for
Foreign Affairs for the sole purpose of discussing the
oil situation, to await an opportunity to bring up the
question when interviewing the Minister on some other
subject. This decision was strengthened by the fact
that Mr. Kurusu had indicated to Mr. Neville that the
Japanese Government is perturbed over the situation and
is giving it serious attention. Unless something occurs
to alter the situation, therefore, the first opportunity
will be taken to make informal and oral representations
to the Minister or Vice Minister for Foreign Affairs,
in the course of conversation on other subjeots.
Respectfully yours,
Joseph C. Grew.

from the Embassy at Tokyo.
14th Decemger, 1935.
Your Excellenoy:
We beg leave to advise you that we have received by
cable from our principals a communication in regard to the
information and explanations kindly given to us by Your
Excellency and Mr. Kurusu, Chief of the Bureau of Com-
mercial Affairs of the Foreign Office, concerning the policy
and plans of the Imperial Japanese Government as embodied
in the Petroleum Industry Law and the revision thereof
effected by Imperial Ordinance No. 272 and Commerce and
Industry Departmental Ordinance No. 5 of 19th September, 1935.
Our principals wish us to inform Your Excellency that
they have given most exhaustive consideration to the position
but they regret they have come to the conclusion that the
stock-holding compensation plan does not appear to contain
any real alleviation of the situation. They quite understand
that no Government can bind itself for a long period on such
a matter: therefore, in their opinion and entirely from a
commercial point of view, they cannot see that our companies
are assured an economic return on the additional capital
expenditure required by the six months' stock-holding ob-
ligation - particularly under a system of annual quotas which
gives our companies no security for trade for longer than
twelve months.
Quite apart from this question of capital expenditure
there is also an uncertain basis at all times for payment
for stocks of petroleum held in Japan : and here again, from
a purely commeroial standpoint, our principals feel that our
companies are placed in a position that no bona fide trader
should

=== Page 482 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
should be asked to undertake. Still another condition which
no commeroial concern could accept is the requirement that
stocks must be replaced after Government requisition, which
in effeot would give the Government an option on the whole of
our companies' production.
Therefore, although our companies are willing and anxious
to supply the needs of the Japanese Empire in the same manner
as we have always done under all circumstances in the past, our
principals are regretfully compelled to instruct us to inform
Your Excellency's Government that they cannot undertake any
extra capital expenditure beyond that envisaged in the Five
Point Memorandum which was drawn up after prolonged discussions -
and after, our principals believe, the officials of the Imperial
Japanese Government and our companies both had fully realized
each other's desires and difficulties - and which was reached
as an effective and satisfactory compromise to meet the conditions
of today.
Nevertheless, although our principals have reached the
foregoing conclusion after mature deliberation and believe
it to be the only one which a commercial concern could consider
in the circumstances, they wish to put forward one suggestion
which may enable the Imperial Japanese Government to confirm
the Five Point Memorandum as originally negotiated and at the
same time achieve the aim which they have clearly shown they
have in mind, namely the safeguarding of the petroleum supply
position in time of emergency.
As Your Excellenoy is no doubt aware, our companies are
part-owners of the only satisfactory patent for the liquefaction
of

=== Page 483 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
of coal by the process of hydrogenation and our principals
have authorized us to state that they can arrange with our
companies' co-partners in this patent that the license for
its use be granted to the Imperial Japanese Government -
or to such commercial or other body as they may appoint -
to enable them to ereot plant to produce petroleum products
from the indigenous supply of coal. The terms and condi-
tions of such a licence would include expert advice for the
erection of the plant and its preliminary running.
In communicating the foregoing to Your Excellency we beg
to emphasize that by utilizing our experience and patents
to make the domestic coal supply readily available for the
manufacture of petroleum by the hydrogenation process, the
Imperial Japanese Government would always have under their
control a substantial source of constant supply in any
emergenoy. We feel therefore that this proposal made by
our principals with a view to solving our respective prob-
lems is of the highest constructive value and importance
and we shall await with interest the views of Your Ex-
cellency's Government.
We are,
Your Excellency's obedient servants,
For the Rising Sun Petroleum Company, Ltd.
Managing Director
For the Standard-Vacuum Oil Company.
Assistant General Manager.
His Excellenoy Yoshino Shinji,
Vice-Minister of Commerce & Industry.
T O K YO.

Tokyo Embassy
MEMORANDUM
December 23, 1935.
Conversation: Mr. S. Kurusu, Chief of the Bureau of
Commercial Affairs, Foreign Orrice, Tokyo.
Mr. E. R. Dickover.
In the course of a long conversation with Mr.
Kurusu last evening, he brought up the matter of the
Petroleum Industry Law and the position of the foreign
oil companies. He first said that he thought that it
was "rather silly" of the oil companies to send to the
Department of Commerce and Industry a definite refusal
to store sir months' stocks of oil in Japan, because
it put the Japanese Government in a position where it
would have to take a decided stand. I pointed out that
the companies had no alternative - the Shokosho was de-
manding a definite reply, and as the storing of six
months' supply of oil was not feasible as a commercial
proposition, the companies could only refuse to do it.
He said that the Japanese oil companies were carrying
out the requirement of storing the oil. and if they
could do it, why could not the foreign companies? I
pointed out that the foreign oil companies were not in
the same position as the Japanese companies, as the
. latter were sure that the Government would take care of
them and assist them in developing refining in Japan,
whereas the foreign companies were in a most precarious
position, with uncertain quotas and prices and moreover
were faced with the problem of meeting the extreme
nationalism in Japan. Mr. Kurusu then inveighed against
the

=== Page 485 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
the tendenoy toward economic nationalism throughout the
world, stating that he himself, like Mr. Machida, the
Minister for Commerce and Industry, was a liberal economist
of the old Adam Smith school of thought, believing in the
principle of laissez faire . He stated that he thought
that something might be done to make the position of the
foreign oil companies more secure, such as guaranteeing
them minimum quotas. I said that the quotas would have
to be guaranteed for a long period, say twenty years, in
order that the companies would have a reasonable opportunity
to amortize their investments. Moreover, I said, there
would have to be some sort of guarantee that the companies
would be able to sell at a price which would give them a
profit, as the present system of price-fixing by the Govern-
ment was an added factor of uncertainty. I pointed out
that during recent years the foreign oil compenies had not
made any considerable profit in Japan - in fact, during
some years they had had a loss. Kurusu said that he
could not understand that, because the Japanese refiners
were able to buy our crude, refine it, and sell the pro-
ducts at a profit. If they could do it, why could not
the American companies, with their much larger plants
and turnover? I said that that did not quite state the
case, because the importers of refined products had to
operate under a tariff system which gave a decided ad-
vantage to the domestic refiners. Moreover, I said, the
Japanese refiners, as I understood it, had not been making
a profit. Such dividends as they had paid, according to
my information, had been paid out of capital. Anyway,
ga1d

=== Page 486 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
said Kurusu, the unprofitable price of gasoline is
the companies' own fault. They starteda price war
before the Government ever became involved in the oil
question.
Mr. Kurusu then stated that the Ministry of Com-
merce and Industry would soon send the foreign oil com-
panies a "pretty stiff" reply to their letters notifying
the Government that they could not agree to store six
months' supply of oil. He said that the representa-
tives of the foreign oil companies, after receiving
their replies, had better not run to Yoshino or others
in the Ministry of Commerce and Industry to discuss
the matter but had better come to him (Kurusu). He
said that he would not invite them to come to him, but
that he would be glad to discuss matters with them in a
friendly spirit if they should desire it after receiving
the replies. I said that I would convey his suggestion
to the American oil interests.
Mr. Kurusu then expressed the opinion that there
were three alternatives which the foreign oil companies
could follow, namely, (1) they could "pack up" and leave
Jepan, but this was the least desirable of the alterna-
tives, (2) they could agree to put in six months' storage
on condition that they are given some sort of govern-
mental guarantee of minimum business over a period of
time, or (3) they could have their quotas out to a point
where their present tankage would represent six months'
storage of the reduoed quotas. I said that I had lived
in the Orient for a long time, and that I knew that
affairs here were not usually as cut and dried as was
intimated by Mr. Kurusu - that generally there was
found to be some method of compromise. I asked Mr.
Kurusu

=== Page 487 === (confidence 0.99)
Kurusu if he had any ideas as to a compromise soheme,
especially any soheme which would permit a reversion
to the Five Point program negotiated last April. Mr.
Kurusu said that he had not liked to speak of it for
fear of being misunderstood, but that he had been con-
siduring, for some time past, the plan of having Japa-
nese capital carry six months' stock for the foreign
companies. He said that some arrangement might be
reaohed whereby large Japanese capitalists, such as the
Mitsuis, could take over the Standard-Vacuum distri-
buting organization in Japan and put up the additional
capital to provide for the six months' storage, the
profits from the business to be divided between the
two organizations according to the value of the invest-
ments. The arrangement of details, said Mr. Kurusu,
would be none of the Government's business - the com-
panies would have to work that out themselves. But the
foreign oil companies would thereby be relieved of the
burden of stock-keeping and would simply sell o.i.f.
Japanese ports to the concerns in Japan.
I stated that there were several objeotions to this
scheme. In the first place, the foreign oil companies
had been building up their businesses in Japan for some
forty or fifty years, and they would not like to turn over
this carefully-nurtured trade to Japanese interests. In the
second place, the profits from the trade at present were
insufficient to pay a decent return on the capital in-
vested by the oil interests, and therefore the profits
could not be expected to pay adequate returns to both
sets of investors. In the third place, the large Japa-
nese interests , such as Mitsui and Mitsubishi, already
had refineries in Japan, and it was only to be expected
that

=== Page 488 === (confidence 0.98)
- 5 -
that they would throw all the available business to their
products, in order to obtain the refining as well as the
distributing profit. As a result, the foreign oil com-
panies would find their sales to Japan gradually decreasing.
Kurusu said that those questions could all be settled be-
tween the business men - that contracts could be made for
long-term deliveries. I said that I believed that Mitsui
had had some such plan in view before the oil law went
into effect, as I knew that they had approached the Amer-
ioan oil interests on the subject, but that they had
dropped the negotiations when the oil law was passed by
the Diet, presumably because they thought the plan no
longer feasible. Kurusu said that that was just busi-
ness - they were using the oil law as a means of getting
lower valuations on the Standard properties and good-will.
He said, however, that as far as he knew, no Japanese
interests had ever negotiated with the British oil in-
terests in Japan, and he supposed that the Staniard in-
torests would not now desert their friends and make some
kind of a contract alone. I said that I had no informa-
tion on that point,that their future actions would of course
depend upon future developments, but that my impression was
that neither one would take any important step unless the
other took the same step.
Mr. Kurusu then turned to the subject of the offer made
by the oil interests to trade their coal hydrogenation pro-
cess to the Japenese Government for a reversion to the Five
Point program negotiated last April. Mr. Kurusu said that
he did not think that the scheme would work. In the first
place, the precess is not commercially profitable - very
heavy intestments would be required to erect the necessary
plants

=== Page 489 === (confidence 0.98)
- 6 -
plants and the gasoline produced cost considerably more
than gasoline made from crude oil. The Government had no
money to invest in the plants and could not subsidize the
product, as is done in England. Moreover, said Kurusu, the
Japanese have been working on coal hydrogenation for a long
time, end have developed a process even better than the
I.G. process which the oil companies propose to trade to
Japan. The process developed in Japan is a military secret
but is said to be the best in the world. I said that I
knew nothing of the process or the proposal to trade it to
Japan, as the American Government was in no way associated
with the proposal.
The conversation then turned to other subjects.
E. R. D.

(Translation)
Momorandum of Verbal Notice
(Dec. 23rd, 1935
Shiniohi Kojima, Director
of the Mine Bureau)
We understand with great regret, from your letter
addressed to the Vice-Minister of Commerce and Industry
under date of Dec. l4th, that the principals of your com-
pany have not yet understood the spirit of the legisla-
tion of our Petroleum Industry Law and the authorities'
polioy for the operation of this law. Thinking that they
might lack a clear cognizance of it, we herewith candidly
reiterate the view of the authorities, which we wish you
to convey to the principals of your head office.
As for the 6 months' supply holding duty provided for
in the Petroleum Industry Law, the Government, in view of
the actual condition of the oil-industrialists, gave the best
consideration to it and revised the Law the other day, and
thereby made it olear that the 6 months' supply holding duty
remains unchanged and at the same time took a step to grant
a reasonable period of delay in the performance of the stock
holding duty. Though you, in your letter, refer us to the
memorandum of five paragraphs, the result of the recent
private talk, as pointed out previously, is by no means
binding upon the Government, and in the course of the talk,
we fully explained the purposes of the Petroleum Industry
Law as well as our policy for the operation of the same,
and repeatedly stated that it would be very difficult sub-
stantially to ohange the 6 months' supply holding duty.
The Government, having already taken the above-mentioned
step, must rigidly enforce the provisions of the Law - it
can not manage to change the same in any way.
Therefore

=== Page 491 === (confidence 0.98)
-2 -
Therefore an oil-industrialist, regardless of its
nationality, has no means to continue its business legi-
timately otherwise than by observing the rigid laws of the
country. If it disregards the same, a legal sanotion will
naturally be applied against it. That you, in your letter
of Dec. l4th, stated your inability to hold a stock over
3 months' supply, will create a state of the violation of
the Law. In that case, we shall be obliged to apply the
prescribed sanction against your company.
In addition to the above-mentioned legal sanction, we
shall have to take necessary administrative measures, such
as reduction in the sales quota allotted you, so as to make
it possible for you to carry on your business legitimately
in the future, and to prevent the violation of the Law as
far as possible. We will take into consideration the re-
lations of your equipment for stock-holding.
The question of liquefaction of coal is one which is
entirely different from the question of observing the Law.
How to operate the Petroleum Industry Law on account of a
change in the coal liquefaction industry or in any other
industry has nothing whatever to do with the enforcement of
the legal provisions regarding the stock-holding duty, it
being clearly a question to be dealt with in the future.

894.6363/244
Subject: Oil situation in Japan.
Mr. Thompson referred to the oil situation in Japan
and said that, while in Washington on other business, he
wished to acquaint the Department orally with certain
recent developments which, at least in part, are of a
confidential nature.
Mr. Thompson said that a sharp cleavage of opinion
had ocourred recently between the Standard Oil Company of
New York on the one hand and the Standard Oil Company of
New Jersey and the Shell interests on the other hand;
that whereas, in view of the failure of the foreign oil
companies to interest the Japanese Government in the
FILED
hydrogenation process for the manufacture of oil, the
Standard of New York desired to associate the Standard-
Vacuum Oil Company with the Mitsui interests, insofar as
the Japan trade is concerned, the Standard of New Jersey .
and the Shell interests were adamant in their refusal to
accept

=== Page 497 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
accept in any form the principle of maintaining six-
months' supplies of oil; that the Standard of New Jersey
had, however, declined to approve of the issuance to the
Japanese Government of a further blunt statement to the
effect that unless adherence is made to the conditions
outlined in the five-point memorandum of last spring the
foreign oil companies will cease all operations in Japan;
that in consequence of the foregoing a deadlock had oc-
curred; and that it is likely that there will ensue a de-
lay of approximately three weeks before there are reached
any final conclusions in regard to the matter.
At this point Mr. Thompson handed to Mr. Hornbeck a
statement (later shown to Mr. Hamilton and to Mr. Mackay)
which purported to give the substance of a conversation
by long distance telephone on January 3 between Mr. Agnew
of the Shell interests in London and Mr. H. Wilkinson of
the Shell interests in New York. Briefly stated, the mes-
sage conveyed to Mr. Wilkinson was to the effeot that news
recently received by the British Foreign Office in regard
to the Far Eastern situation had been "enoouraging"; that
relations between Great Britain and Japan have improved
materially of late; and that Japan now realizes that her
excessively aggressive policy will have to be modified to
the extent of at least taking into account Japanese rela-
tions with Great Britain and other powers.
The

=== Page 498 === (confidence 0.94)
- 3 -
The message also inoluded a statement to the effeot
that Japan, through indirect channels, had attempted to
sound out London and Paris on the subject of recent de-
velopments in North China; and that such approaches had
been received coldly with a statement to the effect that
if Japan had any ideas to convey they should be put in
the form of a concrete proposal.
Considerable doubt was expressed by all present
whether the facts of the situation would substantiate the
opinions and conclusions set forth in the message under
reference.
Mr. Thompson, before turning to another subject,
said that the Standard Oil Company desired to keep the
Department fully informed of all developments of impor-
tanoe but preferred that, at least for the moment, the
message under reference be considered as confidential and
as having been received orally.

BION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
January 30, 1936.
Tokyo's despatch No. 1629, January 10,
1936, reports current developments with
rogard to the Japanese Petroleum Industry
Law. It refers particularly to a con-
versation on December 27 between the
Standard and Shell representatives and
Mr. Kurusu of the Foreign Office in
which Mr. Kurusu was apparently sounding
out the companies further; and to a con-
versation on January 8 between
Mr. Dickover and Mr. Kurusu, the meat
of which was that Mr. Kurusu came to the
conclusion that the problem boils down
to the question of governmental guarantees
of future business at a profit to the
foreign oil companies.
Several days after this despatch
was written (January 10) the Embassy
sent 1ts telegram No. 12, January 15,
11 a.m., which reported that Mr. Kurusu
at a recent conference with the oil
companies stated that the Vice Minister
for Commerce and Industry agreed in
prinoiple with the plan of giving the
oil companies governmental guarantees
covering quotas over an extensive
period, reasonable profit and maximum
stock holding obligation, as well as
•qual treatment with Japanese refiners
should the oil companies desire to
refine in Japan. In its telegram the
Embassy

=== Page 501 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
Imbassy stated: "The Embassy and the
local representatives of the oil
companies are of the opinion that the
Japanese authorities are now sincerely
endeavoring to find some solution of
the problem which will permit opposing
oil companies to continue in business
in Japan although it appears highly
improbable that they will be able to
allow stock holding obligation. It,
therefore, appears desirable that the
Japanese proposal outlined above be
carefully considered."
In the light of the important
statements in the Embassy's telegram
of January 15, the attached despatch
of January 10 has lost its importance
and in its details does not require
your attention.

Conversations.

NOTES OF MEETING AT FOREIGN OFFICE ON
DECEMBER 27th. 1935.
PRESENT:
Mr. S. Kurusu (Chief of Bureau of Commercial Affairs,
Foreign Office)
Messrs. Ely (R.S.P.Co.) and Meyer (S.V.O.C.)
Contrary to the manner in whioh he had chosen to arrang•
this meeting through Mr. Dickover of the U.S. Embassy, Mr.
Kurusu did not in any way take up the attitude that it had been
sought by the Companies but proceeded at once to talk along
a line that had clearly been prepared in advance.
He said that, as he saw it, the Companies' reply to Mr.
Yoshino had consisted simply of the offer of the Hydrogenation
process- in which the Japanese Government were not interested -
as a quid pro quo for the stock-holding obligations, and he
described the Companies' attitude as "belligerant" in that they
had stated olearly that they intended to be law-breakers.
Messrs. Ely and Meyer demurred strongly. They emphasized
that, so far from being belligerent or desirous of breaking the
law, their companies had made what they considered to be a very
important and constructive suggestion with the specific object
of obviating any question of law-breaking; and the Hydrogenation
offer, of which Mr. Kurusu had disposed so briefly, showed that
their companies recognized the fundamental objective which the
Japanese Government had in mind and, they felt, would be of
great interest and value to those concerned with the question
of safeguarding Japan's oil supplies in time of emergency.
They emphasized also the intrinsic value of the I.H.P.Co. patents
whioh were the result of the pooled investigations of several
groups and provided without further costly experiments the only
known satisfactory means of liquifying coal by hydrogenation.
Mr. Kurusu said that research in Japan had progressed
beyond the I.H.P.Co. patents. The Government could not consider
the suggestion that had been made and consequently the Companies'
statement that for commercial reasons they could not keep more
than three months' stocks had made it necessary for the Commerce
Department to emphasize its position. This left the two parties
facing each other, each in an uncompromising attitude, and what
Mr. Kurusu wanted to do was to find a way of overcoming such a
situation. He himself had been able to think of only three, or
perhaps four possible solutions:-
The Companies could withdraw altogether from business in
Japan - but the Government did not desire this and Mr. Kurusu
did not think that the Companies themselves wanted to do so.
Or the Companies could reduce their trade so as to accom-
modate in their existing storage the stocks required by the Law.
Incidentally, however, as far as he could see, even so the
Companies must be law-breakers during the second half of next
year, as their stock-holding obligations during that period were
incurred on the basis of their imports during the preceding
twelve months. He suggested that they could easily arrange to
do the trade so sacrificed by making an arrangement to sell
wholesale c.i.f. to Japanese interests who would then be re-
sponsible for the obligations and expenditure inourred.
Then

=== Page 509 === (confidence 0.99)
Then there was another possible solution - the Companies
1ght be willing to keep the stocks stipulated by the Law in
return for suitable guarantees. Mr. Kurusu himselr felt that
any commercial concerns, foreign or Japanese, were justified
in asking for adequate guarantees, in fact they would be fools
if they did not. The sort of guarante he had in mind were
first in respect of a period of trading and the amount of trade
to be done - and he remembered that at the time when the Di-
rectors were here and the Memorandum had been drawn up Mr.
Yoshino had mentioned 50% of the increase in consumption as soon
as slack refining capacity had been used up, or some such
arrangement. Then there was the question of profits - and
here again Mr. Kurusu remembered that there had been something
in the Memorandum to the effect that prices would not be un-
remunerative and that the Companies would not have to sell at a
loss. A commercial concern must obtain a profit on its in-
vestments and of course trade at remunerative prices - and in
regard to the cuestion of prices he could not understand the
reason for there being any trouble at present. If the Japanese
Companies were able to make profits, why not the foreign Com-
panies? Anyhow the Oil Companies themselves were to blame
for the price position .....
Here Messrs. Meyer and Ely interpolated that in the first
place the Japanese Companies were not, in their opinion, making
profits; secondly refiners had in any case a big duty advantage:
and, thirdly, as regards their own Conpanies, so far from con-
sideration having been given to the question of whether they
were making any profit they had been told most frankly at the
time the last gasoline price increase was sanctioned that the
Government's decision had been based entirely on refiners'
costs to the exclusion of those of importers. They also pointed
out the incorrectness of r. Kurusu's statement concerning the
culpability of the petroleum industry.
Mr. Kurusu seid that, anyhow, the price question was
genuinely a difficult one. The Army fellows could not under-
stand the position and said "put up prices if necessary and
let the consumer pay": but it was Mr. Yoshino who had to bear
the brunt of any trouble and had had his office blookaded by
protesting taxi drivers. Also the position of the Commerce
Department had been made exceedingly difficult by state-
ments made by the present Minister's predecessor. Mr. Kurusu
said he did not know what Mr. Yoshino would be able to say
or do about prices, but he himself felt that if the Companies
could be given guarantees on the lines he had suggested they
ought to be willing to continue trading according to the Law.
As regards the continuing stock-holding obligation - a point
which he knew Messrs. Meyer and Ely would bring up in a minute -
he wanted to say that he himself was convinced that it was
impraoticable and that some means must be found to put a
maximum figure on stock-holding: this could be done, he thought,
by departmental regulations without changing the Law and the
Imperial Ordinances. If Messrs. Ely and Meyer thought that
there was any chance of a solution along these lines he would
discuss the matter in detail with Mr. Yoshino.
Messrs. Ely and Meyer said that they would be very glad
to tell their Directors what Mr. Kurusu had suggested. The
guarantees he had mentioned were those which had been dis-
oussed so much when the Directors were here, and, es they
remembered, it had been stressed previously that it was im-
possible for the Government to give such guarantees: there
wore

=== Page 510 === (confidence 0.99)
-3 -
were also many other uncertainties involved in the Law,
such as the basis of the prices at which stocks could be
taken over by the Government .......
Then, said Mr. Kurusu, why did not the Companies
consider what he himself feit to be the most practicable
solution - the use of Jepanese interests who, although
like any other business concern they would want to know
where they stood before investing money, probably would be
satisfied with less specific guarantees? He did not see
why the foreign Companies did not try to make an arrange-
ment whereby the cost of stock-holding and all the un-
certainties which the foreign Companies feared were left
to Japanese interests who would take over the distribution
of the Companies' products in Japan and to whom the Com-
panies could sell wholesale. He went on to develop this
idea at some length.
Messrs. Ely and Meyer reminded r. Kurusu that such
an arrengement had been discussed when the Directors were
here, and, as he knew, was entirely contrary to the policy
and practice of their Companies. Reverting, however, to the
question of guarantees they would like to advise their
Directors of what Mr. Kurusu had suggested.
Mr. Kurusu then said that he had been discussing
what were only his own personal ideas and he would prefer,
therefore, that they should not be passed on to the Direct-
ors before he had had an opportunity of discussing speci-
fically with Mr. Yoshino the question of guarantees. He
would do so the following day and then have another talk
with Messrs. Ely and Meyer. Later on he recollected that
Mr. Yoshino was out of town, so that he would not be able
to see him or discuss the position further with the Com-
penies until after the holidays.
In concluding the interview wir. Kurusu mentioned that
he supposed that, as the Government could not consider the
Hydrogenation offer in lieu of stock-holding, the Companies
would withdraw it. If it were not associated with stook-
holding, the Government might be interested in it.

=== Page 511 === (confidence 0.99)

the Embessy at Tokyo.
Memorandum.
January 8, 1936.
Conversation: Mr. Kurusu, Chief of the Bureau of Commercial
Affairs, Foreign Office.
Mr. Dickover.
I called on Mr. Kurusu at 3:30 this afternoon at his
request, to discuss the oil problem. After reviewing the
situation, Kurusu said that there were, of course, the
two solutions, namely, that the foreign oil companies
could withdraw from Japan entirely, which would not be
satisfactory to anyone, or they could stay here without
putting in six months' storage and have their quotas cut
down so that their present storage would represent six
months' storage. That also would not be satisfactory. I
agreed that neither of the solutions would be agreeable to
the foreign oil companies, as I understood their wishes.
Kurusu then said that a third alternative consisted in
giving the foreign oil companies guarantees, covering
minimum annual quotas, the period of years during which
the quotas would be given, and prices of petroleum pro-
ducts. He said that he thought that the companies should be
satisfied if they had guarentees covering quantities, time
and price, as well as government compensetion for storage
costs. I said that the compensation also was appropriated
annually, and that there should be more certainty in re-
gard to this point. I said that as I understood it, it
was purely a business proposition - the Japanese Govern-
ment was demending that the foreign oil companies invest
considerable

=== Page 512 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
considerable additional sums in Japan, and they, as
business men, naturally could not do so unless they
believed that they could obtain interest and profit on
their money and could amortize their investment in a
reasonable time. Kurusu said that that was quite true,
but that he thought that the guarantees should cover it.
I said that I would discuss the matter with the repre-
sentatives of the oil companies and endeavor to obtain
their views. Kurusu then said that he thought that the
agreement covering the guarantees should be made between
the Japanese and American Governments. I said that I did
not know if that could be done, but that I thought that
it would be sufficient if the agreement were made between
a Department of the Japanese Government and the foreign
oil interests, with the cognizance of the American and
British Governments. Kurusu said that that would probably
do.
I then said that I hoped that some satisfactory agree-
ment which would permit the foreign oil companies to con-
tinue in business in Japan could be reached. I said that
it seemed to me that the military in Japan, who were sup-
posed to be behind the six months' oil storage require-
ment, would be much safer if they kept the two large for-
eign oil interests as friends, with only three months'
storage, than having them as enemies with six months'
storage. I pointed out that Japan could now get plenty
of oil from various small producers in the United States
and elsewhere, but that these small producers could not
be relied upon to supply oil indefinitely, as their fields
would be exhausted in the course of time and they would
have

=== Page 513 === (confidence 0.98)
8
- 3 -
no other resources, while the two big interests
controlled seventy-five per cent of the oil resources
in the world, outside the United States and the Soviet
Union, and whenever one field became exhausted, they
could turn to another, thus assuring a steady supply of
oil at all times. Kurusu said that that was quite true,
but suppose that Jepan became involved in war and both
the American and British interests shut off their sup-
plies of oil to Japan, where would Japan be? That was
why the military were insisting on six months' stocks
of oil. The armies of all nations, said Kurusu, are
like a man sitting on the roof of a house with a hose in
his hand, waiting for the house to catch fire. The mili-
tary are always looking for signs of trouble and pre-
paring for it. I then asked if it was hopeless to look
for any change in the storage requirements of the law,
even if the matter comes up in the Diet. Kurusu said
that he did not believe that there was any chance of
changing the law. They had agreed last April to try to
do it but had found it impossible.
Kurusu then turned to the subject of combinations
between the foreign oil companies and Japanese interests,
so that the foreign companies could have security of
trade and the Japanese interests could carry the burden
of the additional investment required to store six months'
stooks of oil. He said that the foreign oil interests
had absolutely refused to consider any such arrangement,
and asked if I knew why. He said that it sounded reason-
able to him. I said that I did not know their speoifio
reasong

=== Page 514 === (confidence 0.99)
- 4 -
reasons but that several reasons occurred to me which
militated against any such plan. The foreign oil com-
panies had spent many years building up their business
and personnel and did not wish to turn them over to
Japanese interests. Moreover, there was hardly any
profit now in the business of importing petroleum pro-
ducts, and there would be still less if the profits had
to be divided between two interests. The discussion then
turned to prices and profits, in the course of which
Kurusu said that Mitsui and Mitsubishi were not any too
happy over the profits available since they were carry-
ing six months' stocks. Kurusu asked me to ascertain,
if I could, the precise reasons for the oil interests'
objections to turning over their local business to Jape-
nese interests. He said that he supposed that they were
the Fords of the oil industry (referring to the fact that
Henry Ford refused to yield control of his local factory
to Japanese interests).
Kurusu then asked what exactly it was that the foreign
oil interests wanted. He said that he recognized that it
would be advantageous to Japan to keep them here, but they
could not "hold up" Japan on that account. He said that
they wanted too much. I explained the present insecurity
of the foreign oil interests and said that naturally they
could not invest in Japan under the circumstances. He
said that the Japanese oil interests were investing. I
pointed out that the two were in very different situations,
both as regards security of trade and as regards profits,
the Japanese refining companies having the advantage of
a tarirf designed to assist them. Kurusu then wanted to
know

=== Page 515 === (confidence 0.99)
-5 -
know why the foreign oil interests did not, in con-
sequence, refine in Japan. I said that I did not know
exactly, but that I supposed that it again was due to
unwillingness to invest further in Japan while the eco-
nomic nationalism here made security of business im-
possible. He said that he thought that some Govern-
mental guarantee of business might be feasible.
Kurusu then said that it appeared that the whole
matter boiled down to governmental guarantess of future
business at a profit. I agreed that some compromise
might be possible if security of business could be ob-
tained. Kurusu promised to see what could be done, and
Industry as soon as possible on the question.
E. R. D.

SUBJECT: AMERICAN OIL INTER STS IN JAPAN.

The Honorable
The Secretary of State,
