Sir:
I have the honor to refer to the mbessy's despatch
246
No. 1629dated January 10, 1936, end to previous correspon-
dence on the subject of the difficulties being experienced
in Japan by the American oil interests under the Petroleum
Industry Law, and to report the following developments in
the situation.
Conversations.
As was stated in the previous conversations between Mr.
Kurusu, members of the staff of this Embassy, and the local
representatives of the foreign oil interests (reported in
despatoh

=== Page 522 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2-
despatch No. 1629), Mr. Kurusu promised to explore the
possibilities of obtaining sufficient guarantees of busi-
ness to warrant the oil companies in complying with the
oil storage provisions of the law end perhaps in establish-
ing refineries in Japan. On January 11, 1936, Ir. Kurusu
called the representatives of the oil interests to his office
and informed them that ir. Yoshino, the Vice Minister for
Commerce and Industry, had agreed in principle with the scheme
of giving adequate guarantees to the foreign oil interests,
in regard to quotas, period of trading and prices. He stated
also that Mr. Yoshino hed agreed that if the foreign oil
interests should desire to refine in Japan, licenses would
be granted to them and they would be given equal treatment
with Japanese refiners. Mr. Kurusu then asked the repre-
Bentatives of the oil interests to obtain from their prin-
cipals concrete proposals embodying the terms upon which
they would continue trading in Japan under the conditions
imposed by the Petroleum Industry Law. lir. Kurusu also
further amplified his previous suggestion that the foreign
oil interests might find it advisable or desirable to work with
Japanese interests and capital, but pointed out tnat he did
not wish to give the impression thet the oil companies were
being coerced into amalgamation with Japanese interests. A
1/
copy of a memorandum of this conversation is enclosed.
The gist of this conversation was telegraphed to their
head offices by the local representatives of the oil interests
and to the Department by this Embassy. From this and previous
conversations with Mir. Kurusu, and from Mr. Kurusu's state-
ments concerning the attitude of the Vice Minister for
Commeroe

=== Page 523 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
Commerce and Industry, it appeared certain that the Japa-
nese officials were endeavoring sincerely to find some
modue operandi which would permit the foreign oil in-
terests to continue to operate in Japan, although the
officials insisted that it was impossible to alter the
six months' stock-holding provision of the law. From
this point of view it appeared desirable that the pro-
posal of the Japanese authorities to give adequate guaran-
tees to the oil interests shoulā receive careful con-
sideretion, and consequently the representatives of the
oil companies reconmended to their principals that trey
eccept the Jepanese proposal and specify definitely the
terms upon w.ich they would continue to operate in Japan.
On January 15, 1936, the local o fice of the Standard-
Vacuum Oil Company received a telegram irom its head orfice
2/
(copy enclosed) stating that concrete proposals would be
forwarded as soon as they could be compiled, expressing
appreciation of the helpful attitude adopted by the Japa-
nese Government, but instructing the local representative
of the Company to point out to Kurusu that the oil interests
are restrained in accepting the six months' stock-holding
requirement not entirely because of the extra expenditure
required but also because of the fear that, should the
storage legislation be successful in Japan, it will be
copied by other nations which possess insufficient oil
supplies, thus placing a burden upon the oil industry which
it might become physically impossible to carry because of
the lack of oil with which to fill the requirements. The
telegram, which was very conciliatory in tone, stated that
the

=== Page 524 === (confidence 0.99)
- 4 -
the Company could probably meet the Japanese Government's
desire for some measure of additional storage if the de-
struotive fecture or the law, referred to above, could be
softened in some way. The local representative of the
Shell interests received an identical telegram from his
head office.
On January 18, 1936, the r presentatives of the oil
interests interviewed Mr. hurusu at the Foreign Uffice and
pointed out to him the dangers existing in the Japanese
six-months'-stock-holding precedent. r. Kurusu promised
to discuss the matter with r. Yoshino, the Vice Minister
for Commerce and Industry. A memorandum of this conversa-
3/
tion is also enclosed.
On January 21, 1936, .r. Kurusu called the local re-
presentatives of the oil interests to the Foreign Office
and informed them thet, although the authorities heve found
it impossible to elter the law, they do not wish to drive
the foreign oil companies from Japan and therefore welcome
the oil companies' willingness to make the concrete proposals
requested by the Japanese. In regard to the cuestion of the
precedent which will be established by Japan in requiring
six months' stocks, r. Kurusu said that Japan had also
established a precedent in that compensation is to be given
for carrying such large stocks, and that if the oil com-
panies should consider the compensation inadequate, the
Japanese Government would be willing to consider any pro-
posals which the oil companies might care to make in this
regard. He also said that the oil companies might protect
themselves in this connection by including a stipulation
that

=== Page 525 === (confidence 0.98)
- 5 -
that they would be relieved of the stock-holding ob-
ligation should it be found that other countries have
copied the Japanese stock-holding law end thus rendered
it physically impossible for the companies to carry the
stocks demended. Mr. Kurusu added that, although recent
developments in Europe indicated the possibility that the
stock-holding legislation might be copied elsewhere, those
developments had also strengthened the conviction of the
military in Japan of the necessity for carrying large
stocks. A copy of a telegram from the local or'fice of the
Staniard-Vacuum Oil Company to the head office, containing
4/
the gist of the conversation, is enclosed herewith.
As can be observed from the above, the conversations
between the Jepanese orficials ari the representatives
of the foreign oil companies are proceeding in a friendly
and conciliatory spirit, and there is now hope that some
working agreement can be reached wnich will be reesonably
satisfactory to both sides. It appears probable that the
Jenenese officials thought that the foreign oil companies
were "bluffing" end that they would eventually yield to
the Japanese Government's demand that they carry six months'
stocks without adequate compensation or guarantees, but
when they discovered that the oil companies were firm in
their decision to withdraw from Japan before complying
with the Japanese demand, the officials changed their
attitude and are now ready to make the concessions
necessary to retain the foreign oil compenies in the
Japanese field. The concessions which will be made, it
appears, will consist of fixing the quantity of stocks
to be carried at one-half the amount imported in a certain
year, which will not increase as the volume of business
increases

=== Page 526 === (confidence 0.96)
- 6 -
increases year by year and thus will represent, in the
course of a few years, not six months' stocks, but three
or four months' stocks. In addition, governmental
guarantees will be given covering the volume of business,
period of trading, prices, etc. It mey be possible, how-
ever, that in return the Japenese Govern..ent will ask
for some guarantee from the companies covering the
certain and steady supplying of oil to Japan.

OTES OF INTERVIEW AT FOREIGN OFFICE ON JANUARY 11. 1936
PRESENT: Mr. S. Kurusu (Chief of Bureau of Commercial Affairs,
Foreign Ofrice)
Messrs. Ely (R.S.P.Co.) and Meyer(S.V.O.Co.)
Mr. Kurusu said that since he had last seen the Companies'
representatives he had been able to discuss with Mr. Yoshino the
question of guarantees. Messrs. Ely and Meyer would remember that he
had expressed to them the opinion that their companies as commercial
concerns were entitled to adequate guarantees in regard to period of
trade, volume of trade and remunerative prices: he had represented
those views to Mr. Yoshino and r. Yoshino had agreed in principle.
Furthermore, in view of the duty advantage which refiners had under
the import tariff, he had also told ..r. Yoshino that the two com-
panies should be assured that, ir they applied, they would be allowed
to refine and that as refiners they would receive equal treatment
with Japanese companies and ir. Yoshino had definitely agreed to this.
What Mr. Kurusu now wished messrs. Ely and meyer to do was to
ask their Prineipals on what terms they would continue trading under
the Petroleum Law. He would like tne companies to make a concrete
proposal, stating exactly whet guarantees they required - which he
thought would be a pretty long period, 10 or 20 years, participation
in the increase in consumption, and something in regard to remune-
rative prices. He was anxious to find a solution of the present
situation which would accomplish the Government's desire that the
Companies should continue trading in Jenan under the Law, and the
Companies' proposals would be considered by the Government in a
conciliatory spirit.
Messrs. Ely and ieyer said that they would be glad to pass on
Mr. Kurusu's request to London and New York, but they pointed out
that there were other unsetisfactory points in the Law which would
not be covered by the guarantees Mr. Kurusu had mentioned. Mr.
Kurusu intimated that the Companies were at liberty to say exactly
what they required: they had stated that for commercial reasons,
and for commercial reasons only, they could not continue trading
under the Law and he would like them to state the terms on which they
could comply. Messrs. Ely and Meyer asked if, in view of the opinion
previously expressed by Mr. Kurusu, it could be taken for granted
that the Government would set a quantitative maximum on stock-holding
obligations. Mr. Kurusu said that he had given what was only his
personal opinion, and intimated that the Companies could propose
a maximum.
Mr. Kurusu went on to suggest that the Companies' proposals
should be made through him and he asked that they might be forth-
coming as soon as possible: there was not much time to spare, as
quotas had been fixed only for the first 3 months of the year and
the Shokosho would want to be in a position some time before the
end of March to make the necessary arrangements for the remainder
of the year.
In concluding the conversation Mr. Kurusu made a brief re-
ference to his previous suggestion that the Companies should work
with Japanese interests and capital - to the effect that at present,
in view of the situation in regard to the Law, a Japanese concern
might be considered to be more advantageously placed than the two
Companies

=== Page 528 === (confidence 0.97)
-2-
Companies and he had realized, therefore, that even if they
wished to make some such arrangement the Companies might not
be in a position satisfactorily to do so unless and until
they were on equal terms with Japanese: he did not wish the
impression to be given that coercion was being brought to bear
on them.

NOTES OF INTERVIEW AT FOREIGN OFFICE ON
Jan. 10. 1936
ENT: Mr. S. Kurusu (Chief of Bureau of Commercial Affairs,
Foreign Office)
Messrs Ely (R.S.P.Co.) and Meyer (S.V.O.Co.)
Messrs. Ely and Meyer said that they wished to let Mr. Kurusu
know that since their last interview with him they hed had cables
from London and New York. Their Directors wished them to thank Mr.
Kurusu for his message and to express their appreciaticn of the
spirit in which it had been sent: they were going to respond to it
as soon as possible in the same spirit and were studying the whole
question with a view to drawing up concrete proposals as suggested
by Mr. Kurusu - that is to say, on the basis of the guarantees men-
tioned by him plus, Messrs. Ely end Meyer anticipated, certain
other points including a quantitative maximum for the stock-holding
obligation.
There was however one important point which the Directors
wished Messrs. Ely and Meyer to put quite frankly before Lr. Kurusu.
Even if an arrangement on the lines he had suggested were to
materialize, they still feared that the Jenan Law, especially
the stock-holding stipulations, would almost certainly be copied
by other countries: r. Kurusu would be able to eppreciate what
this would involve from the standpoint of production alone and
that if it happened it would be physically impossible for the
Petroleum Industry to comply and eventually the object of the
Japanese Government would be defeated. This was therefore an
exceedingly important question, which in LMessrs. Ely end Meyer's
opinion must have an important bearing on the proposals which their
Directors were considering putting forvard. Could not something be
done to obviate this risk which was so clearly involved in the Law?
Mr. Kurusu said he "got the point". He could understand that
Italy, for instance, might want to bring in similar legislation and
that other countries also might follow. What the Companies evi-
dently wanted him to do was to find a formula which would avoid
the creation of a precedent, although he felt that the Japan Law
herdly constituted a precedent; France for instance had a stock-
holding obligation - but he realized that France might increase it
from three months to six if other countries did so.
Messrs. Ely and ieyer said they were glad that Mr. Kurusu
appreciated the force of the point the Directors had made and they
hoped that, notwithstanding the difficulties involved, he would be
able to do something which would remove the Companies' fear that the
stock-holding legislation would spread. The Directors had intimated
in their cables that if this could be done the Companies on their side
might be able to carry some stocks over and above normal commercial
requirements - in return of course for suitable guarantees, etc.
Mr. Kurusu remarked that Messrs. Ely and Meyer had said only
"some" extra stocks. He would see what could be done but felt it
was up to Mr. Yoshino. He therefore telephoned to the latter, advising
him of the point that had been raised, and arranged to discuss it
with him on the morning of 20th January.
